On sampling Diluted Spin-Glasses with unbounded interactions.
Charilaos Efthymiou and Kostas Zampetakis

Abstract
. Spin-glasses are natural Gibbs distributions that have been studied in theoretical computer science for many decades. Recently, they have been gaining renewed attention from the community as they emerge naturally in neural computation and learning, network inference, optimisation, and many other areas. We study the problem of efficiently sampling from spin-glass distributions when the underlying graph is a typical instance of 𝑮 ⁡ ( 𝒏 , 𝒅 / 𝒏 ) \mathbold{G}(n,d/n) , i.e., the random graph on n n vertices such that each edge appears independently with probability d / n d/n , where the expected degree d = Θ ⁡ ( 1 ) d=\Theta(1) . Our main focus is on the 2-spin model at inverse temperature β \beta , which serves as a prototypical example of a disordered system. We consider this distribution to be one of the most interesting case of spin-glasses, and one of the most challenging to analyse, since its Gaussian couplings give rise to unbounded interaction between the sites of the system. We employ the well-known Glauber dynamics to sample from the aforementioned distribution. We show that for the typical instances of the 2-spin model on 𝑮 ⁡ ( 𝒏 , 𝒅 / 𝒏 ) \mathbold{G}(n,d/n) , the mixing time of Glauber dynamics is O ⁡ ( n ( 1 + 25 log ⁡ d ) ) O\left(n^{\left(1+\frac{25}{\sqrt{\log d}}\right)}\right) , for any β < 1 4 ​ d \beta<\frac{1}{4\sqrt{d}} . Our results can also be adapted for the case of spin-glass distributions where the interactions are bounded. In that respect, we obtain rapid mixing of Glauber dynamics for the Viana-Bray model on 𝑮 ⁡ ( 𝒏 , 𝒅 / 𝒏 ) \mathbold{G}(n,d/n) when β < 1 4 ​ d \beta<\frac{1}{4\sqrt{d}} . This improves on the current best bound which is β < 0.18 d \beta<\frac{0.18}{\sqrt{d}} . We utilise stochastic localisation in our analysis, and in particular, we build and improve on the scheme introduced in [Liu, Mohanty, Rajaraman and Wu: FOCS 2024]. This is the first time that stochastic localisation is used for diluted spin-glasses, where both degrees and interactions can be unbounded. Our results rely on introducing an elaborate block partition for the analysis with the stochastic localisation. We deal with the unbounded interactions by introducing appropriate matrix norms to control the entropy decay along the localisation path.



1.
Introduction

Spin glasses
are natural, high-dimensional Gibbs distributions that have been studied in theoretical computer science for many decades.
Recently, they have been gaining renewed attention from the community as they emerge naturally in
neural computation
and
learning
,
e.g., in the Hopfield model, as models of
network inference
,
e.g., in the stochastic block model, in
optimisation
,
counting-sampling
and many other areas, e.g., see

[
47
,
33
,
17
,
3
,
37
,
29
,
23
]
.

Spin-glasses are widely considered to be canonical models of
extremely
disordered
systems
[
40
,
47
]
and, as such, have been
studied extensively in mathematics and mathematical physics, e.g., see

[
49
,
43
,
31
,
36
]
. They have also
been studied in statistical physics, e.g., see
[
40
,
47
,
44
]
,
since the early 1980s. The seminal, groundbreaking work of Giorgio Parisi on spin-glasses
earned him the Nobel Prize in Physics in 2021.

We focus on a natural, well-known case of spin glasses, the
2-spin model
.
For fixed graph
G=(V,E)
and a set of independent identically distributed (i.i.d)
standard Gaussians
\{\mathbold{\gamma}_{e}\}_{e\in E(G)}
, we let the
interaction matrix

\UpJ=\UpJ(G,\{\mathbold{\gamma}_{e}\})\in\mathbb{R}^{V\times V}
be defined by

(1.1)

\displaystyle\UpJ(u,w)

\displaystyle={\mathds{1}}\{u\sim w\}\times\mathbold{\gamma}_{\{u,w\}}

\displaystyle\forall\;u,w\in V\kern 5.0pt.

The 2-spin model with
inverse temperature

\beta\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and
external field

\uph\in\mathbb{R}^{V}
, corresponds to the Gibbs distribution
\mu
such that

(1.2)

\displaystyle\mu_{\UpJ,\beta,\uph}(\sigma)\propto\exp\left(\frac{\beta}{2}\cdot\langle\sigma,\UpJ\cdot\sigma\rangle+\langle\sigma,\uph\rangle\right),

\displaystyle\quad\forall\;\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{V}\kern 5.0pt,

where
\langle{\bf x},{\bf y}\rangle
stands for the standard inner product of vectors
{\bf x}
and
{\bf y}
,
while the symbol
\propto
stands for “proportional to”.

Due to the Gaussian couplings
\{\mathbold{\gamma}_{e}\}
, note that
\mu_{\UpJ,\beta,\uph}
is a
random

Gibbs distribution on
\{\pm 1\}^{V}
.

The 2-spin model on the complete graph corresponds to the well-known
Sherrington-Kirkpatrick
model
(SK model)
[
45
]
. A more tame version of the above distribution is when, instead of the Gaussian
variables, we use random
\{\pm 1\}
. This corresponds to the
Viana-Bray
model.

In this work, we focus on the distribution in (
1.2
) for the case where the underlying graph is an
instance of the sparse random graph
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
. Recall that
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
is the random graph on the vertex set

V_{n}=\{1,\ldots,n\}
where each edge appears independently with probability
d/n
. Here, we assume that the
expected degree
d\in\Theta(1)
, i.e., it is a fixed number. We obtain an instance of the 2-spin model by first drawing
the underlying graph from the distribution
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
and then, given the graph, we generate the random Gibbs
distribution as in (
1.1
) and (
1.2
).

On a first account, the 2-spin model on
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
may appear innocent, i.e., it resembles the standard Ising model with just
the addition of Gaussian couplings. It turns out, though, that it is a
fascinating
distribution with a lot of intricacies,
while the configuration space has an extremely rich structure; e.g., in various settings, it is conjectured to exhibit “
infinite

Replica Symmetry Breaking”
[
40
]
.

In this work, we consider the natural problem of efficiently sampling from the 2-spin model on
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
.
We employ the powerful Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) method, and in particular, we use the

Glauber dynamics
. This is a popular Markov chain utilised for sampling from high-dimensional
Gibbs distributions such as the Hard-core model, the colouring model, spin-glasses, etc. In this setting,
the measure of efficiency is the
mixing time
of the Markov chain.

Sampling from Gibbs distributions induced by instances of
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
, or, more generally, instances of the
so-called
random Constraint Satisfaction Problems
, is at the heart of the endeavours to investigate
connections between
phase transitions
and the efficiency of algorithms, e.g.

[
1
,
2
,
16
,
32
,
34
,
46
,
42
]
.
The MCMC sampling problem on the sparse random graph has garnered considerable attention, e.g., see

[
20
,
6
,
24
,
41
,
42
,
25
,
21
,
15
]
,
and is considered an intriguing case to study. Until recently, the focus has been on sampling
standard Gibbs distributions, which already is a very challenging problem. The study of spin-glasses takes
us a step further. Working with the 2-spin model, we introduce an
extra level
of disorder due to the
Gaussian couplings at the edges of the graph. Hence, in our analysis, we need to deal with the disorder of
both
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
and the random Gibbs distribution on this graph.

The objective of this work is to investigate the range of the inverse temperature
\beta
such that the corresponding
Glauber dynamics exhibits
fast mixing
. It is a folklore conjecture that we have fast mixing time for any

\beta<\beta_{\rm rec}
, where
\beta_{\rm rec}=\beta_{\rm rec}(d)
specifies the onset of the so-called

reconstruction region
of the 2-spin model on
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
. In particular,
\beta_{\rm rec}
satisfies

(1.3)

\displaystyle d\cdot\textstyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\tanh(\mathbold{\gamma}\cdot\beta_{\rm rec})\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=1\kern 5.0pt,

where the expectation is with respect to the standard normal random variable
\mathbold{\gamma}
.
An upper bound for
\beta_{\rm rec}
was found in
[
36
]
, while in

[
26
]
it was shown that this bound is tight, yielding the above relation.
It is elementary to verify that
\lim_{d\to\infty}\beta_{\rm rec}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}
.

For our results, we also define
\beta_{c}
such that,

(1.4)

\displaystyle d\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\tanh(\mathbold{\gamma}\cdot\beta_{c})\right)^{2}\right]=\upkappa=1/4\kern 5.0pt,

where again the expectation is with respect to the standard Gaussian random variable
\mathbold{\gamma}
.

The following is the main result of our paper.

Theorem 1.1
.

There exists a sufficiently large constant
d_{0}>0
such that for any
d\geq d_{0}
and any

0<\beta\leq\beta_{c}(d)
the following is true:

Let
\mu
be the 2-spin model on
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
, with inverse temperature
\beta
.
Then, with probability
1-o(1)
over the instances of
\mu
,
Glauber dynamics exhibits mixing time
{\rm T}_{\rm mix}\leq n^{\left(1+\frac{25}{\sqrt{\log d}}\right)}
.

Theorem

1.1
implies that we are a factor 4 away from the conjectured bound, i.e. we require

\beta\leq\frac{1}{4\sqrt{d}}
. The factor 4 seems to be a natural bound with the approach we adopt to
analyse the mixing time of Glauber dynamics. Improving this factor seems to require a
significantly different approach.

We did not try to optimise the bound on the mixing time in
Theorem

1.1
. It seems plausible
that our analysis gives mixing time
O\left(n^{1+\frac{c}{\log d}}\right)
for a constant
c>0
.
It has been conjectured, e.g., see
[
27
]
, that the mixing time of Glauber dynamics
for the typical instances of the 2-spin model on
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
is
n\exp(\Omega(\sqrt{\log n}))
.
This is because we expect to have isolated stars with rather large couplings at the edges.

Theorem

1.1
still holds if we replace the Gaussians with couplings drawn from an
arbitrary
sub-Gaussian
distribution. Our results can be easily adapted to apply to the
Viana-Bray model, i.e., the couplings are random
\{\pm 1\}
. In this case, one obtains rapid
mixing for Glauber dynamics for any
\beta\leq 0.25/\sqrt{d}
. This improves on the bound obtained
in
[
38
]
for the Viana-Bray model on
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
. Specifically, they obtain
rapid mixing for any
\beta<0.18/\sqrt{d}
. Let us remark, though, that in
[
38
]

the mixing time bound is
n\exp(o(\log n))
, which is slightly better than the one we obtain here.

Related Work:

In this part, we give a high-level overview of the related work on the problem. For the economy
of space, we focus on the results for “diluted systems”, i.e., with bounded (expected) degree.

Related to our paper is the recent work of Anari, Koehler and Vuong in
[
4
]
, which studies
the Viana-Bray spin-glass model on the random
d
-regular graph. They establish rapid mixing of Glauber
dynamics for
0<\beta<\frac{0.295}{\sqrt{d-1}}
. This model differs from what we consider here because
both the degrees of the vertices and the couplings at the edges are
bounded
. Note that in
the Gaussian case, we typically have many edges whose couplings are unbounded.

The subsequent work of Liu, Mohanty, Rajaraman and Wu in
[
38
]
considers the
Viana-Bray model on
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
. That is, random
\{\pm 1\}
couplings, but the degrees of the graph can
grow
unbounded
. Note that for typical instances of
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
, the vast majority of the vertices
are of degree
\approx d
, while there are vertices of degree as huge as
\Theta(\frac{\log n}{\log\log n})
.
In
[
38
]
, they establish rapid mixing for Glauber dynamics for any
0<\beta<\frac{0.18}{\sqrt{d}}
.
The bound they obtain for
\beta
is smaller than that in
[
4
]
, which is for the random

d
-regular graph. On the positive side, the approach in
[
38
]
allows for unbounded degrees.

Interestingly, both of the aforementioned works rely on the
stochastic localisation
technique to
establish their mixing bounds. This technique was introduced by Eldan in
[
28
]
and
has been pivotal in proving functional inequalities in various settings, e.g., it gives the state-of-the-art
bounds for the Kannan-Lovász-Simonovich (KLS) conjecture in
[
11
]
. Recently,
it has been used to analyse the mixing time of Glauber dynamics, e.g., see the influential works of
Eldan, Koehler and Zeitouni in
[
29
]
and Chen and Eldan in
[
12
]
.
Stochastic localisation, naturally, imposes spectral conditions for the rapid mixing of Glauber dynamics
for distributions such as the Ising model and spin-glasses. This turns out to be quite useful in our case with
the spin-glasses, because it yields bounds for
\beta
which are of the form
c/\sqrt{d}
, for a constant

c>0
independent of
d
.

The work of Efthymiou and Zampetakis in
[
27
]
considers the same distribution as
the one we consider here. They obtain a
\beta<\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cdot\frac{1}{d}
bound for the rapid
mixing of Glauber dynamics on the 2-spin model on
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
. The bound on
\beta
they obtain is
even worse than that in
[
38
]
, but their analysis allows for unbounded couplings
and degrees. The work in
[
27
]
does not use stochastic localisation, it rather uses the

path coupling
technique introduced by Bubley and Dyer in
[
9
]
.

Considering the previous works, we conclude that the approaches utilising stochastic localisation
seem to give better bounds for
\beta
, but, on the other hand, they do not seem to handle well
the unbounded couplings. Indeed, incorporating such couplings into the analysis turns out to be a
challenging task. Already getting from the bounded-degree case in
[
4
]
to the
unbounded one in
[
38
]
is a non-trivial technical achievement.

Our objective here is to utilise the stochastic localisation technique to obtain rapid mixing bounds for the 2-spin model
on
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
, i.e., establish rapid mixing for diluted spin-glasses which allow unbounded couplings and degrees.

Block Partition for the analysis:

To establish rapid mixing for Glauber dynamics on
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
, not necessarily for spin-glasses
but also for general Gibbs distributions, the main challenge is to deal with the “effect of high-degree
vertices”. That is, to show that the relatively rare, high-degree vertices we encounter in the
typical instances of
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
have a negligible effect. Hence, it is only the vertices of
degree
\approx d
that are important in the analysis. In turn, this allows us to obtain rapid
mixing bounds in terms of
d
.

The standard strategy to circumvent the effect of high-degree vertices in most approaches so far
is to introduce a partition on the set of vertices of the underlying graph into two parts: the “good”
and the “bad” part. The “bad” part contains the high-degree vertices
and their nearby small-degree neighbours. The components induced by the vertices in the bad part are typically small and simply structured,
i.e., each component is a tree or a unicyclic graph. In each such component, the high-degree vertices
are hidden deep inside, such that there is a large “buffer” of low-degree vertices that separates them
from the good part of the graph. Usually, this buffer of low-degree vertices diminishes the effect of the
high-degree ones.

The above approach was introduced by Dyer, Flaxman, Frieze and Vigoda in
[
20
]

to establish rapid mixing of Glauber dynamics on the colourings of
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
, using path coupling.
Further improvements on
[
20
]
that utilise path coupling build on this approach,
including the work in
[
27
]
for the 2-spin model. The improvements obtained in this setting
typically amount to introducing a better partition of the set of vertices, i.e., one that allows for tighter analysis.

The authors in
[
38
]
follow a similar strategy, partitioning the vertex set
to establish their mixing bound for the Viana-Bray model on
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
. However, rather than using
path coupling, they utilise the stochastic localisation technique. Somehow, they combine the two different
approaches to obtain their results.

Here, we also follow a strategy that combines the partitioning of the set of vertices into good and bad parts
with stochastic localisation. However, we obtain our results by introducing an entirely

different
block partition from that considered in
[
38
]
.
The reason is not only because we use Gaussian couplings instead of
\{\pm 1\}
. We introduce a more
elaborate partition that gives better rapid mixing bounds with respect to the inverse temperature
\beta
.

Notation

Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph and
B\subseteq V
. We write
\partial_{\rm in}B
for inner boundary of the set
B
, i.e., the set of vertices in
B
that have a neighbor outside
B
. We also denote with
\partial_{\rm out}B
, the outer boundary of
B
, i.e., the set of vertices that do not belong to
B
and have a neighbor inside
B
.

2.
Approach

In this section, we provide a high-level description of our approach, assuming minimal prior knowledge on the reader’s part.
The presentation of the material is intended to be informal.

The modified log-Sobolev constant:

We obtain our rapid mixing result by utilising functional inequalities, and in
particular, by establishing a bound on the so-called
modified
log-Sobolev constant.

For concreteness, let
G=(V_{n},E)
be a fixed graph. Consider the Glauber dynamics on a Gibbs distribution

\mu:\{\pm 1\}^{V_{n}}\to[0,1]
.

For a function
f\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, we let
\mu(f)
be the
expected
value of
f
with respect to
\mu
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mu(f)

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{V_{n}}}\mu(\sigma)\cdot f(\sigma)\kern 5.0pt.

Similarly, we define the
entropy
of
f
with respect to
\mu
by

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ent}_{\mu}(f)

\displaystyle=\textstyle\mu\left(f\log f\right)-\mu\left(f\right)\log\mu(f)\kern 5.0pt.

The associated
Dirichlet form
of Glauber dynamics for function
f\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
is given by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{\mu}(f,\log f)

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{\sigma,\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{V_{n}}}(f(\sigma)-f(\tau))\cdot\log\frac{f(\sigma)}{f(\tau)}\cdot\mu(\sigma)\cdot{P}(\sigma,\tau)\kern 5.0pt,

where
{P}(\sigma,\tau)
is the transition probability from state
\sigma
to state
\tau
for
Glauber dynamics.

We say that Glauber satisfies the modified log-Sobolev inequality with constant
{C}_{\rm mLSI}>0
,
if for any
f\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}(f,\log f)\geq{C}_{\rm mLSI}\cdot\mathrm{Ent}_{\mu}(f)\kern 5.0pt.

We can use
{C}_{\rm mLSI}
to bound the mixing time of Glauber dynamics. Results from

[
7
]
imply

{\rm T}_{\rm mix}\leq({C}_{\rm mLSI})^{-1}\cdot\left(\log(\log(\mu^{-1}_{\min}))+\log 2+1\right)\kern 5.0pt,

where
\mu_{\min}=\min\{\mu(\sigma)\ |\ \sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{V},\ \mu(\sigma)>0\}
.

Note that we aim to obtain an appropriate lower bound on
{C}_{\rm mLSI}
, i.e.,
show that
{C}_{\rm mLSI}\geq\frac{1}{n^{c}}
, for a small constant
c>0
.

General Stochastic Localisation:

We obtain bounds on
{C}_{\rm mLSI}
by employing the
stochastic localisation
technique
introduced by Eldan in
[
28
]
. Specifically, we use results of Chen & Eldan from

[
12
]
as well as results of Liu, Mohanty, Rajaraman and Wu from

[
38
]
for the Viana-Bray model on
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
.

With stochastic localisation, we introduce a process
\{\mu_{t}\}_{0\leq t\leq 1}
over probability
measures on
\mathbb{R}^{V_{n}}
such that
\mu_{0}=\mu
. This process specifies a

localisation path
from
\mu_{0}
to
\mu_{1}
. The idea is to restrict the distributions along
this path so that
\mu_{1}
is a “simple” distribution that is easy to analyse, e.g.,
\mu_{1}

can be a product measure. Typically,
\mu_{1}
is a random variable such that
\mathbb{E}[\mu_{1}]=\mu_{0}
,
where the expectation is over the random choices of the localisation process.

The method allows us to obtain a lower bound on the modified log-Sobolev constant of
\mu_{0}

(and hence
\mu
) as a function of the modified log-Sobolev of the “simple” distribution
\mu_{1}
and the decay of entropy
along the localisation path. Let us be a bit more concrete.

The process
\{\mu_{t}\}_{0\leq t\leq 1}
is parametrised with respect to the positive-definite

V_{n}\times V_{n}
matrices
\{\UpC_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
. We set

\mu_{0}=\mu
, while
\mu_{t}
satisfies the following system of stochastic differential equations: for
every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{V_{n}}
, we have

(2.1)

\displaystyle d\left(\frac{\mu_{t}}{\mu_{0}}\right)(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mu_{t}}{\mu_{0}}(x)\cdot\langle x-\Upb_{t},\UpC_{t}\cdot d\UpB_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle 0\leq t\leq 1\kern 5.0pt,

where
\Upb_{t}=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbold{\sigma}}\sim\mu_{t}}[{\mathbold{\sigma}}]
, while
\{\UpB_{t}\}_{0\leq t\leq 1}
is
the standard Brownian motion in
\mathbb{R}^{V_{n}}
.

One can show, e.g., see
[
12
]
, that almost surely we have

(2.2)

\displaystyle\mu_{t}(\sigma)

\displaystyle\propto\exp\left(\frac{\beta}{2}\cdot\langle\sigma,\UpJ_{t}\cdot\sigma\rangle+\langle\sigma,\uph_{t}\rangle\right),

\displaystyle\quad\forall\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{V_{n}}\kern 5.0pt,

where
\UpJ_{t}=\UpJ-\int^{t}_{0}\UpC^{2}_{s}ds
and
\uph_{t}=\int^{t}_{0}[\UpC_{s}\cdot d\UpB_{s}+\UpC^{2}_{s}\cdot\Upb(\mu_{s})]ds
.

Intuitively, the above implies that at time
t
, we can have a “simpler” interaction matrix
\UpJ_{t}
obtained
by removing matrix
\int^{t}_{0}\UpC^{2}_{s}ds
from the initial interaction matrix
\UpJ
. The process also
changes the external field with
t
, but for our purposes here, this is an unimportant phenomenon.

Suppose that the choice of
\{\UpC_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
is such that for each function

f\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, we have

(2.3)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{Ent}_{\mu_{1}}(f)]}{\mathrm{Ent}_{\mu_{0}}(f)}\geq\upxi\kern 5.0pt,

where the expectation is for the random measure
\mu_{1}
.

Furthermore, suppose that almost surely
\mu_{1}
is such that

(2.4)

\displaystyle{C}_{\rm mLSI}(\mu_{1})>\uppsi\kern 5.0pt.

Then, from
[
12
]
, we have

(2.5)

\displaystyle{C}_{\rm mLSI}(\mu_{0})>\uppsi\cdot\upxi\kern 5.0pt.

Usually (
2.3
) is called the
conservation of entropy inequality

and expresses the decay-conservation of entropy along the localisation path.

All the above make it clear that we would like the entropy along the localisation path to be conserved, i.e.,

\upxi
to be large. Furthermore, we would like
\mu_{1}
to be simple enough so that we can easily estimate

{C}_{\rm mLSI}(\mu_{1})
while
\uppsi
is large.

Control Matrices Vs Entropy Conservation:

A natural question at this point is how to choose the control matrices
\{\UpC_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}

for the distribution
\mu_{\UpJ,\beta,\uph}
on
\{\pm 1\}^{V_{n}}
defined in (
1.2
).
Our focus is on the effect of the choice of the control matrices
\{\UpC_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
on (
2.3
) and (
2.4
).

The covariance matrix of
\mu_{\UpJ,\beta,\uph}
, denoted as
\mathrm{Cov}(\UpJ,\beta,\uph)
, is defined by

(2.6)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cov}(\UpJ,\beta,\uph)=\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbold{\sigma}}\cdot{\mathbold{\sigma}}^{T}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbold{\sigma}}\right]\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbold{\sigma}}^{T}\right]\kern 5.0pt,

where
\mathbold{\sigma}\in\{\pm 1\}^{V_{n}}
is distributed as in
\mu_{\UpJ,\beta,\uph}
.

We study the
entropy conservation by means of the covariance matrix. Suppose that
\{\UpC_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
is such
that almost surely, for all
t\in[0,1]
and any external field
\uph^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{V_{n}}
, we have

(2.7)

\displaystyle\beta\cdot\|\UpC_{t}\cdot\mathrm{Cov}(\UpJ_{t},\beta,\uph^{\prime})\cdot\UpC_{t}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\upalpha_{t}\kern 5.0pt.

for
\upalpha_{t}>0
. Then, as shown in
[
12
]
, for any
f\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, we have

(2.8)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{Ent}_{\mu_{1}}(f)]\geq\exp\left(-\int^{1}_{0}\upalpha_{t}\cdot dt\right)\cdot\mathrm{Ent}_{\mu_{0}}(f)\kern 5.0pt.

The above provides the connection between
\{\UpC_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
and the conservation of entropy we are using.

A Toy Example

To cast more light on the aforementioned connection, consider an example in which
\mu
is the standard
Ising model with inverse temperature
\beta
and external field
\uph
on a fixed graph
G=(V,E)
.
The use of the Ising model in this example is to keep the discussion simple.

Note that with the Ising model, the interaction matrix
\UpJ
can be equal to the
adjacency
matrix

\UpA_{G}
of graph
G
. Recall that
\UpA_{G}
is such that for any two vertices

u,w
, we have

\UpA_{G}(u,w)={\mathds{1}}\{\textrm{$u,w$ are adjacent in $G$}\}\kern 5.0pt.

In this case, stochastic localisation provides naturally rapid mixing bounds for inverse temperature
\beta

expressed in terms of the
spectral radius

\varrho
of the adjacency matrix
\UpA_{G}
, i.e.,

\varrho=\|\UpA_{G}\|_{2}
. More specifically, we set

(1)

\UpJ=\UpA_{G}+(1+\epsilon)\cdot\varrho\cdot\UpI
, for small
\epsilon>0
,

(2)

for each
t\in[0,1]
, set
\UpC_{t}=\UpJ^{\frac{1}{2}}
.

Item (i) implies that the interaction matrix
\UpJ
we use is not exactly the standard
\UpA_{G}
,
but a “shifted version” of this matrix. This shifting guarantees that
\UpJ
is positive definite, i.e.

\UpJ\succ 0
. Note that adding a diagonal matrix to
\UpA_{G}
does not alter the Gibbs distribution.
Furthermore, due to the positive definiteness of
\UpJ
, the matrix
\UpC_{t}
is well-defined.

The above choice of
\UpJ
and
\UpC_{t}
implies that almost surely
\UpJ_{1}
is the
empty
graph.
In this case,
\mu_{1}
is a product measure on
\mathbb{R}^{V_{n}}
, while computing the modified log-Sobolev
for
\mu_{1}
is trivial. The mixing bounds in terms of
\beta
arise from the entropy conservation and,
in particular, our desire to control the quantity

\displaystyle\|\UpC_{t}\cdot\mathrm{Cov}(\UpJ_{t},\beta,\uph^{\prime})\cdot\UpC_{t}\|_{2}\kern 5.0pt.

As mentioned above, the natural bound we obtain for
\beta
is in terms of the spectral radius
\varrho
of

\UpA_{G}
.

A vanilla application of the above to the Ising model on
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
makes it challenging to control

\|\UpC_{t}\cdot\mathrm{Cov}(\UpJ_{t},\beta,\uph^{\prime})\cdot\UpC_{t}\|_{2}
for
\beta
expressed in terms of the expected degree
d
.
The natural bound one gets from the above approach for
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
require
\beta\in o(1)
as
\|\UpA_{\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)}\|_{2}\approx\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{\log\log n}}
.

Liu, Mohanty, Rajaraman and Wu in
[
38
]
propose a natural scheme that allows us
to control the above norm. Even though they consider the Viana-Bray model on
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
, i.e., spin-glass
with random couplings in
\{\pm 1\}
, the scheme also applies to the standard Ising model. To make the
contributions in
[
38
]
clearer, we stick to the Ising model on
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
.

They propose a partition of the set of vertices into two parts, the “good” and the “bad” part. The “bad” part contains
the high-degree vertices along with the nearby low-degree vertices. By low-degree here, we mean vertices of degree

\leq(1+\epsilon)d
for a small
\epsilon>0
. The vertices which are not in the bad part belong to the good one.

Let
\UpA_{\mathbold{G}}
be the adjacency matrix of
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
. Also, let
\UpA_{\rm good}
be the adjacency
matrix supported only at vertices in the good part of
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
, while let
\varrho_{\rm good}
be the spectral
radius of
\UpA_{\rm good}
. Then, the set-up is (roughly) as follows:

(1)

\UpJ=\UpA_{\mathbold{G}}+(1+\epsilon)\cdot\varrho_{\rm good}\cdot\UpI
,

(2)

for each
t\in[0,1]
set
\UpC_{t}=(\UpA_{\rm good}+(1+\epsilon)\cdot\varrho_{\rm good}\cdot\UpI)^{\frac{1}{2}}
.

Note that the control matrices only reduce the good part of
\UpA_{\mathbold{G}}
. Hence,
at time
t=1
, the off-diagonal entries of the interaction matrix
\UpJ_{1}
are only supported by
vertices at the bad part of
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
.

Furthermore, they show that one can control the quantity
\|\UpC_{t}\cdot\mathrm{Cov}(\UpJ_{t},\beta,\uph^{\prime})\cdot\UpC_{t}\|_{2}

and subsequently the entropy decay by taking
\beta\in O(1/\varrho_{\rm good})
.
This is a significant improvement as
\varrho_{\rm good}\approx d
.

For the typical instances of
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
, the vertices of the bad part induce simple subgraphs of
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
. This makes it relatively easy to obtain a lower bound on the modified log-Sobolev
inequality of
\mu_{1}
.

From Ising to Viana-Bray and Beyond

Given the above setup of Liu et al.,
[
38
]
one obtains the bounds for the Viana-Bray spin-glass by essentially estimating
the spectral radius
\varrho_{\rm good}
when the adjacency matrix
\UpA_{\mathbold{G}}
of
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
is randomly
signed, i.e., each non-zero entry of the matrix is randomly set to
\{\pm 1\}
.

Unfortunately, the analysis in

[
38
]
relies heavily on the fact that the entries of
\UpJ
are bounded, i.e., they are random
signs of the entries of
\UpA_{\mathbold{G}}
. This limits the applicability of the method and excludes some natural
spin-glass Gibbs distributions. Furthermore, as pointed out in
[
38
]
, the block decomposition
they propose for the “good” and “bad” parts of the graph is
not
optimal, i.e., it does not give the natural bound
on
\beta
one expects to get from stochastic localisation.

In light of the above observations, we revisit the stochastic localisation framework proposed in

[
38
]
. We propose a
new
approach for the analysis that allows us to obtain improved
bounds on
\beta
, while at the same time, we are able to handle unbounded interactions, yielding results
for some important spin-glass distributions such as the 2-spin model.

Recall that the 2-spin model has
Gaussian couplings
on the edges of the graph. For typical realisations of the
distribution, we expect to have many edges whose coupling is as huge as
\Theta(\sqrt{\log n})
. This
is one key difference with the distribution considered in
[
38
]
, where
all

couplings are in
\{\pm 1\}
.

Contribution 1:
Introduce an appropriate partition of the set of vertices of
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
.

In our setting with the Gaussians, we still have a partition of the set of vertices into a “good” and a “bad”
part. Rather than focusing only on the degree of the vertices, we consider the
combined
effect of
the degree and the magnitude of the couplings at the edges. This gives rise to a quite different partition
from what is proposed in
[
38
]
.

The block construction we introduce allows us to control the conservation-decay of entropy along
the localisation path, i.e., get a handle on
\|\UpC_{t}\cdot\mathrm{Cov}(\UpJ_{t},\beta,\uph^{\prime})\cdot\UpC_{t}\|_{2}
.

Let
H\subset V_{n}
be the set of vertices in the bad part in the partition of graph
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
, while
let
S\subset V_{n}
be the set of vertices in the good part. Also, let
\partial H\subset H
be the set of bad vertices which are at the boundary between the
good and the bad part. It turns out that we can control the entropy conservation along the localisation
path by bounding appropriately the quantities

(2.9)

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{Cov}_{H}(\mu_{1})\|_{2}

and

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{Cov}_{\partial H}(\mu_{1})\|_{2}\kern 5.0pt,

where
\mathrm{Cov}_{H}(\mu_{1})
and
\mathrm{Cov}_{\partial H}(\mu_{1})
denote the covariance matrices of distribution

\mu_{1}
in the localisation process, restricted to the set of vertices
H
and
\partial H
, respectively.

The connected components induced by
H
contain vertices with very large degrees and very
strong couplings, i.e., both can get unbounded values. This makes it very challenging to study the
above quantities, and it is not possible to use techniques from
[
38
]
. An
important contribution of this work comes from introducing an approach that allows us to control
the above quantities under unbounded couplings. To this end, we have our second contribution.

Contribution 2:
Introduce a bespoke matrix norm to bound the quantities in (
2.9
).

The use of the matrix norm here comes in contrast to
[
38
]
, where a simple counting argument is
sufficient to bound the norms in (
2.9
).

We would like to note that the matrix norm and the block partition are interrelated constructions in our analysis. For further discussions, see the paragraphs below.

The new Block Partition.

For our approach, we build on a weighting scheme for the set of vertices and paths of
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
introduced
and developed in
[
22
,
25
,
27
]
. Consider the graph
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
,

\beta>0
,
\uph\in\mathbb{R}^{V_{n}}
and the interaction matrix
\UpJ
as specified in (
1.1
).
Also, let
\mu=\mu_{\UpJ,\beta,\uph}
.

For each edge
e
of
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
consider the
influence

{\rm\Upgamma}_{e}
, defined by

\displaystyle{\rm\Upgamma}_{e}

\displaystyle=|\tanh(\beta\cdot\UpJ_{e})|\kern 5.0pt.

The influence
{\rm\Upgamma}_{e}
is a natural quantity to consider in our setting.

For every vertex
w\in V_{n}
, we let the
aggregate squared influence
be defined by

\displaystyle{\Uptheta}(w)

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{{z\sim w}}({\rm\Upgamma}_{\{w,z\}})^{2}\kern 5.0pt.

The choice of the parameters in Theorem
1.1
implies that for vertex
w
,
we have
\mathbb{E}[{\Uptheta}(w)]<1
, while the expectation is with respect to both the degree of
w

and the couplings at its incident edges.

A useful observation is that
{\Uptheta}(w)
is well-concentrated, i.e., for fixed
\delta>0
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr[{\Uptheta}(w)>\mathbb{E}[{\Uptheta}(w)]+\delta]\leq\exp(-\Omega(d))\kern 5.0pt.

The above implies that the “heavy vertices” , i.e., the vertices
w
such that
{\Uptheta}(w)>1
, are
relatively rare. But still, we typically have
\Theta(n)
of those.

We introduce a weighting scheme for the vertices of the graph
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
that uses
{\Uptheta}(w)
’s.
Given a parameter
\varepsilon>0
, for any
u\in V_{n}
, define the
\varepsilon
-
weight
by

\displaystyle\UpM(u)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}{1-\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}&\text{ if }{\Uptheta}(u)\leq{1-\frac{\varepsilon}{2},}\\
d\cdot{\Uptheta}(u)&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

Furthermore, each path
P=(v_{0},\ldots,v_{\ell})
in
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
is assigned weight
\UpM(P)
such that

\UpM(P)=\prod\nolimits_{0\leq i\leq\ell}\UpM(v_{i})\kern 5.0pt.

We also introduce the notion of the
\varepsilon
-
block
vertex in
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
. We say
w
is an

\varepsilon
-block vertex if every path
P
that emanates from
w
is “light”, i.e., it has weight

\UpM(P)<1
. Intuitively, the heavier a vertex
u
is, the further apart it is from the

\varepsilon
-block vertices.

Using the above weighting scheme, we obtain what we call the
\varepsilon
-block partition
{\mathcal{B}}
. This is a
partition of the set of vertices into blocks such that every multi-vertex block
B\in{\mathcal{B}}
belongs to the bad part
of the graph, while every single-vertex block belongs to the good part.

We think of each multi-vertex block
B\in{\mathcal{B}}
as containing heavy vertices
w
, i.e., having
{\Uptheta}(w)>1
,
deep inside them. There is a sufficiently large buffer of light vertices between a heavy vertex in
B
and
the boundary of the block. The internal boundary of
B
consists of
\varepsilon
-block vertices.
Furthermore, each multi-vertex block
B
is small and simply structured, i.e., it is a tree with at most one extra
edge.

There are additional
secondary
properties of the blocks that we exploit in the analysis.
For example, every vertex in
u\in\partial_{\rm in}B
, i.e., the inner boundary of block
B
, is weakly connected
to the rest of the block. That is, it has only one neighbour
z\in B
, while
{\rm\Upgamma}_{\{u,z\}}\ll 1
. Establishing these
more “subtle” properties makes the analysis for the block partition intricate.

Perhaps, the scheme with the
\varepsilon
-weights we introduce here is reminiscent of the one considered in

[
27
]
. The weight of vertex
w
, there, is given by

\displaystyle{\UpA}(w)=\sum\nolimits_{{z\sim w}}{\rm\Upgamma}_{\{w,z\}}\kern 5.0pt.

The weight
{\UpA}(w)
seems relevant to the path-coupling analysis, whereas the weight

{\Uptheta}(w)
is more relevant to bounding the norms of the covariance matrices, i.e.,
\|\mathrm{Cov}_{H}(\mu_{1})\|_{2}

and
\|\mathrm{Cov}_{\partial H}(\mu_{1})\|_{2}
.

For a more detailed - formal description of all the above, see
Section

4
.
In what follows, we highlight the connection between the weight-scheme and bounding the two matrix norms.

Bounding the Covariance Matrices

We focus our discussion on how we bound
\|\mathrm{Cov}_{H}(\mu_{1})\|_{2}
;
for
\|\mathrm{Cov}_{\partial H}(\mu_{1})\|_{2}
, the high-level approach is similar.

\mathrm{Cov}_{H}(\mu_{1})
is a block matrix supported on the multi-vertex blocks
B\in{\mathcal{B}}
.
We have

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{Cov}_{H}(\mu_{1})\|_{2}=\max_{\widehat{B}}\{\|\mathrm{Cov}_{\widehat{B}}(\mu_{1})\|_{2}\}\kern 5.0pt,

where the variable
\widehat{B}
runs over the multi-vertex blocks in
{\mathcal{B}}
.

Suppose the maximum is achieved for some block
B\in{\mathcal{B}}
. Then, the focus is on bounding
\|\mathrm{Cov}_{B}(\mu_{1})\|_{2}
.
Recall that
\mathrm{Cov}_{B}(\mu_{1})
is the covariance matrix of
\mu_{1}
supported only on the vertices of block
B
.
To avoid being too technical at this early stage of the discussion, assume that
B
is a tree rooted at vertex
r
.

Since
\mathrm{Cov}_{B}(\mu_{1})
is symmetric, for a non-singular, diagonal,
V_{n}\times V_{n}
matrix
\UpD
, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{Cov}_{B}(\mu_{1})\|_{2}\leq\|\UpD^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{Cov}_{B}(\mu_{1})\cdot\UpD\|_{\infty}\kern 5.0pt.

The matrix
\UpD
we use here is defined as follows: For a parameter
\delta>0
, we specify the weight function

\upchi\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
on the set of vertices in
B
.
For the root
r
, we have
\upchi(r)=1
. Then, inductively, for every vertex
u

whose parent
z
has
\upchi(z)
specified, we let

\displaystyle\upchi(u)

\displaystyle=(1+\delta)\cdot{\rm\Upgamma}_{\{z,u\}}\cdot\upchi(z)\kern 5.0pt.

Then, for each vertex
w\in B
, we set
\UpD(w,w)=\upchi(w)
, while for each
w\notin B
, we set

\UpD(w,w)=1
.

Working like that, it turns out that the important quantities that arise in the analysis for bounding

\|\UpD^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{Cov}_{B}(\mu_{1})\cdot\UpD\|_{\infty}
, i.e., the absolute row-sums of
\UpD^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{Cov}_{B}(\mu_{1})\cdot\UpD
,
are closely related to the
\varepsilon
-weights
we introduce for the block partition. We obtain our desirable bounds for
\|\mathrm{Cov}_{H}(\mu_{1})\|_{2}
and

\|\mathrm{Cov}_{\partial H}(\mu_{1})\|_{2}
by arguing about the properties of the
\varepsilon
-block partition

{\mathcal{B}}
and calibrating
\delta
appropriately.

We obtain the bound on the modified log-Sobolev constant of
\mu_{1}
,
{C}_{\rm mLSI}(\mu_{1})
, by utilising the fact that
the components of the bad part of the graph are trees with at most one extra edge. This allows us to employ some
standard, but technical,
comparison argument
from
[
39
]
.

Concluding remarks

As mentioned earlier, our approach can be easily adapted to the case of
\{\pm 1\}
interactions,
i.e., as in
[
38
]
. In this case, we obtain rapid mixing for Glauber
dynamics for any
\beta<0.25/\sqrt{d}
, improving on
[
38
]
, which requires
\beta<0.18/\sqrt{d}
.

The improvements we achieve here over
[
38
]
stem from the different block-partition.
The main utility of matrix norms is handle the large -unbounded- couplings
we have inside the multi-vertex blocks of
{\mathcal{B}}
.

3.
Rapid Mixing using the Modified log-Sobolev Constant

Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph and let
\Omega=\{\pm 1\}^{V}
.
Consider a discrete-time, ergodic
1
1

1

A Markov chain is
ergodic
when it is aperiodic and irreducible.

Markov chain
\{X_{t}\}
with state space
\Omega
, transition matrix
P
and equilibrium distribution
\mu
. Assume also that
\{X_{t}\}
is
time-reversible

2
2

2

A Markov chain with transition matrix
P
and stationary measure

\mu
is time-reversible if it satisfies the
detailed balance equation
, i.e., for any
x,y\in\Omega
we have

\mu(x)P(x,y)=P(y,x)\mu(y).
.

The
mixing time
of this Markov chain is defined by

(3.1)

\displaystyle{\rm T}_{\rm mix}

\displaystyle=\max_{\sigma\in\Omega}\min{\left\{t>0\mid\|P^{t}(\sigma,\cdot)-\mu\|_{\rm TV}\leq{1}/{(2\mathrm{e})}\right\}}\kern 5.0pt,

where, for
t\geq 0
and
\sigma\in\Omega
, we let
P^{t}(\sigma,\cdot)
denote the distribution of
X_{t}
when the
initial state of the chain satisfies
X_{0}=\sigma
.

For function
f:\{\pm 1\}^{V}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, let
\mu(f)
be the expected value of
f
with respect to
\mu
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mu(f)

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{V}}\mu(\sigma)\cdot f(\sigma)\kern 5.0pt.

Similarly, we define the entropy of
f
with respect to
\mu
by

(3.2)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ent}_{\mu}(f)

\displaystyle=\textstyle\mu\left(f\log f\right)-\mu\left(f\right)\log\mu(f)\kern 5.0pt,

with the convention that
0\log 0=0
.

For the Markov chain
\{X_{t}\}
defined above and for any two functions
f,g:\{\pm 1\}^{V}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, we define
the associated
Dirichlet form

{\mathcal{E}}(f,g)
by

(3.3)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}(f,g)

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{\sigma,\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{V}}(f(\sigma)-f(\tau))\cdot(g(\sigma)-g(\tau))\cdot\mu(\sigma)\cdot P(\sigma,\tau)\kern 5.0pt.

We say that
\{X_{t}\}
satisfies the
modified log-Sobolev inequality
with constant
{C}_{\rm mLSI}>0
, if for any
function
f:\{\pm 1\}^{V}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
we have

(3.4)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}(f,\log f)\geq{C}_{\rm mLSI}\cdot\mathrm{Ent}_{\mu}(f)\kern 5.0pt.

The following well-known result provides an upper bound on
{\rm T}_{\rm mix}
in terms of the modified log-Sobolev constant
{C}_{\rm mLSI}
.

Fact 3.1

(
[
7
]
)
.

For an ergodic, time-reversible Markov chain with transition matrix

P
and stationary distribution
\mu
that satisfies (
3.4
), we have

(3.5)

{\rm T}_{\rm mix}\leq({C}_{\rm mLSI})^{-1}\cdot\left(\log(\log(\mu^{-1}_{\min}))+\log 2+1\right)\kern 5.0pt,

where
\mu_{\min}=\min\{\mu(\sigma)\ |\ \sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{V},\ \mu(\sigma)>0\}
.

In light of all the above, we prove
Theorem

1.1
by showing the following result.

Theorem 3.2

(Bound for the mLSI constant)
.

For any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
there exists
d_{0}=d_{0}(\varepsilon)>0
, such that for any
d\geq d_{0}
, and any

\beta\leq\beta_{c}(d)
the following is true:

Let
\mu
be the 2-spin model on
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
, with inverse temperature
\beta
. Then, with probability at
least
1-o(1)
over the instances of
\mu
, Glauber dynamics satisfies the modified log-Sobolev inequality
with constant
{C}_{\rm mLSI}\geq n^{-\left(1+\frac{24}{\sqrt{\log d}}\right)}
.

Theorem

1.1
follows as a corollory from
Theorem

3.2
and
3.1
. The proof of
Theorem

3.2
appears in
Section

6
.

4.
Vertex Weights & Block Partitions of
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)

Let
\mathbold{G}=\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
, and let
\{\mathbold{\gamma}_{e}\}_{e\in E(\mathbold{G})}
be a collection of independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) standard Gaussian random variables. We define the
V_{n}\times V_{n}

interaction matrix
\UpJ
by

(4.1)

\displaystyle\UpJ(u,w)

\displaystyle={\mathds{1}}\{u\sim w\}\times\mathbold{\gamma}_{\{u,w\}}

\displaystyle\forall u,w\in V_{n}\kern 5.0pt.

For
\beta\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and
\uph\in\mathbb{R}^{V_{n}}
, let the Gibbs distribution

\mu_{\UpJ,\beta,\uph}

be such that

(4.2)

\displaystyle\mu_{\UpJ,\beta,\uph}(\sigma)

\displaystyle\propto\exp\left(\frac{\beta}{2}\cdot\langle\sigma,\UpJ\cdot\sigma\rangle+\langle\sigma,\uph\rangle\right),

\displaystyle\quad\forall\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{V_{n}}\kern 5.0pt.

Furthermore, for each
e\in E(\mathbold{G})
we define the
edge influence
of
e
as

(4.3)

\displaystyle{\rm\Upgamma}_{e}

\displaystyle=|\tanh(\beta\cdot\UpJ_{e})|\kern 5.0pt.

For every vertex
u\in V_{n}
, we also define the
aggregate squared influence
by

(4.4)

\displaystyle{\Uptheta}(u)

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{{z\sim u}}({\rm\Upgamma}_{\{u,z\}})^{2}\kern 5.0pt.

In
Section

20.1
we prove the following tail bound for
{\Uptheta}(u)
, establishing it is well
concentrated.

Theorem 4.1
.

For
\updelta\in(0,1)
,
d>20
, and
0<\beta\leq\beta_{c}(d)
the following is true:

Consider
\mathbold{G}=\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
and the 2-spin model on this graph at inverse temperature
\beta
. For a fixed vertex
u
in
\mathbold{G}
, we have that

(4.5)

\displaystyle\Pr\left[{\Uptheta}(u)\geq{\mathbb{E}[{\Uptheta}(u)]+\frac{\updelta}{2}}\right]\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{\updelta^{2}}{8+2\updelta}\cdot d\right)\kern 5.0pt.

\varepsilon
-Block Partition:

We introduce a weighting-scheme for the vertices of the graph
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
that uses
{\Uptheta}(u)
’s.
Specifically, given
\varepsilon>0
, for any
u\in V_{n}
define the
\varepsilon
-
weight
by

(4.6)

\displaystyle\UpM(u)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}{1-\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}&\text{ if }{\Uptheta}(u)\leq{1-\frac{\varepsilon}{2},}\\
d\cdot{\Uptheta}(u)&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

Similarly, we define weights over the paths of the graph. For path
P=(w_{0},\ldots,w_{\ell})
in
\mathbold{G}
,
we define the
\varepsilon
-
weight
by

(4.7)

\displaystyle\UpM(P)

\displaystyle=\prod\nolimits_{w\in P}\UpM(w)\kern 5.0pt.

Finally, we introduce the notion of
\varepsilon
-
block vertex
. A vertex
u\in V_{n}
is called
\varepsilon
-block vertex if the following holds

(1)

every path
P
of length
\leq\log n
emanating from
u
, satisfies
\UpM(P)<1
,

(2)

for every neighbour
z
of
u
, we have
{\rm\Upgamma}_{\{u,z\}}\leq d^{-1/10}
,

(3)

\sum_{w\sim u}(\UpJ_{u,w})^{2}\leq(1+\varepsilon)d
.

We use the above
\varepsilon
-weights to introduce the notion of the
\varepsilon
-block partition.

Definition 4.2

(
\varepsilon
-Block Partition)
.

For parameter
\varepsilon>0
, consider the
\varepsilon
-weights defined in (
4.6
) and (
4.7
).
The partition
\mathcal{B}=\{B_{1},\ldots,B_{N}\}
of vertex set
V_{n}

is called
\varepsilon
-
block partition
if for every
B\in{\mathcal{B}}
the following is true:

(1)

the subgraph of
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
induced by
B
is a tree with at most one extra edge,

(2)

if
B
is a multi-vertex block, then we have the following:

(a)

every
w\in\partial_{\rm in}B
is an
\varepsilon
-block vertex and has exactly one neighbour in
B
,

(b)

if
B
contains a cycle, this is a short one, i.e., its length is at most
4\frac{\log n}{(\log d)^{4}}
,

(c)

the distance of the short cycle from the
\partial_{\rm out}B
is
\geq(150/\varepsilon)\cdot\log\log n
.

(3)

if
B=\{u\}
is a single-vertex block, then
u
is an
\varepsilon
-block vertex.

For the rest of this work, we view the
\varepsilon
-block partition of
V_{n}
as being induced by the Gibbs distribution
\mu_{\UpJ,\beta,\uph}
.

Theorem 4.3
.

For any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
\uph\in\mathbb{R}^{V_{n}}
, there exists
d_{0}=d_{0}(\varepsilon)
such that for any
d>d_{0}
and
\beta<\beta_{c}(d)
, the following is true:

Let the Gibbs distribution
\mu_{\UpJ,\beta,\uph}
, specified by
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
and interaction matrix
\UpJ
defined as
in (
4.1
). Then, with probability
1-o(1)
over the instance of

\mu_{\UpJ,\beta,\uph}
, the vertex set
V_{n}
admits an
\varepsilon
-block partition.

The proof of
Theorem

4.3
appears in
Section

15
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{B}}
is an
\varepsilon
-block partition of
V_{n}
. Let us write
H\subseteq V
for the set of vertices that belong to multi-vertex blocks of
{\mathcal{B}}
, and
\partial H=\cup_{B\in{\mathcal{B}}}\{u\in\partial_{\rm in}B\}
. We also write
S=(V\setminus H)\cup\partial H
. Then, we let the
V_{n}\times V_{n}
“partition matrices”
(\UpJ_{S},\UpJ_{H})

be such that

(4.8)

\displaystyle\UpJ_{S}(u,w)

\displaystyle=\UpJ(u,w)\cdot{\mathds{1}}\{u,w\in S\}

and

\displaystyle\UpJ_{H}(u,w)

\displaystyle=\UpJ(u,w)\cdot{\mathds{1}}\{u,w\in H\}\kern 5.0pt.

It is easy to verify that

(4.9)

\displaystyle\UpJ=\UpJ_{S}+\UpJ_{H}\kern 5.0pt.

For the sake of definiteness, when there is no
\varepsilon
-block partition for
V_{n}
,
set
S=\emptyset
and
H=\UpJ
. This also implies that
\UpJ_{S}
is the all-zeros

V_{n}\times V_{n}
matrix.

5.
Stochastic Localisation and Block Partition

In the section we show how we combine the
stochastic localisation
method and the
block partition construction we describe in
Section

4
to prove

Theorem

3.2
.

We start by giving a general overview of the use of stochastic localisation to bound
the mixing time of Glauber dynamics.

5.1.
Stochastic Localisation

For the
V_{n}\times V_{n}
interaction matrix
\UpJ
, for
\beta>0
and

\uph\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, let the Gibbs distribution
\mu_{\UpJ,\beta,\uph}

on
\{\pm 1\}^{V_{n}}
.

Stochastic localisation introduces a stochastic process
\{\mu_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}

on the probability measures on
\mathbb{R}^{V_{n}}
. This process is parametrised
with respect to the
positive-definite

V_{n}\times V_{n}
matrices

\{\UpC_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
, which are allowed to vary with
t
.

We set
\mu_{0}=\mu_{\UpJ,\beta,\uph}
, while
\mu_{t}
satisfies the following
system of stochastic differential equations: for each
x\in\mathbb{R}^{V_{n}}
,
we have

(5.1)

\displaystyle d\left(\frac{\mu_{t}}{\mu_{0}}\right)(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mu_{t}}{\mu_{0}}(x)\cdot\langle x-\Upb_{t},\UpC_{t}\cdot d\UpB_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle\forall\;0\leq t\leq 1\kern 5.0pt,

where
\Upb_{t}\in[-1,+1]^{V_{n}}
is the
barycenter
of
\mu_{t}
, i.e.,

\Upb_{t}=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbold{\sigma}}\sim\mu_{t}}[{\mathbold{\sigma}}]
. Also,
\{\UpB_{t}\}_{0\leq t\leq 1}

is the standard Brownian motion in
\mathbb{R}^{V_{n}}
.

Fact 5.1

(
[
12
]
)
.

For every
t\in[0,1]
, almost surely
\mu_{t}
attains the form

(5.2)

\displaystyle\mu_{t}(\sigma)

\displaystyle\propto\exp\left(\frac{\beta}{2}\cdot\langle\sigma,\UpJ_{t}\cdot\sigma\rangle+\langle\sigma,\uph_{t}\rangle\right),

\displaystyle\quad\forall\;\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{V_{n}}\kern 5.0pt,

such that
\UpJ_{t}=\UpJ-\int^{t}_{0}\UpC^{2}_{s}ds
and
\uph_{t}=\int^{t}_{0}[\UpC_{s}\cdot d\UpB_{s}+\UpC^{2}_{s}\cdot\Upb(\mu_{s})]ds
.

We use stochastic localisation to bound the modified log-Sobolev constant
for Glauber dynamics on
\mu_{0}
by exploiting results from
[
12
]
.

If the process
\{\mu_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
is such that for each function

f:\mathbb{R}^{V_{n}}\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, we have

(5.3)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{Ent}_{\mu_{1}}(f)]}{\mathrm{Ent}_{\mu_{0}}(f)}\geq\upxi\kern 5.0pt,

while, almost surely
\mu_{1}
is such that

(5.4)

\displaystyle{C}_{\rm mLSI}(\mu_{1})>\uppsi\kern 5.0pt,

then,
[
12
, Theorem 47]
implies that

(5.5)

\displaystyle{C}_{\rm mLSI}(\mu_{0})>\uppsi\cdot\upxi\kern 5.0pt.

The general idea is to introduce appropriate control matrices
\{\UpC_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}

that would guarantee that the quantities
\upxi
and
\uppsi
in (
5.3
)
and (
5.4
), respectively, are not too small. A standard case to
have is that
\mu_{1}
is a product measure on
\{\pm 1\}^{V_{n}}
. For the distributions
we consider here, we do not expect to have such a simple measure for
\mu_{1}
.

We bound the parameter
\upxi
in (
5.3
) by means of the
control matrices
\UpC_{t}
and the
covariance matrix
of
\mu_{t}
. Recall that
the
V_{n}\times V_{n}
covariance matrix of
\mu_{t}=\mu_{J_{t},\beta,\uph_{t}}
,
denoted as
\mathrm{Cov}(\UpJ_{t},\beta,\uph_{t})
, is defined by

(5.6)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cov}(\UpJ_{t},\beta,\uph_{t})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbold{\sigma}}\cdot{\mathbold{\sigma}}^{T}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbold{\sigma}}\right]\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbold{\sigma}}^{T}\right]\kern 5.0pt,

where the random variable
{\mathbold{\sigma}}\in\{\pm 1\}^{n}
is distributed according to

\mu_{\UpJ_{t},\beta,\uph}
. Also, recall from
5.1
that

\UpJ_{t}=\UpJ-\int^{t}_{0}\UpC^{2}_{s}ds
and

\uph_{t}=\int^{t}_{0}[\UpC_{s}\cdot d\UpB_{s}+\UpC^{2}_{s}\cdot\Upb(\mu_{s})]ds
.

More specifically, suppose that almost surely, for all
t\in[0,1]
and any

\uph^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, there exists
\alpha_{t}>0
such that

(5.7)

\displaystyle\beta\cdot\|\UpC_{t}\cdot\mathrm{Cov}(\UpJ_{t},\beta,\uph^{\prime})\cdot\UpC_{t}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\upalpha_{t}\kern 5.0pt.

Then, for any function
f:\{\pm 1\}^{V_{n}}\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{Ent}_{\mu_{1}}(f)]

\displaystyle\geq\exp\left(-\int^{1}_{0}\upalpha_{t}\cdot dt\right)\cdot\mathrm{Ent}_{\mu_{0}}(f)\kern 5.0pt.

The above approach is formally presented in the following results which is proved
in
[
38
]
. It is worth mentioning that
Theorem

5.2

relies heavily on results in
[
12
]
.

Theorem 5.2

(
[
38
]
)
.

Let
\beta>0
, also let
\UpM_{1}
and
\UpM_{2}
be matrices
\mathbb{R}^{V_{n}\times V_{n}}

with
\UpM_{1}
being positive definite and suppose that there are
\Upq,\updelta>0

such that for every external field
\uph^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{V_{n}}
and
t\in[0,1]
we have

(1)

\beta\cdot\|\UpM^{1/2}_{1}\cdot\mathrm{Cov}((1-t)\UpM_{1}+\UpM_{2},\beta,\uph^{\prime})\cdot\UpM^{1/2}_{1}\|_{2}\leq\Upq
,

(2)

Glauber dynamics for
\mu_{\UpM_{2},\uph^{\prime}}
satisfies

{C}_{\rm mLSI}(\mu_{\UpM_{2},\beta,\uph^{\prime}})\geq\updelta
.

Then, for any external field
\uph\in\mathbb{R}^{V_{n}}
, we have

(5.8)

\displaystyle{C}_{\rm mLSI}(\mu_{\UpM_{1}+\UpM_{2},\beta,\uph})\geq\updelta\cdot e^{-\Upq}\kern 5.0pt.

We use a slightly different notation from
[
38
]
. For this reason
the above theorem may look different than what is stated in the paper, e.g. in the
first condition we have an extra factor
\beta
.

5.2.
Stochastic Localisation with Block Partition

For a
V_{n}\times V_{n}
interaction matrix
\UpJ
, for
\beta>0
and

\uph\in\mathbb{R}
, let the Gibbs distribution
\mu_{\UpJ,\beta,\uph}
.
For appropriately chosen
\varepsilon>0
, suppose that
(\UpJ,\beta)

admits a
\varepsilon
-block partition with partition matrices
(\UpJ_{S},\UpJ_{H})
.
Consider the
V_{n}\times V_{n}
matrix

(5.9)

\displaystyle\overline{\UpJ}=\UpJ_{S}+\UpJ_{H}+(1+\updelta)\cdot\|\UpJ_{S}\|_{2}\cdot\UpI_{S}+\frac{\upzeta\cdot\sqrt{d}}{\log n}\cdot\UpI_{H\setminus\partial H}

where
\updelta>0
and
\upzeta>0
are parameters which we specify later in
the analysis. We think of those as very small numbers. Also, consider the Gibbs
distribution
\mu_{\overline{\UpJ},\beta,\uph}
. Note that, since

\overline{\UpJ}-\UpJ
is a diagonal matrix,
\mu_{\overline{\UpJ},\beta,\uph}
and

\mu_{\UpJ,\beta,\uph}
correspond to exactly the same distribution.

We exploit the above set-up to introduce a stochastic localisation process

\{\mu_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
such that
\mu_{0}=\mu_{\overline{\UpJ},\beta,\uph}
,
while for each
t\in[0,1]
, we have

(5.10)

\displaystyle\UpC_{t}

\displaystyle=\left(\UpJ_{S}+\left(1+{\textstyle\frac{\updelta}{2}}\right)\cdot\|\UpJ_{S}\|_{2}\cdot\UpI_{S}+{\textstyle\frac{\upzeta}{\log n}}\cdot\UpI_{H\setminus\partial H}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\kern 5.0pt,

where the quantities
\updelta
and
\upzeta
above are the same as those in
(
5.9
). Note that the process is well-defined. It is
straightforward to check that
\UpC_{t}\succ 0
.

We use the above scheme to bound the modified log-Sobolev constant in

Theorem

3.2
using
Theorem

5.2
. Specifically,
we apply
Theorem

5.2
for
\UpM_{1}\approx(1-t)\UpC^{2}_{t}

and
\UpM_{2}\approx\UpJ_{H}
.

6.
Proof of
Theorem

3.2

Consider
\mathbold{G}=\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
, and the set i.i.d standard normal random
variables
\{\mathbold{\gamma}_{e}\}_{e\in E(\mathbold{G})}
, while we let the
V_{n}\times V_{n}

interaction matrix
\UpJ
be such that

(6.1)

\displaystyle\UpJ(u,w)

\displaystyle={\mathds{1}}\{\{u,w\}\in E(\mathbold{G})\}\times\mathbold{\gamma}_{\{u,w\}}

\displaystyle\forall u,w\in V_{n}\kern 5.0pt.

For
\beta\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
\uph\in\mathbb{R}^{V_{n}}
, we let the Gibbs distribution
\mu_{\beta,\uph,\UpJ}

be such that

(6.2)

\displaystyle\mu_{\UpJ,\beta,\uph}(\sigma)

\displaystyle\propto\exp\left(\frac{\beta}{2}\cdot\langle\sigma,\UpJ\cdot\sigma\rangle+\langle\sigma,\uph\rangle\right),

\displaystyle\quad\forall\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{V_{n}}\kern 5.0pt.

Theorem 6.1
.

For any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
there exists
d_{0}=d_{0}(\varepsilon)>0
, for any
d\geq d_{0}
and
0<\beta\leq\beta_{c}(d)

the following is true:

Consider
\UpJ=\UpJ(\mathbold{G}(n,d/n),\{\mathbold{\gamma}_{e}\})
defined as in (
6.1
).
With probability
1-o(1)
, there is an
\varepsilon
-block partition of
(\UpJ,\beta)
with partition matrices
(\UpJ_{S},\UpJ_{H})
such that

(6.3)

\displaystyle\|\UpJ_{S}\|_{2}\leq 2\sqrt{d}(1+o(1))\kern 5.0pt.

The proof of
Theorem

6.1
appears in
Section

8
.

Theorem 6.2
.

For any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
satisfying
1-\varepsilon/8\geq\upkappa
, there exist constants

\upzeta>0
,
d_{0}=d_{0}(\varepsilon)>0

and
C=C(\zeta)>0
such that for any
d\geq d_{0}
, for any field
\uph\in\mathbb{R}^{V_{n}}
and

0<\beta\leq\beta_{c}(d)
the following is true:

Consider
\UpJ=\UpJ(\mathbold{G}(n,d/n),\{\mathbold{\gamma}_{e}\})
defined as in (
6.1
).
With probability
1-o(1)
, there is an
\varepsilon
-block partition of
(\UpJ,\beta)
with partition matrices
(\UpJ_{S},\UpJ_{H})
, while for matrices

\displaystyle\overline{\UpJ}

\displaystyle=\UpJ_{S}+\UpJ_{H}+\left(1+{\textstyle\frac{\varepsilon}{100}}\right)\cdot\|\UpJ_{S}\|_{2}\cdot\UpI_{S}+\frac{\upzeta}{L}\cdot\UpI_{H\setminus\partial H}\kern 5.0pt,

\displaystyle\UpC_{t}

\displaystyle=\left(\UpJ_{S}+\left(1+{\textstyle\frac{\varepsilon}{200}}\right)\cdot\|\UpJ_{S}\|_{2}\cdot\UpI_{S}+{\textstyle\frac{\upzeta}{\sqrt{d}\cdot L}}\cdot\UpI_{H\setminus\partial H}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

and
\UpJ_{t}=\overline{\UpJ}-\int^{t}_{0}\UpC^{2}_{s}\cdot ds
, we have

(6.4)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cov}[\UpJ_{t},\beta,\uph]

\displaystyle\preceq C\cdot L\cdot\UpI

\displaystyle\forall t\in[0,1]\kern 5.0pt,

where
L=\frac{\log n}{\sqrt{d}}
.

In
Theorem

6.2
, the quantification of
C>0
implies that the constant is independent of
d
.
We prove
Theorem

6.2
in
Section

9
.

Theorem 6.3
.

For any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
there exists
d_{0}=d_{0}(\varepsilon)>0
such that for any
d>d_{0}
,
for any
\uph\in\mathbb{R}^{V_{n}}
and
0<\beta\leq\beta_{c}(d)
there exists a constant
C_{0}>0
such that the following is true:

Consider
\UpJ=\UpJ(\mathbold{G}(n,d/n),\{\mathbold{\gamma}_{e}\})
defined as in (
6.1
).
With probability
1-o(1)
, there is an
\varepsilon
-block partition of
(\UpJ,\beta)
with partition matrices
(\UpJ_{S},\UpJ_{H})
such that

(6.5)

\displaystyle{C}_{\rm mLSI}(\mu_{\UpJ_{H},\beta,\uph})

\displaystyle\geq C_{0}\cdot n^{-\left(1+\frac{24}{\sqrt{\log d}}\right)}\kern 5.0pt,

where
{C}_{\rm mLSI}(\mu_{\UpJ_{H},\beta,\uph})
is the modified log-Sobolev constant for
Glauber dynamics on
\mu_{\UpJ_{H},\beta,\uph}
.

The proof of
Theorem

6.3
appears in
Section

13
.

Proof of
Theorem

3.2
.

Consider the interaction matrix
\UpJ=\UpJ(\mathbold{G}(n,d/n),\{\mathbold{\gamma}_{e}\})
defined as in (
6.1
).
Let
{\mathcal{E}}
be the event that
(\UpJ,\beta)
admits an
\varepsilon
-partition with partition matrices
(\UpJ_{S},\UpJ_{H})
such that
for any
t\in[0,1]
, for
0<\beta\leq\beta_{c}
, for constants
C,C_{0}>0
and
L
as specified in
Theorem

6.2
and for any
\uph\in\mathbb{R}^{V_{n}}
,
we have

(1)

\|\UpJ_{S}\|_{2}\leq 2\sqrt{d}(1+o(1))
,

(2)

\mathrm{Cov}[\UpJ_{t},\beta,\uph]\preceq C\cdot L\cdot\UpI
,

(3)

{C}_{\rm mLSI}(\mu_{\UpJ_{H},\beta,\uph})\geq C_{0}\cdot n^{-\left(1+\frac{24}{\sqrt{\log d}}\right)}
.

where matrix
\UpJ_{T}
is defined with respect to
\UpJ_{S},\UpJ_{H}
, as specified
in the statement of
Theorem

6.2
.

From
Theorems

6.1
,
6.2
and
6.3
we have that
\Pr[{\mathcal{E}}]=1-o(1)
.
Then,
Theorem

3.2
follows by showing that on the event
{\mathcal{E}}
,
Glauber dynamics on
\mu_{\UpJ,\beta,\uph}
satisfies the modified log-Sobolev inequality with constant

{C}_{\rm mLSI}
such that

(6.6)

\displaystyle{C}_{\rm mLSI}\geq C_{0}\cdot n^{-\left(1+\frac{24}{\sqrt{\log d}}+\frac{1}{d^{1/4}}\right)}>n^{-\left(1+\frac{25}{d^{1/100}}\right)}\kern 5.0pt.

We show that on the event
{\mathcal{E}}
, we have

(6.7)

\displaystyle\beta\cdot\|\UpC_{t}\cdot\mathrm{Cov}[\UpJ_{j},\beta,\uph]\cdot\UpC_{t}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\cdot L\leq{\textstyle\frac{\log n}{d^{1/4}}}\kern 5.0pt,

(6.8)

\displaystyle{C}_{\rm mLSI}(\mu_{\UpJ_{H},\beta,\uph})

\displaystyle\geq C_{0}\cdot n^{-\left(1+\frac{24}{\sqrt{\log d}}\right)}\kern 5.0pt,

where matrix
\UpC_{t}
is defined in the statement of
Theorem

6.2
.

Before proving that (
6.7
), (
6.8
) hold, we observe that
(
6.6
) follows by combining
these two inequalities and
Theorem

5.2
.
Hence, it remains to show (
6.7
) and
(
6.8
).

Event
{\mathcal{E}}
implies that (
6.8
) is true, i.e., due to item 3.
We proceed to show that
{\mathcal{E}}
also implies that (
6.7
) is true.

Standard calculations, e.g., see
A.1
, imply

(6.9)

\displaystyle\beta\cdot\|\UpC_{t}\cdot\mathrm{Cov}[\UpJ_{t},\beta,\uph]\cdot\UpC_{t}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\beta\cdot\|\mathrm{Cov}[\UpJ_{t},\beta,\uph]\|_{2}\cdot\|\UpC^{2}_{t}\|_{2}\kern 5.0pt.

The definition of matrix
\UpC_{t}
in
Theorem

6.2
, item 1, and
our assumption that
\beta\leq\beta_{c}
, imply

(6.10)

\displaystyle\beta\cdot\|\UpC^{2}_{t}\|_{2}\leq 1\kern 5.0pt.

Item 2 above implies

(6.11)

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{Cov}[\UpJ_{t},\beta,\uph]\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\cdot L\kern 5.0pt.

Plugging (
6.11
) and
(
6.10
) into (
6.9
)
we obtain (
6.7
).

All the above conclude the proof of
Theorem

3.2
.
∎

7.
Preliminaries for the analysis

7.1.
Tree of self-avoiding walks construction

Consider a (fixed) graph
G=(V,E)
and the Gibbs distribution
\mu:\{\pm 1\}^{V}\to[0,1]
.

For
\Lambda\subset V
and a configuration
\tau
at
\Lambda
, the
pairwise
influence matrix

\mathcal{I}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
is indexed by the vertices in

V\setminus\Lambda
such that, for
u,w\in V\setminus\Lambda
, we have

(7.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}(w,u)

\displaystyle=\mu_{u}(+1\ |\ (\Lambda,\tau),(w,+1))-\mu_{u}(+1\ |\ (\Lambda,\tau),(w,-1))\kern 5.0pt,

where
\mu_{u}(+1\ |\ (\Lambda,\tau),(w,+1))
is the probability of the event that vertex

u
has configuration
+1
, conditional on the configuration at set
\Lambda
is
\tau
and
the configuration at
w
is
+1
. We define
\mu_{u}(+1\ |\ (\Lambda,\tau),(w,-1))
, analogously.

When set
\Lambda=\emptyset
, then we denote the influence matrix by
\mathcal{I}_{G}
.

Lemma 7.1
.

For the
V\times V
interaction matrix
\UpJ
and
\beta>0
, let the Gibbs distribution

\mu_{\UpJ,\beta,0}
, i.e. the external field
\uph=0
.
We have
\mathrm{Cov}(\UpJ,\beta,0)=4^{-1}\cdot\mathcal{I}_{G}
.

Proof.

We abbreviate
\mathrm{Cov}(\UpJ,\beta,0)
to
\mathrm{Cov}
.

The definitions of
\mathrm{Cov}
and
\mathcal{I}_{G}
imply that for any
u,v\in V
we have

\mathcal{I}_{G}(u,v)=\frac{\mathrm{Cov}(u,v)}{\mathrm{Cov}(u,u)}
. Then,
Lemma

7.1

follows from the observation that
\mathrm{Cov}(u,u)=4^{-1}
, for all
u\in V
.
∎

The
T_{\rm SAW}
construction:

Let
G=(V,E)
be a fixed graph and let the Gibbs distribution
\mu_{G}:\{\pm 1\}^{V}\to[0,1]
.
Assume w.l.o.g. that there is a
total ordering
of the vertices in
G
.

For each vertex
w\in V
, we define
T={T_{\rm SAW}}(G,w)
, the
tree of self-avoiding walks

starting from
w
, as follows:
Consider the set consisting of every walk
v_{0},\ldots,v_{r}
in graph
G
that emanates
from vertex
w
, i.e.,
v_{0}=w
, while one of the following two holds

(1)

v_{0},\ldots,v_{r}
is a self-avoiding walk,

(2)

v_{0},\ldots,v_{r-1}
is a self-avoiding walk, while there is
j\leq r-3
such that
v_{r}=v_{j}
.

Each of these walks corresponds to a vertex in
T
. Two vertices in
T
are adjacent
if the corresponding walks are adjacent, i.e., one walk extends the other by one vertex.

The vertex in
T
that corresponds to the walk
v_{0},\ldots,v_{r}
,
is called “copy of vertex
v_{r}
”, i.e.,
v_{r}
is the last vertex in the path.
For each vertex
v
in
G
, we let
{\tt A}(v)
be the set of its copies in
T
.
Note that vertex
|{\tt A}(v)|\geq 1
.

In tree
T
we follow the convention to consider as its root the vertex that corresponds to
the self-avoiding walk of length
\ell=0
. Clearly, the root is a copy of vertex
w
.

For
\Lambda\subset V
and
\tau\in\{\pm\}^{\Lambda}
, let the influence matrix

\mathcal{I}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
induced by
\mu^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
. We describe how
the entry
\mathcal{I}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}(w,u)
can be expressed using an appropriately
defined spin-system on
T={T_{\rm SAW}}(G,w)
.

Let
\mu_{T}
be a Gibbs distribution on
T
which has the same specification as
\mu_{G}
.
Each
z\in{\tt A}(x)
in tree
T
, such that
x\in\Lambda
, is pinned to configuration

\tau(x)
. Furthermore, if vertex
z
in
T
corresponds to walk
v_{0},\ldots,v_{\ell}

in
G
such that
v_{\ell}=v_{j}
, for
0\leq j\leq\ell-3
, then we set a pinning at vertex
z
,
as well. This pinning depends on the total ordering of the vertices. Particularly, we set at
z

(1)

-1
if
v_{j+1}>v_{\ell-1}
,

(2)

+1
otherwise.

Let
\Gamma=\Gamma(G,\Lambda)
be the set of vertices in
T
which have been pinned
in the above construction, while let
\sigma=\sigma(G,\tau)
be the pinning at
\Gamma
.

Let
\mathcal{I}^{\Gamma,\sigma}_{T}
be the influence matrix that is induced by the Gibbs distribution

\mu^{\Gamma,\sigma}_{T}
, i.e., in tree
T
. The following results gives a relation between the influence
matrices
\mathcal{I}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}
and
\mathcal{I}^{\Gamma,\sigma}_{T}
.

Proposition 7.2

(
[
5
,
13
]
)
.

For every
u,w\in V\setminus\Lambda
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{\Lambda,\tau}_{G}(w,u)

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{v\in{\tt A}(u)}\mathcal{I}^{\Gamma,\sigma}_{T}(r,v)\kern 5.0pt,

where
r
is the root of
{T_{\rm SAW}}(G,w)
, while recall that
{\tt A}(u)
is the set of copies of vertex
u
in this tree.

Bounding Influences on trees:

Consider tree
T=(V,E)
rooted at vertex
r
. Let the Gibbs distribution
\mu_{T}
be specified as
in (
7.1
). For
K\subseteq V\setminus\{r\}
and
\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{K}
, let
the
ratio of marginals

(7.2)

\displaystyle{R}^{K,\tau}_{r}=\frac{\mu^{K,\tau}_{r}(+1)}{\mu^{K,\tau}_{r}(-1)}\kern 5.0pt.

Recall that
\mu^{K,\tau}_{r}(\cdot)
denotes the marginal of
\mu^{K,\tau}(\cdot)

at the root
r
. The above allows for
{R}^{K,\tau}_{r}=\infty
when

\mu^{K,\tau}_{r}(-1)=0
.

For vertex
u\in T
, we let
T_{u}
be the subtree of
T
that includes
u
and all its descendants.
We always assume that the root of
T_{u}
is vertex
u
. With a slight abuse of notation, we let

{R}^{K,\tau}_{u}
denote the ratio of marginals at the root for the subtree
T_{u}
, where the
Gibbs distribution is with respect to
T_{u}
, while we impose pinning
\tau(K\cap T_{u})
.

For any edge
e\in E
, we let the function

(7.3)

\displaystyle h_{e}:[-\infty,+\infty]\to\mathbb{R}

s.t.

\displaystyle x\mapsto-\frac{(1-\exp(4\beta\UpJ_{e}))\cdot\exp(x)}{(\exp(x+2\beta\UpJ_{e})+1)(\exp(x)+\exp(2\beta\UpJ_{e}))}\kern 5.0pt.

For each edge
e
in
T
, we specify weight
\upphi(e)
as follows: letting
e=\{x,z\}
be
such that
x
is the parent of
z
in
T
, we set

\displaystyle\upphi(e)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}0&&\textrm{if there is a pinning at either $x$ or $z$},\\
\textstyle h_{e}\left(\log{R}^{\Gamma,\sigma}_{z}\right)&&\textrm{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

We have the following result from
[
13
]

Lemma 7.3

(
[
13
]
)
.

Let vertex
u\in T
,
K\subset V\setminus\{v,r\}
and
\tau\in\{\pm 1\}^{K}
.
For the influence matrix
\mathcal{I}^{K,\tau}_{T}
, induced by
\mu^{K,\tau}_{T}
,
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{K,\tau}_{T}(r,u)

\displaystyle=\prod\nolimits_{e\in P}\upphi(e)\kern 5.0pt,

where
P
is the path from the root
r
to vertex
u
in
T
.

7.2.
All Paths Tree

We introduce a tree construction for the multi-vertex blocks of the
\varepsilon
-partition

{\mathcal{B}}
that we call the
all paths tree
. That is, this tree construction applies only to
graphs that are trees or unicyclic.

Consider first the unicyclic graph
G=(V,E)
. That is, suppose for some integer
\ell>2
,

G
contains the cycle
C=x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell}
. We assume a total ordering of the vertices
of
G
.

Consider a vertex
w\in V
, while w.l.o.g. assume that vertex
x_{1}
in cycle
C
is the vertex
that is closest to
w
, while
\{x_{1},z\}
is the first edge of the shortest path from
x_{1}
to
w
.
Also, assume that
x_{2}>x_{\ell}
in the total ordering.

Let
G_{1}
be the connected component containing the vertices in cycle
C

after we delete the edges
\{x_{1},z\}
and
\{x_{1},x_{\ell}\}
from
G
.
Similarly, let
G_{2}
be the connected component containing the vertices in
C
after
deleting the edges
\{x_{1},z\}
and
\{x_{1},x_{2}\}
from
G
.

We define the all paths tree
\mathcal{T}_{\rm AP}(G,w)
rooted at
w
as follows:
We let the trees
T_{\rm base}={T_{\rm SAW}}(G,w)
,
T_{\rm high}={T_{\rm SAW}}(G_{1},x_{1})
and
T_{\rm low}={T_{\rm SAW}}(G_{2},x_{1})
.
The tree
\mathcal{T}_{\rm AP}(G,w)
is constructed by appending
T_{\rm high}
and
T_{\rm low}
to
T_{\rm base}
.
Specifically, since
T_{\rm base}
is a tree of self-avoiding walks that start from
w
, there are exactly
two leaves in
T_{\rm base}
which are copies of vertex
x_{1}
. The first copy, vertex
u_{\rm high}
corresponds
to the path from
w
to
x_{1}
and then to
x_{2},\ldots x_{1}
. Similarly, the second copy, vertex

u_{\rm low}
corresponds to the path from
w
to
x_{1}
and then to
x_{\ell},x_{\ell-1},\ldots x_{1}
.
We obtain
\mathcal{T}_{\rm AP}(G,w)
by hanging the two trees
T_{\rm high}
and
T_{\rm low}
, from the leaves
u_{\rm high}

and
u_{\rm low}
, of the tree
T_{\rm base}
, respectively.

If graph
G
is a tree, then we have the above construction with

T_{\rm base}={T_{\rm SAW}}(G,w)
while
T_{\rm high}
and
T_{\rm low}
being empty. That is,
\mathcal{T}_{\rm AP}(G,w)
and
G

are identical.

For what follows, we extend the notion of the copy of a vertex to
\mathcal{T}_{\rm AP}(G,w)
in the natural way,
i.e., as in the case of self-avoiding walk trees.

Also, when we define the all-path tree
\mathcal{T}_{\rm AP}(G,w)
we refer to the subtrees
T_{\rm base}
,
T_{\rm high}

and
T_{\rm low}
in a standard way, i.e., without redefining them. Similarly, for
u_{\rm low}
and
u_{\rm high}
,
the copies of vertex
x_{1}
.

Lemma 7.4

(All paths property)
.

For
G=(V,E)
which is a tree with at most one extra edge and for
w\in V
the following
is true: For every self-avoiding walk
P=z_{1},\ldots,z_{\ell}
in
G
, there exists a self-avoiding
walk
Q=y_{1},\ldots,y_{\ell}
in
\mathcal{T}_{\rm AP}(G,w)
such that
y_{i}
is a copy of vertex
z_{i}
,
for all
i=1,\ldots,\ell
.

Proof.

When
G
is a tree,
Lemma

7.4
is true because
\mathcal{T}_{\rm AP}(G,w)
is identical
to
G
. We now focus on the case where
G
is a unicyclic graph, with cycle
C=x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell}

as we describe above.

It is not hard to verify that each self-avoiding walk
P=z_{1},\ldots,z_{\ell}
that does
not

include the subpath
x_{\ell},x_{1},x_{2}
or subpath
x_{2},x_{1},x_{\ell}
, there exists walk

Q=y_{1},\ldots,y_{\ell}
in
\mathcal{T}_{\rm AP}(G,w)
such that
y_{i}
is a copy of vertex
z_{i}
, for all

i=1,\ldots,\ell
. In particular, such walk
Q
appears in tree
T_{\rm base}
. Hence, since

T_{\rm base}
is a subtree of
\mathcal{T}_{\rm AP}(G,w)
,
Q
is also included in
\mathcal{T}_{\rm AP}(G,w)
.

Consider now a self-avoiding walk
P
that includes the subpath
x_{\ell},x_{1},x_{2}
. For
such
P
let the subpath
P_{1}=z_{1},\ldots,z_{k-1},z_{k}
be such that
z_{k-1}=x_{\ell}

and
z_{k}=x_{1}
. Also, let the subpath
P_{2}=z_{k},\ldots,z_{\ell}
. We have
z_{k}=x_{1}

and
z_{k+1}=x_{2}
.

It is not hard to verify that in
T_{\rm base}
there is walk
Q_{1}=y_{1},\ldots,y_{k}
such that
y_{i}

is a copy of
z_{i}
, for all
1\leq i\leq k
. Moreover, vertex
y_{k}
is the leaf
u_{\rm high}
of

T_{\rm base}
, i.e., the leaf from which we hang
T_{\rm high}
. In the subtree
T_{\rm high}
, there is walk

Q_{2}=y_{k},\ldots,y_{\ell}
such that
y_{i}
is a copy of
z_{i}
, for all
k\leq i\leq\ell
, while

y_{k}
is the root of
T_{\rm high}
. Then, the walk
Q
in
\mathcal{T}_{\rm AP}(G,w)
we are looking for
corresponds to the concatenation of walks
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
.

With a very similar argument we treat the case of self-avoiding walk
P=z_{1},\ldots,z_{\ell}

that includes the subpath
x_{2},x_{1},x_{\ell}
.

All the above conclude the proof of
Lemma

7.4
.
∎

From the above construction it is not hard to obtain the following corollary

Corollary 7.5
.

For
G=(V,E)
which is a tree with at most one extra edge and for
w\in V
the following
is true: Each each vertex
v\in V
has at most 4 copies in
\mathcal{T}_{\rm AP}(G,w)
.

7.3.
Properties of the random graph
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)

In a few places in the analysis, we make use of the following, standard to prove,
lemma about the random graph
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
.

Lemma 7.6
.

There exist
d_{0}\geq 1
such that for every
d\geq d_{0}
, the following is true:

Let
{\mathcal{E}}_{S}
be the event that “every set of vertices
S
in
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
with

|S|\leq 2\frac{\log n}{(\log d)^{2}}
, spans at most
|S|
edges.” Then, we have

\Pr[{\mathcal{E}}_{S}]\geq 1-n^{-3/4}
.

For a proof, see
Section

A.1
.

8.
Proof of
Theorem

6.1

For
\varepsilon,\beta,d
as specified in the statement of
Theorem

6.1
,
consider the
V_{n}\times V_{n}
matrix
\UpJ
specified as in (
6.1
),
with respect to the random graph
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
.

We let
{\mathcal{P}}
be the event that
(\UpJ,\beta)
admits an
\varepsilon
-block partition
{\mathcal{B}}
.
On the event
{\mathcal{P}}
, recall from
Section

4
the partition sets
S
and
H
, as
well as the partition matrices
(\UpJ_{S},\UpJ_{H})
.

We utilise the weighted non-backtracking spectrum of
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
and a
generalisation of the Ihara-Bass type formula from
[
30
]
to obtain
the desired bound on
\|\UpJ_{S}\|_{2}
.
A similar approach was followed in
[
38
]
, and it
utilises results from the work of Stephan & Massoulié
[
48
]
.
A technical challenge we need to circumvent is here
that the typical instances of
\UpJ
have unbounded entries.

Let the set of i.i.d. random variables
\{\widetilde{\mathbold{\gamma}}_{e}:e\in E(\mathbold{G})\}
, where
each
\widetilde{\mathbold{\gamma}}_{e}
is a standard Gaussian truncated on the interval

[-\varepsilon\sqrt{d},\varepsilon\sqrt{d}]
. Then, matrix
{\Upsigma}
is such that

(8.1)

\displaystyle{\Upsigma}(u,w)

\displaystyle={\mathds{1}}\left\{|\UpJ(u,w)|>\varepsilon\sqrt{d}\right\}\cdot\widetilde{\mathbold{\gamma}}_{\{u,w\}}+{\mathds{1}}\left\{|\UpJ(u,w)|\leq\varepsilon\sqrt{d}\right\}\cdot\UpJ(u,w)

\displaystyle\forall u,w\in V_{n}\kern 5.0pt.

From the above definition, it is elementary to verify that for each
\{u,w\}\in E(\mathbold{G})
, the entry

{\Upsigma}(u,w)
is distributed as in
{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
, conditional on
|{\Upsigma}(u,w)|<\varepsilon\sqrt{d}
.

On the event
{\mathcal{P}}
, we define
{\Upsigma}_{S}
, i.e., similarly to the partition matrix
\UpJ_{S}
.
That is,

\displaystyle{\Upsigma}_{S}(u,w)

\displaystyle={\mathds{1}}\{u,w\in S\}\cdot{\Upsigma}(u,w)

\displaystyle\forall u,w\in V_{n}\kern 5.0pt.

Recall that if the event
{\mathcal{P}}
does not hold, then the partition set
S=\emptyset

and hence
{\Upsigma}_{S}
is the all-zeros matrix.

The definition of
{\Upsigma}
implies that for edges
\{u,w\}\in E(\mathbold{G})
such that
|\UpJ(u,w)|\leq\varepsilon\sqrt{d}
,
we also have
\UpJ(u,w)={\Upsigma}(u,w)
. Furthermore, the definition of the
\varepsilon
-block partition, implies
that all the edges
e=\{u,w\}
with
u,w\in S
satisfy
|\UpJ(u,w)|\leq\varepsilon\sqrt{d}
. We have

(8.2)

\displaystyle\Pr[\UpJ_{S}

\displaystyle={\Upsigma}_{S}]=1\kern 5.0pt.

Proposition 8.1
.

For
d>1
,
\beta>0
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
as in the statement of
Theorem

6.1
,
we have

(8.3)

\displaystyle\Pr[{\mathds{1}}\{{\mathcal{P}}\}\cdot\|{\Upsigma}_{S}\|_{2}\leq 2\sqrt{d}\cdot(1+o(1))]=1-o(1)\kern 5.0pt.

Proof of
Theorem

6.1
.

We have

\displaystyle\Pr[\|\UpJ_{S}\|_{2}\leq 2\sqrt{d}\cdot(1+o(1))\ |\ {\mathcal{P}}]

\displaystyle=\Pr[{\mathds{1}}\{{\mathcal{P}}\}\cdot\|\UpJ_{S}\|_{2}\leq 2\sqrt{d}\cdot(1+o(1))\ |\ {\mathcal{P}}]

\displaystyle=\frac{\Pr[{\mathds{1}}\{{\mathcal{P}}\}\cdot\|\UpJ_{S}\|_{2}\leq 2\sqrt{d}\cdot(1+o(1))]}{\Pr[{\mathcal{P}}]}=1-o(1)\kern 5.0pt.

For the last equality we bound from above the nominator by using (
8.2
) and
Proposition

8.1

while we bound from below the denominator using
Theorem

4.3
.
∎

8.1.
Proof of
Proposition

8.1

In what follows, we let
{\rho}(W)
denote the
spectral radius
of matrix
W
, i.e.,
the maximum in magnitude eigenvalue.

Furthermore, for each
symmetric
matrix
W\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, we
let
\UpB_{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{n(n-1)\times n(n-1)}
be such that

(8.4)

\displaystyle\UpB_{W}\left((i,j),(k,\ell)\right)=\begin{cases}W_{k,\ell}&\text{ if }i\neq\ell\text{ and }j=k,\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

We call
\UpB_{W}
the
non-backtracking matrix induced by

W
.

Proposition 8.2
.

For any
\delta\in(0,1)
, for any
L>0
, for any
symmetric
\UpQ\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, such that
\max_{i,j}|\UpQ(i,j)|\leq L
the following is true:

For
\lambda=\max\{{\rho}(\UpB_{\UpQ}),L\}
, where
\UpB_{\UpQ}
is
defined as in (
8.4
),
we have

(8.5)

\displaystyle\|\UpQ\|_{2}<\frac{\lambda}{1-\delta}+\frac{1-\delta}{\lambda-(1-\delta)L}\cdot\left\|\UpQ^{\circ 2}\right\|_{\infty}\kern 5.0pt,

where
\UpQ^{\circ 2}
denotes the
2
nd Hadamard power of
\UpQ
.

Furthermore, (
8.5
) holds for
\widehat{\UpQ}
, any principal submatrix of
\UpQ
.

Lemma 8.3
.

For matrix
{\Upsigma}
defined in (
8.1
) and
\UpB_{{\Upsigma}}

defined as in (
8.4
), we have

\displaystyle\Pr[{\rho}(\UpB_{{\Upsigma}})\leq(1+o(1))\cdot\sqrt{d}]=1-o(1)\kern 5.0pt.

The above lemma is a consequence of
[
48
, Theorem 2]

Proof of
Proposition

8.1
.

Let
{\mathcal{E}}_{1}
be the event that
{\rho}(\UpB_{{\Upsigma}})\leq(1+o(1))\cdot\sqrt{d}
is true.

On the event
{\mathcal{E}}_{1}
,
we apply
Proposition

8.2
, with
\UpQ={\Upsigma}_{S}
,
\lambda=\sqrt{d}\cdot(1+o(1))
, and
L=\varepsilon\sqrt{d}
, yielding

\displaystyle{\mathds{1}}\{{\mathcal{P}}\}\cdot\|{\Upsigma}_{S}\|_{2}\leq\|{\Upsigma}_{S}\|_{2}

\displaystyle<\frac{\lambda}{1-\delta}+\frac{1-\delta}{\lambda-(1-\delta)\varepsilon\sqrt{d}}\cdot\left\|{{\Upsigma}_{S}}^{\circ 2}\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq(1+o(1))\sqrt{d}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{1-\delta}+\frac{(1-\delta)(1-\varepsilon)}{1-(1-\delta)\varepsilon}\right)\leq 2\sqrt{d}\cdot(1+o(1))\kern 5.0pt.

In the second inequality we use the fact that
\left\|{{\Upsigma}_{S}}^{\circ 2}\right\|_{\infty}\leq(1+\varepsilon)d
.

From the all above, we conclude that on the event
{\mathcal{E}}_{1}
we have
{\mathds{1}}\{{\mathcal{P}}\}\cdot\|{\Upsigma}_{S}\|_{2}\leq 2\sqrt{d}\cdot(1+o(1))
.

Proposition

8.1
follows from
Lemma

8.3
which implies that
\Pr[{\mathcal{E}}_{1}]=1-o(1)
.

∎

8.2.
Proof of
Proposition

8.2

Let us first introduce a few notions.
For a symmetric matrix
\UpQ\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
and

t\in\mathbb{R}
, we define the
n\times n
matrices
\UpA_{t}=\UpA(\UpQ,t)
and

\UpD_{t}=\UpD(\UpQ,t)
by

(8.6)

\displaystyle\UpA_{t}(i,j)=\frac{t\UpQ(i,j)}{1-\left(t\UpQ(i,j)\right)^{2}},

and

\displaystyle\UpD_{t}(i,j)={\mathds{1}}\{i=j\}\cdot\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{\left(t\UpQ(i,k)\right)^{2}}{1-\left(t\UpQ(i,k)\right)^{2}}\kern 5.0pt.

Note in particular that
\UpD_{t}
is a diagonal matrix. The following result is proven in
[
30
]
.

Theorem 8.4

(Generalised Ihara-Bass formula
[
30
]
)
.

Let
\UpQ\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be symmetric, and let
\UpB_{\UpQ}
, and
\UpA_{t},\UpD_{t}
be defined as in (
8.4
), and (
8.6
), respectively. Assume that
t
is such that

|t\UpQ(i,j)|\neq 1
for all
i,j
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\det\left(\UpI-t\UpB_{\UpQ}\right)=\det(\UpI+\UpD_{t}-\UpA_{t})\prod\nolimits_{1\leq i\leq j\leq n}\left(1-\left(t\UpQ(i,j)\right)^{2}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

The above theorem, yields the following inequality.

Lemma 8.5
.

For any symmetric
\UpQ\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, let
\lambda=\max\{{\rho}(\UpB_{\UpQ}),\max_{i,j}{|\UpQ(i,j)|}\}
, where
\UpB_{\UpQ}
is defined in (
8.4
). Then for every
t\in\left(-\lambda^{-1},\lambda\right)
, we have

(8.7)

\displaystyle\UpI+\UpD_{t}-\UpA_{t}\succ 0\kern 5.0pt,

where
\UpA_{t}=\UpA(\UpQ,t),\UpD_{t}=\UpD(\UpQ,t)
are defined in (
8.6
).

Proof of
Lemma

8.5
.

From
Theorem

8.4
, we have

(8.8)

\displaystyle\det\left(\UpI-t\UpB_{\UpQ}\right)=\det(\UpI+\UpD_{t}-\UpA_{t})\prod\nolimits_{1\leq i\leq j\leq n}\left(1-\left(t\UpQ(i,j)\right)^{2}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Choosing
|t|<\lambda^{-1}
has two consequences. Firstly, it implies that
|t\UpQ(i,j)|<1
for all
i,j
, and hence,
the product in the r.h.s. of (
8.8
) is strictly positive. Secondly, it implies that

|t\cdot{\rho}(\UpB_{\UpQ})|<1
, yielding
\det\left(\UpI-t\UpB_{\UpQ}\right)>0
. Therefore, have that

\det(\UpI+\UpD_{t}-\UpA_{t})>0
, for all
t\in\left(-\lambda^{-1},\lambda\right)
.

When
t=0
we observe that
\UpI+\UpD_{0}-\UpA_{0}=\UpI\succ 0
. Since the eigenvalues of
