\UpI+\UpD_{t}-\UpA_{t}
vary continuously with respect to
t
, we conclude that none of them changes sign
in
\left(-\lambda^{-1},\lambda\right)
, i.e.,
\UpI+\UpD_{t}-\UpA_{t}\succ 0
, as desired.
∎

In light of
Lemma

8.5
, we establish
Proposition

8.2
as follows.

Proof of
Proposition

8.2
.

The arguments that prove (
8.5
) for a principal submatrix of

\UpQ
, are similar to those we use for
\UpQ
. They rely on the observation
that for any
t\in(-\lambda^{-1},\lambda^{-1})
it is not only that
matrix
\UpM=\UpI+\UpD_{t}-\UpA_{t}\succ 0
, i.e., as shown in

Lemma

8.5
, but also any principal submatrix of
\UpM
is positive definite.
To avoid the tedious notations, in this proof, we focus on the case of
\UpQ
.

For
t\in\mathbb{R}
, consider the matrices
\UpA_{t}=\UpA(\UpQ,t)
and

\UpD_{t}=\UpD(\UpQ,t)
as defined in (
8.6
).
Also, let us define the matrix
\UpK_{t}=\UpK(\UpQ,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
with

\displaystyle\UpK_{t}(i,j)=\frac{\left(t\UpQ(i,j)\right)^{3}}{1-\left(t\UpQ(i,j)\right)^{2}}\kern 5.0pt.

Note that
\UpA_{t}=t\UpQ+\UpK_{t}
. Let
\theta=\frac{1-\delta}{\lambda}
. Applying

Lemma

8.5
on
\UpQ
with
t=\theta
, and rearranging gives

\displaystyle\UpQ\prec\theta^{-1}\cdot\left(\UpI+\UpD_{\theta}-\UpK_{\theta}\right)\kern 5.0pt,

so that

(8.9)

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max}(\UpQ)<\theta^{-1}\cdot\lambda_{\max}\left(\UpI+\UpD_{\theta}-\UpK_{\theta}\right)\leq\frac{1}{{\theta}}\cdot\left\|\UpI+\UpD_{\theta}-\UpK_{\theta}\right\|_{\infty}\kern 5.0pt.

Similarly, applying (
8.7
) with
t=-{\theta}
, and rearranging gives

\displaystyle\UpQ\succ-\theta^{-1}\cdot\left(\UpI+\UpD_{-\theta}-\UpK_{-\theta}\right)\kern 5.0pt,

so that

(8.10)

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min}(\UpQ)>-\theta^{-1}\cdot\lambda_{\max}\left(\UpI+\UpD_{-\theta}-\UpK_{-\theta}\right)\geq-\theta^{-1}\cdot\left\|\UpI+\UpD_{-{\theta}}-\UpK_{-{\theta}}\right\|_{\infty}\kern 5.0pt.

Noticing that
\UpD_{t}=\UpD_{-t}
, and
\UpK_{t}=-\UpK_{-t}
, for every
t
, and using the triangle inequality, we observe

\displaystyle\max\{\left\|\UpI+\UpD_{\theta}-\UpK_{\theta}\right\|_{\infty},\left\|\UpI+\UpD_{-\theta}-\UpK_{-\theta}\right\|_{\infty}\}\leq\left\|\,\UpI+\left|\UpD_{\theta}\right|+\left|\UpK_{\theta}\right|\,\right\|_{\infty}\kern 5.0pt,

where for matrix
\UpD
, we let
|\UpD|
denote the matrix having entries
|\UpD_{i,j}|
.
Eq. (
8.9
) and (
8.10
) imply

(8.11)

\displaystyle\|\UpQ\|_{2}={\rho}(\UpQ)

\displaystyle<\theta^{-1}\cdot\left\|\,\UpI+\left|\UpD_{\theta}\right|+\left|\UpK_{\theta}\right|\,\right\|_{\infty}\kern 5.0pt.

The first equality follows from the fact that
\UpQ
is symmetric.
The definitions of
\UpD_{\theta}
, and
\UpK_{\theta}
, imply

\displaystyle\left\|\,\UpI+\left|\UpD_{\theta}\right|+\left|\UpK_{\theta}\right|\,\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\max_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\left\{1+\left|\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{\left({\theta}\UpQ(i,k)\right)^{2}}{1-\left({\theta}\UpQ(i,k)\right)^{2}}\right|+\sum_{q=1}^{n}\left|\frac{\left({\theta}\UpQ(i,q)\right)^{3}}{1-\left({\theta}\UpQ(i,q)\right)^{2}}\right|\right\}

\displaystyle=1+\max_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\left\{\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{\left({\theta}\UpQ(i,k)\right)^{2}+\left|{\theta}\UpQ(i,k)\right|^{3}}{1-\left({\theta}\UpQ(i,k)\right)^{2}}\right\}

\displaystyle=1+\max_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\left\{\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{\left({\theta}\UpQ(i,k)\right)^{2}}{1-\left|{\theta}\UpQ(i,k)\right|}\right\}\kern 5.0pt.

Substituting
\theta=\frac{1-\delta}{\lambda}
into the r.h.s. above, and recalling that
\max_{i,j}|\UpQ(i,j)|\leq L
, we further get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\theta}\cdot\left\|\,\UpI+\left|\UpD_{\theta}\right|+\left|\UpK_{\theta}\right|\,\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\lambda}{1-\delta}\cdot\left(1+\frac{(1-\delta)^{2}}{\lambda^{2}-\lambda(1-\delta)L}\cdot\max_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\left\{\sum_{k=1}^{n}{\left(\UpQ(i,k)\right)^{2}}\right\}\right)

(8.12)

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda}{1-\delta}+\frac{1-\delta}{\lambda-(1-\delta)L}\cdot\left\|\UpQ^{\circ 2}\right\|_{\infty}\kern 5.0pt.

Combining (
8.11
) and (
8.12
), we get
(
8.5
).

Proposition

8.2
follows.
∎

9.
Proof of
Theorem

6.2

Let
\varepsilon>0
be as specified in the statement of
Theorem

6.2
.
As per standard notation, we consider the interaction matrix
\UpJ=\UpJ(\mathbold{G}(n,d/n),\{\mathbold{\gamma}_{e}\})
defined
as in (
6.1
). For
(\UpJ,\beta)
that admits an
\varepsilon
-block partition
{\mathcal{B}}

with partition matrices
(\UpJ_{S},\UpJ_{H})
, we define set

(9.1)

\displaystyle\partial H=\bigcup\nolimits_{B\in{\mathcal{B}}:|B|\neq 1}\partial_{\rm in}B\kern 5.0pt.

We also let
\mathrm{Cov}_{\partial H}(\UpJ_{H},\beta,\uph)
be the restriction of

\mathrm{Cov}(\UpJ_{H},\beta,\uph)
to set
{\partial H}
, i.e., we have

\displaystyle[\mathrm{Cov}_{\partial H}(\UpJ_{H},\beta,\uph)](u,v)

\displaystyle=[\mathrm{Cov}(\UpJ_{H},\beta,\uph)](u,v)\cdot{\mathds{1}}\left\{u,v\in\partial H\right\}

\displaystyle\forall u,v\in V_{n}\kern 5.0pt.

Theorem 9.1
.

For
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
satisfying
1-\varepsilon/8\geq\upkappa
, there is
d_{0}=d_{0}(\varepsilon)>0

such that for a sufficiently large constant
c>0
, for any
d\geq d_{0}
,
\beta\leq\beta_{c}(d)
and any
\uph\in\mathbb{R}^{V_{n}}

the following is true:

With probability
1-o(1)
over the instances of matrix
\UpJ=\UpJ(\mathbold{G}(n,d/n),\{\mathbold{\gamma}_{e}\})
defined as in (
6.1
),

(\UpJ,\beta)
admits an
\varepsilon
-block partition
{\mathcal{B}}
with partition matrices
(\UpJ_{S},\UpJ_{H})
such that

(9.2)

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{Cov}(\UpJ_{H},\beta,\uph)\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq c\cdot L\kern 5.0pt,

(9.3)

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{Cov}_{\partial H}(\UpJ_{H},\beta,\uph)\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c}{\varepsilon\cdot d^{1/10}}\kern 5.0pt,

where
L=\frac{\log n}{\sqrt{d}}
, while
\upkappa
is from the definition of
\beta_{c}
in (
1.4
).

Note that the quantification in
Theorem

9.1

implies that the constant
c>0
is independent of the expected degree
d
.
The proof of
Theorem

9.1
appears in
Section

10
.

To make use of
Theorem

9.1
and complete the proof of
Theorem

6.2
,
we also need the following results proved in
[
38
]
.

Lemma 9.2

(
[
38
]
)
.

Let
\UpM_{1}
and
\UpM_{2}
be
V_{n}\times V_{n}
matrices on
\mathbb{R}
, with
\UpM_{1}
being positive definite, while let
\uph\in\mathbb{R}^{V_{n}}
.
For
\beta>0
, suppose there exists positive definite matrix
\Upgamma
such that for any
z\in\mathbb{R}^{V_{n}}
, we have

\displaystyle(\beta\cdot\UpM_{1})-(\beta\cdot\UpM_{1})\cdot\mathrm{Cov}(\UpM_{2},\beta,\UpM_{1}\cdot z+\uph)\cdot(\beta\cdot\UpM_{1})\succeq\Upgamma\kern 5.0pt.

Then, we have
\mathrm{Cov}(\UpM_{1}+\UpM_{2},\beta,\uph)\preceq\Upgamma^{-1}
.

Lemma 9.3

(
[
38
]
)
.

Let
\UpK
and
\UpL
be
V_{n}\times V_{n}
symmetric matrices. Suppose there exist
\alpha,\eta_{1},\eta_{2}>0
such that

\|\UpK\|_{2}\leq\alpha
and

\eta_{1}\UpI\preceq\UpL\preceq\frac{\eta_{2}}{\alpha}\UpI
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\UpL-\UpL\cdot\UpK\cdot\UpL\succeq\eta_{1}(1-\eta_{2})\cdot\UpI\kern 5.0pt,

where
\UpI
is the
V_{n}\times V_{n}
identity matrix.

We use a slightly different notation from
[
38
]
. For this reason, the above results may look different
from what is stated in that paper.

Proof of
Theorem

6.2
.

Theorem

6.2
follows by using derivations which are similar to those in the proof of Theorem 4.6 in
[
38
]
.
We provide the basic calculations here.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be the event: for
\varepsilon>0
as specified in the statement of
Theorem

6.2
,
for any
\uph\in\mathbb{R}^{V_{n}}
and for constant
c>0
as specified in
Theorem

9.1
, we have

(1)

an
\varepsilon
-block partition
{\mathcal{B}}
with partition matrices
(\UpJ_{S},\UpJ_{H})
,

(2)

\|\UpJ_{S}\|_{2}\leq 2\sqrt{d}(1+o(1))
,

(3)

\|\mathrm{Cov}_{\partial H}(\UpJ_{H},\beta,\uph)\|_{2}\leq c\cdot\left(\varepsilon\cdot d^{1/10}\right)^{-1}
,

(4)

\|\mathrm{Cov}(\UpJ_{H},\beta,\uph)\|_{2}\leq c\cdot L
.

Recall that
L=\frac{\log n}{\sqrt{d}}
.
From
Theorems

4.3
,
9.1
and
6.1
,
we have
\Pr[\mathcal{G}]=1-o(1)
.

From now on, assume that event
\mathcal{G}
holds. We use
Lemma

9.2
for matrices

(9.4)

\displaystyle\UpM_{1}

\displaystyle=(1-t)\cdot\UpK+t\cdot\frac{\varepsilon}{200}\cdot\UpI_{S}+\frac{\upzeta}{L}\cdot\left(1-\textstyle{\frac{t}{\sqrt{d}}}\right)\cdot\UpI_{H\setminus\partial H}

and

\displaystyle\UpM_{2}

\displaystyle=\UpJ_{H}\kern 5.0pt,

where
\UpK=\UpJ_{S}+\left(1+{\textstyle\frac{\varepsilon}{100}}\right)\cdot\|\UpJ_{S}\|_{2}\cdot\UpI_{S}
.
We specify parameter
\upzeta
later in this proof.

W.l.o.g. we choose
t=0
to prove our result.

Theorem

6.2
follows by showing that on the event
\mathcal{G}
, for any
z\in\mathbb{R}^{V_{n}}
,
we have

(9.5)

\displaystyle\beta\cdot\UpM_{1}-\beta^{2}\cdot\UpM_{1}\cdot\mathrm{Cov}(\UpM_{2},\beta,\UpM_{1}\cdot z+\uph)\cdot\UpM_{1}

\displaystyle\succeq\Upgamma\kern 5.0pt,

where
\Upgamma\succeq\frac{1}{d^{1/15}}\cdot\UpI_{S}+\frac{\upzeta}{2L}\UpI_{H\setminus\partial H}
.
In this case, the constant
C
in the statement of
Theorem

6.2
satisfies
C=\frac{3}{\upzeta}
.

Fix any
z\in\mathbb{R}^{V_{n}}
.
When there is no danger of confusion, we abbreviate
\mathrm{Cov}(\UpM_{2},\beta,\UpM_{1}\cdot z+\uph)

to
\mathrm{Cov}
.
Furthermore, for any
\Lambda\subseteq V_{n}
, we let the
V_{n}\times V_{n}
matrix
\mathrm{Cov}_{\Lambda}
be
defined by

(9.6)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cov}_{\Lambda}(u,w)

\displaystyle={\mathds{1}}\{u,w\in\Lambda\}\cdot\mathrm{Cov}(u,w)

\displaystyle\forall u,w\in V_{n}\kern 5.0pt.

Standard calculations which can be found in
[
38
]
(proof of Theorem 4.6) imply that
for any
\delta^{\prime}>0
, we have

(9.7)

\displaystyle\beta\cdot\UpM_{1}-\beta^{2}\cdot\UpM_{1}\cdot\mathrm{Cov}\cdot\UpM_{1}

\displaystyle\succeq(\beta\cdot\UpK-(1+\delta^{\prime})\cdot\beta^{2}\cdot\UpK\cdot\mathrm{Cov}_{S}\cdot\UpK)+\frac{\upzeta}{L}\left(\UpI_{H\setminus\partial H}-\left(1+\frac{1}{\delta^{\prime}}\right)\frac{\upzeta}{L}\cdot\mathrm{Cov}_{H\setminus\partial H}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

From the definition of sets
S
,
H
and (
9.6
) it is not hard to verify that

\mathrm{Cov}_{S}=\mathrm{Cov}_{\partial H}
, since
H\cap S=\partial H
.
From
item

3
in the definition of event
\mathcal{G}
and for large
d>0
, we have

(9.8)

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{Cov}_{S}\|_{2}=\|\mathrm{Cov}_{\partial H}\|_{2}\leq d^{-1/12}\kern 5.0pt.

Our assumption
0<\beta\leq\beta_{c}
and the event
\mathcal{G}
imply that there are numbers
0<\eta,\theta<1

such that

(9.9)

\displaystyle\eta\cdot\UpI_{S}\preceq\beta\cdot\UpK\preceq\theta\cdot\UpI_{S}\kern 5.0pt.

Combining the above inequalities with
Lemma

9.3
, for any
\frac{1}{2}\leq\delta\leq 1
, we obtain

(9.10)

\displaystyle(\beta\cdot\UpK-(1+\delta^{\prime})\cdot\beta^{2}\cdot\UpK\cdot\mathrm{Cov}_{S}\cdot\UpK)\succeq d^{-1/15}\cdot\UpI_{S}\kern 5.0pt.

Similarly, using
item

4
of the definition of the event
\mathcal{G}
, for any
\uph^{\prime}
, we have

(9.11)

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{Cov}_{H\setminus\partial H}(\UpJ_{H},\beta,\uph^{\prime})\|_{2}\leq\|\mathrm{Cov}(\UpJ_{H},\beta,\uph^{\prime})\|_{2}\leq c\cdot L\kern 5.0pt.

For
\delta^{\prime}=\frac{1}{2}
and
\upzeta=\frac{1}{10c(1+\frac{1}{\delta^{\prime}})}
,
the above inequality implies

(9.12)

\displaystyle\UpI_{H\setminus\partial H}-\left(1+\frac{1}{\delta^{\prime}}\right)\frac{\upzeta}{L}\mathrm{Cov}_{H\setminus\partial H}\succeq\frac{1}{2}\UpI_{H\setminus\partial H}\kern 5.0pt.

Plugging (
9.10
) and (
9.12
) into (
9.7
), we get

\displaystyle\beta\cdot\UpM_{1}-\beta^{2}\cdot\UpM_{1}\cdot\mathrm{Cov}\cdot\UpM_{1}\succeq\frac{1}{d^{1/15}}\cdot\UpI_{S}+\frac{\upzeta}{2L}\UpI_{H\setminus\partial H}\kern 5.0pt.

The above proves (
9.5
) and concludes the proof of

Theorem

6.2
.
∎

10.
Covariance Matrix Bounds - Proof of
Theorem

9.1

10.1.
Weighted-Spheres

Consider
\mathbold{G}=\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
, and the set i.i.d standard normal random
variables
\{\mathbold{\gamma}_{e}\}_{e\in E(\mathbold{G})}
, while we let the
V_{n}\times V_{n}

interaction matrix
\UpJ
be such that

\displaystyle\UpJ(u,w)

\displaystyle={\mathds{1}}\{\{u,w\}\in E(\mathbold{G})\}\cdot\mathbold{\gamma}_{\{u,w\}}

\displaystyle\forall u,w\in V_{n}\kern 5.0pt.

For
\beta\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
\uph\in\mathbb{R}^{V_{n}}
, we let the Gibbs distribution
\mu_{\beta,\uph,\UpJ}

be such that

\displaystyle\mu_{\UpJ,\beta,\uph}(\sigma)

\displaystyle\propto\exp\left(\frac{\beta}{2}\cdot\langle\sigma,\UpJ\cdot\sigma\rangle+\langle\sigma,\uph\rangle\right),

\displaystyle\quad\forall\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{V_{n}}\kern 5.0pt.

For each path
P
in
\mathbold{G}
define the quantity

(10.1)

\displaystyle{\tt W}(P)=\prod\nolimits_{e\in P}{\rm\Upgamma}_{e}\kern 5.0pt,

where
{\rm\Upgamma}_{e}
is defined in (
4.3
).

For each vertex
v\in V_{n}
and each integer
\ell>0
, we let
{\tt SQR}(v,\ell)
be defined by

(10.2)

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}(v,\ell)=\sum\nolimits_{P}{\tt W}^{2}(P)\kern 5.0pt,

where variable
P
in the summation varies over all paths of length
\ell
emanating from
v
.

Theorem 10.1
.

For any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
satisfying
1-\varepsilon/8\geq\upkappa
, there exists
d_{0}=d_{0}(\varepsilon)>0
, such that for any

d\geq d_{0}
,
\beta\leq\beta_{c}(d)
, and
0\leq\ell\leq\frac{\log n}{(\log d)^{2}}
the following is true:

With probability
1-o(1)
over the instances of matrix
\UpJ=\UpJ(\mathbold{G}(n,d/n),\{\mathbold{\gamma}_{e}\})
defined as in (
6.1
),
there exists an
\varepsilon
-block partition
{\mathcal{B}}
such that

(1)

for every multi-vertex block
B\in{\mathcal{B}}
, for any
v\in V(B)
we have

{\tt SQR}(v,\ell)\leq 3\cdot L\cdot\upkappa^{\ell}
,

(2)

for every
\varepsilon
-block vertex
v

we have
{\tt SQR}(v,\ell)\leq(1-\varepsilon/8)^{\ell}
.

The quantity
\upkappa
is from (
1.4
), while
L=\frac{\log n}{\sqrt{d}}
.

The proof of
Theorem

10.1
appears
Section

11
.

For
\varepsilon>0
, suppose that
(\UpJ,\beta)
admits the
\varepsilon
-block partition
{\mathcal{B}}
.
For each multivertex block
B\in{\mathcal{B}}
and vertex
\alpha\in\partial_{\rm in}B
, let the all paths
tree
T=\mathcal{T}_{\rm AP}(B,\alpha)
. Consider the 2-spin model on
T
with inverse temperature
\beta
such
that the coupling at each edge
e=\{\overline{x},\overline{z}\}
is equal to

\mathbold{\gamma}_{\{x,z\}}
, where
\overline{x},\overline{z}
are
copies of
x,z\in B
, respectively.

For the above spin model on
T
, vertex
w\in V(T)
and integer
\ell>0
,
consider the quantity
{\tt SQR}_{T}(w,\ell)
as in (
10.2
), in the natural way.
Furthermore, we let
{\tt SQR}_{\partial T}(w,\ell)
be such that

(10.3)

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}_{\partial T}(w,\ell)=\sum\nolimits_{P}{\tt W}^{2}(P)\kern 5.0pt,

where
P
varies over all paths of length
\ell
from
w
to the set of copies of
\partial_{\rm in}B
in
T
.

In the following result, we use
Theorem

10.1
to obtain bounds on
{\tt SQR}_{T}(v,\ell)
and
{\tt SQR}_{\partial T}(w,\ell)
.

Proposition 10.2
.

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
satisfying
1-\varepsilon/8\geq\upkappa
,
let sufficiently large
d_{0}=d_{0}(\varepsilon)>0
, let
d\geq d_{0}
and

\beta\leq\beta_{c}(d)
also let integer
0\leq\ell\leq\frac{\log n}{(\log d)^{2}}
.

With probability
1-o(1)
over the instances of matrix
\UpJ=\UpJ(\mathbold{G}(n,d/n),\{\mathbold{\gamma}_{e}\})
defined as in (
6.1
), there exists an
\varepsilon
-block partition
{\mathcal{B}}
such that for every multi-vertex block
B\in{\mathcal{B}}
, for
\alpha\in\partial_{\rm in}B
and
T=\mathcal{T}_{\rm AP}(B,\alpha)
the following is true:
for any
w\in V(T)
, we have

(1)

{\tt SQR}_{T}(w,\ell)\leq 10\cdot L\cdot{\upkappa}^{\ell}

(2)

if additionally
w
is a copy of a vertex in
\partial_{\rm in}B
, we have

{\tt SQR}_{T}(w,\ell)\leq\frac{10}{d^{1/10}}\cdot{(1-\varepsilon/8)}^{\ell}

(3)

{\tt SQR}_{\partial T}(w,\ell)\leq\frac{2}{d^{1/10}}\cdot(1-\varepsilon/16)^{\ell-1}
.

The quantity
\upkappa
is from (
1.4
),
{\tt SQR}_{\partial T}(w,\ell)

is defined in (
10.3
),
while
L=\frac{\log n}{\sqrt{d}}
.

The proof of
Proposition

10.2
appears in
Section

12
.

10.2.
Proof of eq.(
9.2
) in
Theorem

9.1

Let
\mathcal{G}
be the event that there is an
\varepsilon
-block partition
{\mathcal{B}}

of the set of vertices
V_{n}
with partition matrices
\UpJ_{H}
and
\UpJ_{S}
.

Claim 10.3
.

On the event
\mathcal{G}
, for any multi-vertex block
\hat{B}\in{\mathcal{B}}
we have

(10.4)

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{Cov}(\UpJ_{\hat{B}},\beta,\uph)\|_{2}\leq\|\mathrm{Cov}(|\UpJ_{\hat{B}}|,\beta,0)\|_{2}\kern 5.0pt.

Recall that for a matrix
\UpD\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
, we let

|\UpD|
denote the matrix having entries
|\UpD_{i,j}|
.

Proof.

For brevity, let
\mu^{(1)}
be the Gibbs distribution on
\{\pm 1\}^{V_{n}}
, induced
by the interaction matrix
\UpJ_{\hat{B}}
, inverse temperature
\beta
and
the external field
\uph
. Let
\mathrm{Cov}_{1}
and
\mathcal{I}_{1}
be the covariance
and the influence matrices of
\mu^{(1)}
, respectively.

Similarly, define
\mu^{(2)}
to be the distribution induced by

(|\UpJ_{\hat{B}}|,\beta,\uph)
, with covariance and influence matrices

\mathrm{Cov}_{2}
and
\mathcal{I}_{2}
, respectively.

Also, define
\mu^{(3)}
to be the distribution induced by

(|\UpJ_{\hat{B}}|,\beta,0)
, with covariance and influence matrices

\mathrm{Cov}_{3}
and
\mathcal{I}_{3}
, respectively.

Both
\mu^{(2)}
and
\mu^{(3)}
correspond to (non homogeneous)
ferromagnetic Ising models.
The difference in the two distributions is that in
\mu^{(2)}
there
is an external field, whereas in
\mu^{(3)}
there is not.

Equation

10.4
follows by showing that for any two vertices
w,v\in V_{n}
, we have

(10.5)

0\leq|\mathrm{Cov}_{1}(w,v)|\leq\mathrm{Cov}_{3}(w,v)\kern 5.0pt.

From
Proposition

7.2
and
Lemma

7.3
, we have

(10.6)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{i}(w,v)

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{P}\prod\nolimits_{e\in P}\upphi_{i}(e)\kern 5.0pt,

where the variable
P
runs over the paths in the tree of self-avoiding walks

{T_{\rm SAW}}(\hat{B},w)
from the root to the copies of vertex
v
in the tree.
Also, function
\upphi_{i}(e)
is from (
7.1
),
defined now with respect to the distribution
\mu_{i}
.

For
i\in\{2,3\}
, it is elementary to verify that

(10.7)

\displaystyle\upphi_{i}(e)

\displaystyle\geq 0\kern 5.0pt.

Clearly,
\upphi_{1}(e)
is not always non-negative.
To see the above consider the properties of function
h_{e}
(
7.3
) as the sign of
\UpJ_{e}
changes.

For any path
P
in

{T_{\rm SAW}}(\hat{B},w)
from the root to a copy of vertex
v
in the tree, we have

(10.8)

\displaystyle\prod\nolimits_{e\in P}|\upphi_{1}(e)|

\displaystyle=\prod\nolimits_{e\in P}\upphi_{2}(e)\kern 5.0pt.

Then, we obtain

(10.9)

\displaystyle|\mathcal{I}_{1}(w,v)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum\nolimits_{P}\prod\nolimits_{e\in P}|\upphi_{1}(e)|\ =\ \prod\nolimits_{e\in P}\upphi_{2}(e)=\mathcal{I}_{2}(w,v)

The first inequality above is from (
10.6
) and the triangle inequality.
The equality in the middle is from (
10.8
), while the final
equality is from (
10.6
).

Furthermore, it is standard to show, e.g., see
[
19
]
, that

(10.10)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{2}(w,v)\leq\mathcal{I}_{3}(w,v)\kern 5.0pt.

The two above inequalities imply that for any
v,w\in V_{n}
, we have

(10.11)

\displaystyle|\mathcal{I}_{1}(w,v)|

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{I}_{3}(w,v)\kern 5.0pt.

For any
i\in\{1,2,3\}
and any two vertices
w,v\in V_{n}
,
we have

(10.12)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cov}_{i}(w,v)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{I}_{i}(w,v)\cdot\mu^{(i)}_{w}(+1)\cdot(1-\mu^{(i)}_{w}(+1))\kern 5.0pt.

The above is standard and can be obtained from the definition of
\mathrm{Cov}_{i}
and
\mathcal{I}_{i}
.
We then have

\displaystyle|\mathrm{Cov}_{1}(w,v)|

\displaystyle=|\mathcal{I}_{1}(w,v)|\cdot\mu^{(1)}_{w}(+1)\cdot(1-\mu^{(1)}_{w}(+1))

[from (
10.12
)]

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{I}_{3}(w,v)\cdot\mu^{(1)}_{w}(+1)\cdot(1-\mu^{(1)}_{w}(+1))

[from (
10.11
)]

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{Cov}_{3}(w,v)\cdot 4\cdot\mu^{(1)}_{w}(+1)\cdot(1-\mu^{(1)}_{w}(+1))

[from (
10.12
)]

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{Cov}_{3}(w,v)\kern 5.0pt.

For the third line we use the observation that
\mu^{(3)}_{w}(+1)=1/2
. For the
last inequality we use the observation that

0\leq\mu^{(1)}_{w}(+1)\cdot(1-\mu^{(1)}_{w}(+1))\leq 1/4
.

The above implies (
10.5
) and concludes
the proof of
10.3
.
∎

Assume from now on that the event
\mathcal{G}
holds.

\UpJ_{H}
is a block matrix, and therefore we have

(10.13)

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{Cov}(\UpJ_{H},\beta,\uph)\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\max\nolimits_{\hat{B}}\|\mathrm{Cov}(\UpJ_{\hat{B}},\beta,\uph)\|_{2}\leq\max\nolimits_{\hat{B}}\|\mathrm{Cov}(|\UpJ_{\hat{B}}|,\beta,0)\|_{2}\kern 5.0pt,

where
\hat{B}
varies over all blocks in
{\mathcal{B}}
with more than one vertex.
The last inequality follows from
10.3
.

Let block
B
maximise the r.h.s. of (
10.13
).
Recall that
B
is a tree with at most one extra edge.

For a vertex
w\in\partial_{\rm in}B
, let the all paths tree
T=\mathcal{T}_{\rm AP}(B,w)
(see definition in
Section

7.2
).
Consider the
V_{n}\times V(T)
matrix
\UpK
such that
for any
x\in V_{n}
and any
z\in V(T)
, we have

(10.14)

\displaystyle\UpK(x,z)

\displaystyle={\mathds{1}}\{\textrm{$z$ is a copy of $x$ in $\mathcal{T}_{\rm AP}(B,w)$}\}\kern 5.0pt.

Consider also the
V(T)\times V(T)
matrix
\UpY
, such that for any
x,z\in V(T)
, we have

(10.15)

\displaystyle\UpY(x,z)

\displaystyle=\prod\nolimits_{e\in P}{\rm\Upgamma}_{e}\kern 5.0pt,

where
P
is the unique self-avoiding walk from vertex
x
to vertex
z
in
T
, while
for each edge
e=\{\overline{x},\overline{z}\}
in
P
such that
\overline{x}
is a copy of
x
and
\overline{z}
is a copy of

z
in
B
, we let

(10.16)

\displaystyle{\rm\Upgamma}_{e}

\displaystyle=|\tanh(\beta\cdot\UpJ_{\{x,z\}})|\kern 5.0pt.

Furthermore, let the
V_{n}\times V_{n}
matrix
\UpR
be such that

(10.17)

\displaystyle\UpR

\displaystyle=\UpK\cdot\UpY\cdot\overline{\UpK}\kern 5.0pt,

where
\overline{\UpK}
is the matrix transpose of
\UpK
.

Proposition

7.2
,
Lemma

7.4
and the definition of
{\rm\Upgamma}_{e}
imply
the entry-wise inequality

\UpR\geq\mathrm{Cov}(|\UpJ_{B}|,\beta,0).

Then, since the entries of
\mathrm{Cov}(|\UpJ_{B}|,\beta,0)
are non-negative, it standard

(10.18)

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{Cov}(|\UpJ_{B}|,\beta,0)\|_{2}\leq\|\UpR\|_{2}\kern 5.0pt.

Equation

9.2
follows by showing

(10.19)

\displaystyle\|\UpR\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq 40\cdot\left(1-\upkappa^{1/4}\right)^{-2}\cdot L\kern 5.0pt,

and setting
c=\frac{40}{\left(1-\upkappa^{1/4}\right)^{2}}
. To this end, we use the following claim.

Claim 10.4
.

We have
\|\UpR\|_{2}\leq 4\|\UpY\|_{2}
.

Proof of
10.4
.

From the definition of
\UpR
, we have

(10.20)

\displaystyle\|\UpR\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\|\UpK\cdot\UpY\cdot\overline{\UpK}\|_{2}\leq\|\UpK\|_{2}\cdot\|\UpY\|_{2}\cdot\|\overline{\UpK}\|_{2}\kern 5.0pt.

Furthermore, for any
x,z\in V_{n}
, we have

\displaystyle(\UpK\cdot\overline{\UpK})(x,z)

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{y\in V(T)}\UpK(x,y)\cdot\UpK(z,y)

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{y\in V(T)}{\mathds{1}}\{\textrm{$y$ is a copy of $x$}\}\cdot{\mathds{1}}\{\textrm{$y$ is a copy of $z$}\}

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{y\in V(T)}{\mathds{1}}\{\textrm{$y$ is a copy of $x$}\}\cdot{\mathds{1}}\{z=x\}\kern 5.0pt.

The second equality is from the definition of matrix
\UpK
, while the third one is from the fact that vertex
y\in V(T)
can be a copy of a single vertex
z\in V_{n}
.
From the above, we conclude that
\UpK\cdot\overline{\UpK}
is a
V_{n}\times V_{n}
diagonal matrix such that the entry
(\UpK\cdot\overline{\UpK})(v,v)
is equal to the number of copies of vertex
v
in
\mathcal{T}_{\rm AP}(B,w)
.

From the definition of
\mathcal{T}_{\rm AP}
, and in particular
Corollary

7.5
, the maximum entry in the diagonal matrix
(\UpK\cdot\overline{\UpK})
is
4
. This implies that
\|\UpK\cdot\overline{\UpK}\|_{2}\leq 4
.
We conclude that
\|\UpK\|_{2}=\|\overline{\UpK}\|_{2}=\|\UpK\cdot\overline{\UpK}\|_{2}^{1/2}\leq 2
.

Then, the claim follows from (
10.20
).
∎

For what follows, recall
T=\mathcal{T}_{\rm AP}(B,w)
. For integer
\ell\geq 0
, define the
V(T)\times V(T)
matrix
\UpY_{\ell}
by

(10.21)

\displaystyle\UpY_{\ell}(z,x)

\displaystyle={\mathds{1}}\{{\rm dist}(z,x)=\ell\}\cdot\UpY(z,x)\kern 5.0pt,

where the distance is on tree
T
.
Note that matrices
\UpY
and
\UpY_{\ell}
, for all
\ell\geq 0
, are symmetric.

The triangle inequality implies

(10.22)

\displaystyle\|\UpY\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq 0}\|\UpY_{\ell}\|_{2}\kern 5.0pt.

We proceed to bound
\|\UpY_{\ell}\|_{2}
for each
\ell>0
.

For a parameter
\delta>0
which we specify later, we define the weight function
\upchi:V(T)\to[0,+\infty)
for each vertex of
T=\mathcal{T}_{\rm AP}(B,w)
as follows:

For the root
r
of
T
, we define
\upchi(r)=1
. For every vertex
z
with
\upchi(z)
being determined, we define
\upchi(u)=\upchi(z)(1+\delta)\cdot{\rm\Upgamma}_{\{z,u\}}
for all children
u
of
z
.

A useful observation is that for every
u\in V(T)
we have

(10.23)

\displaystyle\upchi(u)

\displaystyle=(1+\delta)^{{\rm dist}(r,u)}\cdot\UpY(r,u)\kern 5.0pt.

We also define
{\UpD}
to be the diagonal
V(T)\times V(T)
matrix such that

(10.24)

\displaystyle{\UpD}(u,u)

\displaystyle=\upchi(u),

\displaystyle\forall u\in V(T)\kern 5.0pt.

Since
\UpY_{\ell}
is symmetric, we have

(10.25)

\displaystyle\|\UpY_{\ell}\|_{2}\leq\ \|{\UpD}^{-1}\cdot\UpY_{\ell}\cdot{\UpD}\|_{\infty}=\max_{w}\left\{\sum\nolimits_{u}\left(\upchi(w)\right)^{-1}\cdot\UpY_{\ell}(w,u)\cdot\upchi(u)\right\}\kern 5.0pt,

where the last equality follows from the definition of the norm and
the observation that all entries of
\UpY_{\ell}
are nonnegative.

For every
w\in V(T)
, which is at distance
q
from the root
r
of
T
, we show

(10.26)

\displaystyle\sum\nolimits_{u}\left(\upchi(w)\right)^{-1}\cdot\UpY_{\ell}(w,u)\cdot\upchi(u)

\displaystyle\leq 10\cdot L\cdot\sum\nolimits_{0\leq i\leq q}(1+\delta)^{\ell-2i}\cdot\upkappa^{\ell-i}\kern 5.0pt.

Before proving that (
10.26
) is true, let us see how it implies (
10.19
).

We choose
\delta
such that it satisfies both
(1+\delta)\upkappa<1

and
(1+\delta)^{2}\upkappa>1
. Specifically, we set
\delta=\upkappa^{-3/4}-1
.
Let
w_{0}\in V(T)
be the vertex attaining the maximum value of the rhs in (
10.26
), and let
q_{0}
be the distance of
w_{0}
from the root of
T
. For our choice of
\delta
, (
10.26
) implies

(10.27)

\displaystyle\|{\UpD}^{-1}\cdot\UpY_{\ell}\cdot{\UpD}\|_{\infty}\leq 10\cdot L\cdot(1+\delta)^{\ell}\cdot\upkappa^{\ell}\cdot\sum\nolimits_{0\leq i\leq{q_{0}}}(1+\delta)^{-2i}\cdot\upkappa^{-i}\leq 10\cdot L\cdot\frac{\upkappa^{\ell/4}}{1-\sqrt{\upkappa}}\kern 5.0pt.

Plunging the above into (
10.22
) yields

(10.28)

\displaystyle\|\UpY\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq L\cdot\frac{10}{1-\sqrt{\upkappa}}\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq 0}\upkappa^{\ell/4}\leq L\cdot\frac{10}{\left(1-\upkappa^{1/4}\right)^{2}}\kern 5.0pt.

Equation

10.19
follows from (
10.28
)
and
10.4
.
It remains to show that (
10.26
) is true.

Fix vertex
z\in V(T)
and let
(z_{0}=z,z_{1},\ldots,z_{q-1},z_{q}=r)
be the path from
z
to
r
. Recall that
q={\rm dist}(z,r)
.

For
0\leq i\leq q
, let
T_{i}
be the subtree of
T
rooted at
z_{i}
and
also containing its progeny. For
\ell>q
, we have

\displaystyle\sum\nolimits_{u}\left(\upchi(z)\right)^{-1}\cdot\UpY_{\ell}(z,u)\cdot\upchi(u)

\displaystyle\leq\sum\nolimits_{0\leq i\leq q}\left(\upchi(z)\right)^{-1}\cdot\prod\nolimits_{0<j\leq i}{\rm\Upgamma}_{\{z_{j},z_{j-1}\}}\sum\nolimits_{u\in V(T_{i})}\UpY_{\ell-i}(z_{i},u)\cdot\upchi(u)

(10.29)

\displaystyle\leq\sum\nolimits_{0\leq i\leq q}\left(\upchi(z)\right)^{-1}\cdot(1+\delta)^{-i}\sum\nolimits_{u\in V(T_{i})}\UpY_{\ell-i}(z_{i},u)\cdot\upchi(u)\kern 5.0pt,

where (
10.29
) follows from the definition of

\upchi(z)
and
\upchi(z_{i})
. Specifically, due to
(
10.23
), we have

\displaystyle\upchi(z_{i})=\left((1+\delta)^{i}\cdot\prod\nolimits_{0<j\leq i}{\rm\Upgamma}_{\{z_{j},z_{j-1}\}}\right)\cdot\upchi(z)\kern 5.0pt.

Note that, for
u
in
V(T_{i})
with which vertex
z_{i}
is connected through the path
P
, we have

\displaystyle\upchi(u)

\displaystyle=\left((1+\delta)^{|P|}\cdot\prod\nolimits_{e\in P}{\rm\Upgamma}_{e}\right)\cdot\upchi(z_{i})=(1+\delta)^{|P|}\cdot\UpY_{\ell-|P|}(z_{i},u)\cdot\upchi(z_{i})\kern 5.0pt.

Plugging the above into (
10.29
), we obtain

\displaystyle\sum\nolimits_{u}\left(\upchi(z)\right)^{-1}\cdot\UpY_{\ell}(z,u)\cdot\upchi(u)

\displaystyle\leq\sum\nolimits_{0\leq i\leq q}(1+\delta)^{\ell-2i}\sum\nolimits_{u\in V(T_{i})}\left(\UpY_{\ell-i}(z_{i},u)\right)^{2}

(10.30)

\displaystyle\leq\sum\nolimits_{0\leq i\leq q}(1+\delta)^{\ell-2i}\cdot{\tt SQR}_{T}(z_{i},\ell-i)

(10.31)

\displaystyle\leq 10\cdot L\cdot\sum\nolimits_{0\leq i\leq q}(1+\delta)^{\ell-2i}\cdot\upkappa^{\ell-i}\kern 5.0pt.

where (
10.30
) follows from the definition of
{\tt SQR}_{T}(z_{i},\ell-i)
,
while (
10.31
) follows from part (a) of
Proposition

10.2
.
The above implies that (
10.26
) is true for
\ell>q
.

With a similar line of arguments, for
\ell\leq q
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum\nolimits_{u}\frac{1}{\upchi(z)}\cdot\UpY_{\ell}(z,u)\cdot\upchi(u)

\displaystyle\leq 10\cdot L\cdot\sum\nolimits_{0\leq i\leq\ell-1}(1+\delta)^{\ell-2i}\cdot\upkappa^{\ell-i}\kern 5.0pt,

which proves (
10.26
).

All the above conclude the proof of eq. (
9.2
) of
Theorem

9.1
.

10.3.
Proof of eq.(
9.3
) in
Theorem

9.1

In this proof, we use some basic steps which are similar to those we use for the proof of (
9.2
).

We note that
\UpJ_{\partial H}
is a block matrix. Then, arguing as in

10.3
, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{Cov}_{\partial H}(\UpJ_{H},\beta,\uph)\|_{2}\leq\max\nolimits_{\hat{B}}\|\mathrm{Cov}_{\partial\hat{B}}(|\UpJ_{\partial_{\rm in}\hat{B}}|,\beta,0)\|_{2}\kern 5.0pt,

where
\hat{B}
varies over all blocks in
{\mathcal{B}}
with more than one vertex.

Let block
B
maximise the r.h.s. of (
10.13
).
Recall that
B
is a tree with at most one extra edge, while
each vertex of
\partial_{\rm in}B
is an
\varepsilon
-block vertex.

For vertex
w\in\partial_{\rm in}B
, let the all paths tree
T=\mathcal{T}_{\rm AP}(B,w)
(see definition in

Section

7.2
).

Consider the
V(T)\times V(T)
matrices
\UpY
and
\tilde{\UpY}
.
\UpY
is the matrix defined in (
10.15
).
Matrix
\tilde{\UpY}
is such that for any
x,z\in V(T)
, we have

\displaystyle\tilde{\UpY}(x,z)

\displaystyle={\mathds{1}}\{x,z\in\partial_{\rm in}B\}\cdot\UpY(x,z)\kern 5.0pt.

Furthermore, we define the
V_{n}\times V_{n}
matrix
\tilde{\UpR}
be such that

\displaystyle\tilde{\UpR}

\displaystyle=\UpK\cdot\tilde{\UpY}\cdot\overline{\UpK}\kern 5.0pt,

where matrix
\UpK
is defined in (
10.14
), and
\overline{\UpK}
is the transpose of
\UpK
.
Arguing as in (
10.18
), we have

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{Cov}_{\partial B}(|\UpJ_{\partial_{\rm in}B}|,\beta,0)\|_{2}\leq\|\tilde{\UpR}\|_{2}\kern 5.0pt.

We have the following claim which is obtained by using exactly the same steps as in
10.4
.

Claim 10.5
.

We have
\|\tilde{\UpR}\|_{2}\leq 4\|\tilde{\UpY}\|_{2}
.

For integer
\ell\geq 0
, let the
V(T)\times V(T)
matrix
\tilde{\UpY}_{\ell}
be such that

\displaystyle\tilde{\UpY}_{\ell}(z,x)

\displaystyle={\mathds{1}}\{{\rm dist}(z,x)=\ell\}\cdot\tilde{\UpY}(z,x)\kern 5.0pt.

Note that matrices
\tilde{\UpY}
and
\tilde{\UpY}_{\ell}
, for all
\ell\geq 0
, are symmetric.
The triangle inequality implies

(10.32)

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\UpY}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq 0}\|\tilde{\UpY}_{\ell}\|_{2}\kern 5.0pt.

We proceed to bound
\|\tilde{\UpY}_{\ell}\|_{2}
for each
\ell>0
.

For a parameter
\delta>0
which we specify later, we define the same weight function
\upchi:V(T)\to[0,+\infty)

on the vertices of
T=\mathcal{T}_{\rm AP}(B,w)
as in the proof of (
9.2
), in this proposition.

Recall that for each vertex
u\in V(T)
, we have

\displaystyle\upchi(u)

\displaystyle=(1+\delta)^{{\rm dist}(r,u)}\cdot\tilde{\UpY}(r,u)\kern 5.0pt,

while
r
is the root of
T
.

We also define the
V(T)\times V(T)
diagonal matrix
\tilde{\UpD}

such that
\tilde{\UpD}(u,u)=\upchi(u)
for all
u\in V(T)
.
Since
\tilde{\UpY}_{\ell}
is symmetric with non-negative entries, we have

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\UpY}_{\ell}\|_{2}\leq\|\tilde{\UpD}^{-1}\cdot\tilde{\UpY}_{\ell}\cdot\tilde{\UpD}\|_{\infty}\leq\ \max\nolimits_{w}\left\{\sum\nolimits_{u}\left(\upchi(w)\right)^{-1}\cdot\tilde{\UpY}_{\ell}(w,u)\cdot\upchi(u)\right\}\kern 5.0pt.

For every
w\in V(T)
, which is at distance
q
from the root of
T
, we have

(10.33)

\displaystyle\sum\nolimits_{u}\left(\upchi(w)\right)^{-1}\cdot\tilde{\UpY}_{\ell}(w,u)\cdot\upchi(u)

\displaystyle\leq{2}\cdot{d^{-1/10}}\cdot\sum\nolimits_{0\leq i\leq q}(1+\delta)^{\ell-2i}\cdot(1-\varepsilon/16)^{\ell-1-i}\kern 5.0pt.

We omit the calculation that establish the above inequality since they are almost identical to
those we have for (
10.29
) and (
10.31
).

In light of (
10.33
), we get (
9.3
) as follows:
We choose
\delta
such that it satisfies both
(1+\delta)\cdot(1-\varepsilon/16)<1

and
(1+\delta)^{2}(1-\varepsilon/16)>1
. Specifically, we set
\delta=(1-\varepsilon/16)^{-3/4}-1
.
For such
\delta
, (
10.33
) and standard derivations which are similar to
those we have for (
10.27
), we have

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\UpD}^{-1}\cdot\tilde{\UpY}_{\ell}\cdot\tilde{\UpD}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq K\cdot(1-\varepsilon/16)^{\ell/4}\kern 5.0pt.

where
K^{-1}=\frac{1}{2}\cdot d^{1/10}\cdot(1-\varepsilon/16)\cdot(1-\sqrt{1-\varepsilon/16})
.

Plunging the above into (
10.32
) and using standard derivations, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\UpY}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq K\cdot\sum\nolimits_{\ell\geq 0}(1-\varepsilon/16)^{\ell/4}\leq d^{-1/9}\kern 5.0pt,

as desired.
All the above conclude the proof of eq.(
9.3
).

\Box

11.
Proof of
Theorem

10.1

Recall that for
r\geq 1
, and a vertex
v
, we define

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}(v,r)

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{P}\prod\nolimits_{e\in P}|\tanh(\beta\cdot\mathbold{\gamma}_{e})|^{2}\kern 5.74997pt,

where the sum is over all paths
P
of length
r
emanating from the vertex
v
.

In the following, we write
{\tt SQR}_{G}
to indicate that

{\tt SQR}
is considered with respect to the underlying graph
G
.

We now consider the 2-spin model on a Galton-Watson tree that mimics the local structure
of the graph
\mathbold{G}
.
Specifically, for
d>0
, we let
\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{T}(d)
be a Galton-Watson tree rooted at vertex
v
, with offspring distribution
\mathtt{Poisson}(d)
.
For integer
\ell\geq 1
, we write
\mathbf{T}_{\ell}
for the tree obtained from
\mathbf{T}
by deleting all vertices at distance
>\ell
.
We also consider a set of i.i.d. standard Gaussians
\{\mathbold{\gamma}_{e}:e\in E(\mathbf{T}_{\ell})\}
and define the

V(\mathbf{T}_{\ell})\times V(\mathbf{T}_{\ell})
interaction matrix by

(11.1)

\displaystyle\UpJ_{\ell}(u,w)=\begin{cases}\mathbold{\gamma}_{\{u,w\}}&\text{if }u\sim w\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\kern 5.0pt.

The 2-spin model on
\mathbf{T}_{\ell}
at inverse temperature
\beta>0
and external field
\uph\in\mathbb{R}^{V(\mathbf{T}_{\ell})}
is defined
similarly to (
1.2
). To simplify our notation below, we write
{\tt SQR}_{\mathbf{T}}(v,\ell)={\tt SQR}_{\mathbf{T}_{\ell}}(v,\ell)
.

Lemma 11.1
.

For
d>0
, let
r\geq 1
and let
\mathbf{T}_{r}
be a Galton-Watson tree with offspring distribution

\mathtt{Poisson}(d)
, rooted at a vertex
v
, and consider the 2-spin model on
\mathbf{T}_{r}
at inverse temperature
\beta\leq\beta_{c}(d)
.
For any
C>100
, and for any
t\in[0,\frac{d}{2\upkappa})
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[{\tt SQR}_{\mathbf{T}}(v,r)>C\cdot\upkappa^{r}\right]\leq\exp\left((1-C)\cdot t\right)\kern 5.0pt,

where
\upkappa
is from (
1.4
).

Proof of
Lemma

11.1
.

Let us write
{\tt SQR}(r)={\tt SQR}_{\mathbf{T}}(v,r)
.
For
t\in\left[0,\frac{d}{2\upkappa}\right)
we further define

(11.2)

\displaystyle\Psi(r)=\frac{{\tt SQR}(r)}{{\upkappa}^{r}},

and

\displaystyle g_{r}(t)=\mathbb{E}[\exp\left(t\cdot\Psi(r)\right)]\kern 5.0pt.

Let
f=f_{d}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be the function
f(x)=e^{d\cdot(x-1)}
.
Notice that the moment generating function (m.g.f.) of the
\mathtt{Poisson}(d)
distribution is given by

M(t)=f(e^{t})
. For
\mathbold{\gamma}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
, we define

g_{0}(t)=\mathbb{E}[\exp(t\cdot|\tanh(\beta\cdot\mathbold{\gamma})|^{2})]\kern 5.74997pt.

By induction on
r\geq 1
, we prove that for any
t\in\left[0,\frac{d}{2\upkappa}\right)

we have

(11.3)

\displaystyle g_{r}(t)

\displaystyle\leq e^{t}\kern 5.0pt.

To establish the base case, i.e., to prove (
11.3
) for
r=1
, let

v_{1},\ldots,v_{{\mathbold{K}}}
be the children of the root
v
, with corresponding couplings

\mathbold{\gamma}_{1},\ldots,\mathbold{\gamma}_{{\mathbold{K}}}
, where
{\mathbold{K}}\sim\mathtt{Poisson}(d)
. We have

\displaystyle g_{1}(t)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\left({t}/{\upkappa}\right)\cdot\sum\nolimits_{0\leq i\leq{\mathbold{K}}}|\tanh(\beta\cdot\mathbold{\gamma}_{i})|^{2}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbold{K}}}\left[\prod\nolimits_{0\leq i\leq{\mathbold{K}}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbold{\gamma}_{i}}\left[\exp\left(\left({t}/{\upkappa}\right)\cdot|\tanh(\beta\cdot\mathbold{\gamma}_{i})|^{2}\right)\right]\right]

(11.4)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbold{K}}}\left[\left(g_{0}\left({t}/{\upkappa}\right)\right)^{{\mathbold{K}}}\right]=f\left(g_{0}\left({t}/{\upkappa}\right)\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Using the mgf for the chi-square distribution for argument
<1/2
, we bound
g_{0}(t/\upkappa)
as follows:

(11.5)

\displaystyle g_{0}\left({t}/{\upkappa}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-2\cdot{\beta^{2}\cdot t}/{\upkappa}\right)^{-1/2}\leq\left(1-2\cdot{t}/{d}\right)^{-1/2}\leq 1+{t}/{d}\kern 5.0pt.

Combining (
11
) and (
11.5
) gives

(11.6)

\displaystyle g_{1}(t)=f\left(g_{0}\left({t}/{\upkappa}\right)\right)\leq f\left(1+{t}/{d}\right)=e^{t}\kern 5.0pt,

where the inequality follows from the fact that
f
is increasing.

Assume now that (
11.3
) holds for any

t\in\left[0,\frac{d}{2\upkappa}\right)
, and any depth
\leq r
. We establish (
11.3
) for depth
r+1
as follows.

Let
v_{1},\ldots,v_{{\mathbold{K}}}
be the children of the root
v
, with corresponding couplings
\mathbold{\gamma}_{1},\ldots,\mathbold{\gamma}_{{\mathbold{K}}}
, and let

\mathbf{T}_{i}
be the subtree of
\mathbf{T}
containing
v_{i}
and its progeny. For
i=1,\ldots,{\mathbold{K}}
, and
\ell\geq 0
.
We have

\displaystyle g_{r+1}(t)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\left({t}/{\upkappa}\right)\cdot\sum\nolimits_{0\leq i\leq{\mathbold{K}}}\Psi_{i}(r)\cdot|\tanh(\beta\cdot\mathbold{\gamma}_{i})|^{2}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbold{K}}}\left[\prod\nolimits_{0\leq i\leq{\mathbold{K}}}\mathbb{E}_{\Psi_{i},\mathbold{\gamma}_{i}}\left[\exp\left(\left({t}/{\upkappa}\right)\cdot\Psi_{i}(r)\cdot|\tanh(\beta\cdot\mathbold{\gamma}_{i})|^{2}\right)\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbold{K}}}\left[\left(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbold{\gamma}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\Psi}\left[\exp\left(\left({t}/{\upkappa}\right)\cdot\Psi(r)\cdot|\tanh(\beta\cdot\mathbold{\gamma})|^{2}\right)\right]\right]\right)^{{\mathbold{K}}}\right]

\displaystyle=f\left(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbold{\gamma}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\Psi}\left[\exp\left(\left({t}/{\upkappa}\right)\cdot\Psi(r)\cdot|\tanh(\beta\cdot\mathbold{\gamma})|^{2}\right)\right]\right]\right)

(11.7)

\displaystyle=f\left(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbold{\gamma}}\left[g_{r}\left(\left({t}/{\upkappa}\right)\cdot|\tanh(\beta\cdot\mathbold{\gamma})|^{2}\right)\right]\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Since
|\tanh(\beta\cdot\mathbold{\gamma})|^{2}\leq 1
, we apply the inductive hypothesis for
g_{r}
in (
11.7
), and get

\displaystyle g_{r+1}(t)

\displaystyle\leq f\left(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbold{\gamma}}\left[\exp\left(({t}/{\upkappa})\cdot|\tanh(\beta\cdot\mathbold{\gamma})|^{2}\right)\right]\right)\leq f\left(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbold{\gamma}}\left[\exp\left(({t}/{\upkappa})\cdot|\beta\cdot\mathbold{\gamma}|^{2}\right)\right]\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Using the mgf for chi-square as above, we further see that

\displaystyle g_{r+1}(t)

\displaystyle\leq f\left(\left(1-2\cdot{t\cdot\upkappa}/{d}\right)^{-1/2}\right)\leq f\left(1+{t}/{d}\right)\leq e^{t}\kern 5.0pt,

where the last inequality holds for sufficiently large
d
.

Using Markov’s inequality, for any
C>100
, for every
r\geq 1
, and any

t\in\left[0,\frac{d}{2\upkappa}\right)
,
we have

\displaystyle\Pr[{\tt SQR}(v,r)>C\cdot\upkappa^{r}]\leq\frac{g_{r}(t)}{\exp\left(Ct\right)}\leq\exp\left((1-C)\cdot t\right)\kern 5.0pt,

as desired.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

10.1
part (a).

For integer
\ell>0
and vertex
w
in
\mathbold{G}=\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
, let
T=T_{\rm BFS}(w,\ell)
be the breadth-first-search tree
from vertex
w
restricted to its first
\ell
levels.

In what follows, we consider the quantity
{\tt SQR}_{T}(w,\ell)
. Note that this quantity might be
different from
{\tt SQR}(w,\ell)
which we need to bound. Since the weights that induce the two quantities
are non-negative, we always have

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}_{T}(w,\ell)\leq{\tt SQR}(w,\ell)\kern 5.0pt.

We have equality when the ball of radius
\ell
around
w
induces a graph which is a tree.

It is standard to show that in
T=T_{\rm BFS}(w,\ell)
, each vertex at level
h<\ell
has a number of
children which is dominated by
\mathtt{Poisson}(d)
. Hence, there is a coupling between

T_{\rm BFS}(w,\ell)
and the standard Galton-Watson tree
\mathbf{T}
with
\mathtt{Poisson}(d)
offspring
distribution, such that with probability 1,
\mathbf{T}
contains a copy of
T_{\rm BFS}(w,\ell)
.

Let
\widehat{\mathbf{T}}
be a subtree of
\mathbf{T}
which is isomorphic to
T
. Let
F:V(\widehat{\mathbf{T}})\to V(T)

be an isomorphism map between the two trees.

Consider an instance of the 2-spin model on
\mathbf{T}
with inverse temperature
\beta
. Assume that
the couplings at the edges of
\mathbf{T}
are obtained as follows: for each edge

e=\{x,z\}\in\widehat{\mathbf{T}}
, the coupling is equal to
\mathbold{\gamma}_{\{F(x),F(z)\}}
.
For every edge
e\notin\widehat{\mathbf{T}}
, its coupling is an independent standard
normal
\mathbold{\gamma}_{e}
.

Consider the quantity
{\tt SQR}_{\mathbf{T}}(u,\ell)
for the system in
\mathbf{T}
, where
u=F^{-1}(w)
.
Since the weights that specify
{\tt SQR}_{T}(w,\ell)
and
{\tt SQR}_{\mathbf{T}}(u,\ell)
are non-negative
numbers, with probability
1
, we have

(11.8)

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}_{T}(w,\ell)\leq{\tt SQR}_{\mathbf{T}}(u,\ell)\kern 5.0pt.

Recalling that
L=\frac{\log n}{\sqrt{d}}
, an application of
Lemma

11.1

for
\frac{10}{\sqrt{d}}<t<\frac{d}{2\upkappa}
implies

\displaystyle\Pr\left[{\tt SQR}_{\mathbf{T}}(u,\ell)\leq L\cdot{\upkappa}^{\ell}\right]\geq 1-n^{5/2}\kern 5.0pt.

Combining the above with (
11.8
), and a simple union bound, we get

(11.9)

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\forall w\in V_{n},\quad{\tt SQR}_{T}(w,\ell)\leq L\cdot\upkappa^{\ell}\right]\geq 1-n^{3/2}\kern 5.0pt.

Let
\mathcal{E}_{1}
be the event that
\forall w\in V_{n}
we have

{\tt SQR}_{T}(w,\ell)\leq L\cdot\upkappa^{\ell}
. Also, let
{\mathcal{E}}_{2}
be the event that
for all
\ell<\frac{\log n}{(\log d)^{2}}
and for all vertices
w\in V_{n}
,
the graph that is induced by the vertices within distance
\ell
from
w
is
a tree with at most one extra edge.

Note that the desirable event
{\mathcal{E}}_{S}
of
Lemma

7.6
implies
{\mathcal{E}}_{2}
.
Recall that
{\mathcal{E}}_{S}
is the event that “every set of vertices
S
in
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
with

|S|\leq 2\frac{\log n}{(\log d)^{2}}
, spans at most
|S|
edges.” To see why
{\mathcal{E}}_{S}
implies

{\mathcal{E}}_{2}
, assume towards a contradiction that
{\mathcal{E}}_{S}
occurs while we have
\overline{{\mathcal{E}}_{2}}
.

Fix
\ell<\frac{\log n}{(\log d)^{2}}
. Recall that
N_{\ell}(w)
denotes the vertices at distance
\leq\ell
from
vertex
w
. Consider a BFS tree
T_{w,\ell}
emanating from vertex
w
and truncated at
depth
\ell
. If the vertices of
N_{\ell}(w)
span two or more cycles, then
N_{\ell}(w)
spans
two edges
e_{1},e_{2}
that do not appear in
T_{w,\ell}
. Let
M
be the set of vertices containing
the endpoints of
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
, along with their shortest paths to
w
. Then, we have a set
of vertices
M
such that
|M|\leq 2\ell<2\frac{\log n}{(\log d)^{2}}
which spans at least

|M|+1
edges. Clearly, this contradicts our assumption that event
{\mathcal{E}}_{S}
holds.

The above argument and
Lemma

7.6
imply that
\Pr[{\mathcal{E}}_{2}]\geq 1-n^{-3/4}
.
Combining this observation with (
11.9
),
a simple union bound yields

(11.10)

\displaystyle\Pr[{\mathcal{E}}_{1},{\mathcal{E}}_{2}]\geq 1-2n^{-2/3}\kern 5.0pt.

For what follows, assume that events
{\mathcal{E}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{E}}_{2}
hold. Then, for a vertex
w\in V_{n}
,
such that the ball of radius
\ell<\frac{\log n}{(\log d)^{2}}
centred at
w
is a tree, we have

(11.11)

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}(w,\ell)\leq L\cdot\upkappa^{\ell}\kern 5.0pt.

Suppose now that for vertex
w\in V_{n}
, the ball of radius
\ell
induces a unicylic graph.
Recall the BFS tree
T=T_{\rm BFS}(w,\ell)
. Let
\widehat{e}=\{x,z\}
be the crossing edge.

For
{\tt SQR}_{T}(w,\ell)
, we consider only paths in
T=T_{\rm BFS}(w,\ell)
. The extra paths that
we consider with
{\tt SQR}(w,\ell)
correspond to those that either contain the subpath
(x,z)

or the subpath
(z,x)
. Let
{\tt SQR}_{A}
is the weight of the paths of the first category, i.e.,
those that use the subpath
(x,z)
. Similarly,
{\tt SQR}_{B}
be the weight of the paths of the
second one. It is not hard to verify that

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}_{A},\ {\tt SQR}_{B}\leq\max\nolimits_{v\in V_{n}}\{{\tt SQR}_{T}(v,\ell)\}\kern 5.0pt.

Therefore, for such vertex
w
we have

(11.12)

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}(w,\ell)\leq 3\cdot\max\nolimits_{v\in V_{n}}\{{\tt SQR}_{T}(v,\ell)\}\kern 5.0pt.

Clearly, (
11.11
) and (
11.12
) imply that, on the events

{\mathcal{E}}_{1},{\mathcal{E}}_{2}
, we have

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}(w,\ell)

\displaystyle\leq 3\cdot L\cdot\upkappa^{\ell}

\displaystyle\forall w\in V_{n}\kern 5.0pt.

Theorem

10.1
part (a) follows from the above and (
11.10
).
∎

Proof of
Theorem

10.1
part (b).

Let
{\mathcal{E}}_{1}
be the event that
(\UpJ,\beta)
admits an
\varepsilon
-block partition
{\mathcal{B}}
.
Also, let
{\mathcal{E}}_{2}
be the event that for all positive integers
\ell<\frac{\log n}{(\log d)^{2}}
and for all vertices

w\in V_{n}
, the subgraph of
\mathbold{G}=\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
that is induced by the vertices within distance

\ell
from
w
is a tree with at most one extra edge.

From
Theorem

15.2
and
Lemma

7.6
, we have

(11.13)

\displaystyle\Pr[{\mathcal{E}}_{1},{\mathcal{E}}_{2}]

\displaystyle=1-o(1)\kern 5.0pt.

Assume that the events
{\mathcal{E}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{E}}_{2}
hold. Let
v\in V_{n}
be an
\varepsilon
-block vertex.
For the range of
\ell
we consider in this proof, the vertices along paths of length
\ell

that emanate from vertex
v
induce the subgraph
B_{v,\ell}
of
\mathbold{G}
, which is a tree with
at most one extra edge.
We consider two cases. The first corresponds to having
B_{v,\ell}
tree, while
the second one corresponds to having
B_{v,\ell}
unicyclic.

Suppose that
B_{v,\ell}
is a tree. The assumption that
v
is an
\varepsilon
-block vertex implies
the following result.

Corollary 11.2
.

Let
Q
be any path of length
\ell\leq\frac{\log n}{(\log d)^{2}}
emanating from an
\varepsilon
-block vertex
u
.
Let also
M
be the set of heavy vertices in
Q
, i.e., for all
x\in M
we have
{\Uptheta}(x)>1-\varepsilon/2
.
Property
1
in the definition of
\varepsilon
-block vertices implies

\displaystyle\prod\nolimits_{x\in M}{\Uptheta}(x)\leq d^{-|M|}\cdot(1-\varepsilon/4)^{|M|-\ell}\kern 5.0pt.

Unfolding
{\tt SQR}(v,\ell)
we get

(11.14)

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}(v,\ell)

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{v_{i}\sim v}\left|\tanh\left(\beta\cdot\mathbold{\gamma}_{\{v,v_{i}\}}\right)\right|^{2}\cdot{\tt SQR}(v_{i},\ell-1)\kern 5.0pt,

where for each
v_{i}
, we have

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}(v_{i},\ell-1)=\sum\nolimits_{Q}\prod\nolimits_{e\in Q}|\tanh(\beta\cdot\mathbold{\gamma}_{e})|^{2}\kern 5.74997pt,

while
Q
varies over all paths of length
\ell-1
that emanate from
v_{i}
and do not
use the edge
\{v,v_{i}\}
.

From (
11.14
), we have

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}(v,\ell)

\displaystyle\leq\max\nolimits_{v_{i}}\left\{{\tt SQR}(v_{i},\ell-1)\right\}\cdot\sum\nolimits_{v_{i}\sim v}\left|\tanh\left(\beta\cdot\mathbold{\gamma}_{\{v,v_{i}\}}\right)\right|^{2}

(11.15)

\displaystyle\leq\max\nolimits_{v_{i}}\left\{{\tt SQR}(v_{i},\ell-1)\right\}\cdot{\Uptheta}(v)\kern 5.0pt.

Repeating the above step
\ell
times in total, we obtain

(11.16)

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}(v,\ell)

\displaystyle\leq\max\nolimits_{Q}\left\{\prod\nolimits_{w\in Q}{\Uptheta}(w)\right\}\kern 5.0pt,

where
Q
varies over all paths of length
\ell
that emanate from vertex
v
.

Let
P
be a path that maximizes the r.h.s. in (
11.16
).
Let vertex set
M
consist of each
x\in P
such that
{\Uptheta}(x)>1-\varepsilon/2
.
Also, let
m=|M|
.
Using (
11.16
) and
Corollary

11.2
, we have

(11.17)

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}(v,\ell)

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-{\varepsilon}/{2}\right)^{\ell-m}\prod\nolimits_{x\in M}{\Uptheta}(x)

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-{\varepsilon}/{2}\right)^{\ell-m}\cdot d^{-m}\cdot\left(1-{\varepsilon}/{4}\right)^{m-\ell}

(11.18)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{1-\varepsilon/{2}}{1-\varepsilon/{4}}\right)^{\ell-m}\cdot d^{-m}\leq\left(1-{\varepsilon}/{4}\right)^{\ell}\kern 5.0pt,

where the last inequality is valid for large enough
d
.

We proceed with the case where
B_{v,\ell}
is a unicyclic graph. Let
C=x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
be
the unique cycle of
B_{v,\ell}
. W.l.o.g., assume that
x_{1}
is the closest vertex of
C
to
vertex
v
. Let
P
be the path from
x_{1}
to
v
, while let
\{z,x_{1}\}
be the first edge of
the path. Letting
|P|=r
, recall that
r\geq(150/\varepsilon)\cdot\log\log n
.

For
\ell\leq r
,
B_{v,\ell}
is a tree. In this case, the bound on
{\tt SQR}(v,\ell)
follows
by repeating the previous arguments.

Suppose now that
\ell>r
.
Repeating the step in (
11.15
)
r
times in total, we obtain

(11.19)

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}(v,\ell)

\displaystyle\leq\max\nolimits_{Q=y_{1},\ldots,y_{r}}\prod\nolimits_{i\leq r-1}{\Uptheta}(y_{i})\cdot{\tt SQR}(y_{r},\ell-r)\kern 5.0pt,

where
Q
varies over all paths of length
r
that emanate from vertex
v
. If the maximum in
the r.h.s. of (
11.19
) is attained at a path different from
P
, then
all extensions of this path avoid the cycle
C
and therefore we use the same argument as in the tree
case above, and obtain the same bound on
{\tt SQR}(v,\ell)
as in (
11.18
).

Suppose now that path
P
maximises the r.h.s. of (
11.19
). From

Theorem

10.1
part (a), we have

(11.20)

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}(y_{r},\ell-r)\leq(\log n)^{3}\cdot{\upkappa}^{\ell-r}\kern 5.0pt.

Plugging (
11.20
) into (
11.19
) yields

(11.21)

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}(v,\ell)

\displaystyle\leq(\log n)^{3}\cdot{\upkappa}^{\ell-r}\cdot\prod\nolimits_{x\in P}{\Uptheta}(x)\kern 5.0pt.

Working as in (
11.18
) above, we further obtain

\displaystyle\prod\nolimits_{x\in P}{\Uptheta}(x)

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-{\varepsilon}/{4}\right)^{r}\kern 5.0pt.

Since
\varepsilon
is such that
1-\varepsilon/8\geq\upkappa
,
plugging the above into (
11.21
),
we obtain

(11.22)

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}(v,\ell)

\displaystyle\leq(\log n)^{3}\cdot\left(1-{\varepsilon}/{4}\right)^{r}\cdot{\upkappa}^{\ell-r}\leq(\log n)^{3}\cdot\left(1-{\varepsilon}/{8}\right)^{2r}\cdot{\upkappa}^{\ell-r}\leq\left(1-{\varepsilon}/{8}\right)^{\ell}\kern 5.0pt,

where in the last inequality we use that
r\geq(150/\varepsilon)\cdot\log\log n
and hence

(\log n)^{3}\left(1-{\varepsilon}/{8}\right)^{r}<1
.

All the above conclude the proof of
Theorem

10.1
part (b).
∎

12.
Proof of
Proposition

10.2

12.1.
Proof of
Proposition

10.2
, part (a)

If block
B
is a tree,
B
and
\mathcal{T}_{\rm AP}(B,\alpha)
are identical. Then, for any
w\in V(T)
, the
bound on
{\tt SQR}_{T}(w,\ell)
can be obtained using part (a) of
Theorem

10.1
.

Consider now the case where block
B
is unicyclic. For such block
B
and vertex

\alpha\in\partial_{\rm in}B
, consider the all-path tree
T=\mathcal{T}_{\rm AP}(B,\alpha)
. Also
consider the subtrees
T_{\rm base}
,
T_{\rm high}
and
T_{\rm low}
in
T
, i.e., as they are defined
in
Section

7.2
. Similarly, consider the vertices
u_{\rm low}
and
u_{\rm high}
in
T_{\rm base}
.

We focus on bounding
{\tt SQR}_{T}(w,\ell)
, for
w\in V(T)
in
T
. W.l.o.g., assume that

w\in T_{\rm base}
. Let
{\tt SQR}_{0}(w,\ell)
be the contribution to
{\tt SQR}_{T}(w,\ell)
coming
from paths that are restricted to tree
T_{\rm base}
. The paths in
T_{\rm base}
that are not considered for

{\tt SQR}_{0}(w,\ell)
can be divided into two groups:

Group 1:

This group consists of each path
P=y_{1},\ldots,y_{\ell}
in
\mathcal{T}_{\rm AP}(B,\alpha)
such that

y_{1}=w
, and for appropriate
j
,
P^{\prime}=y_{1},\ldots,y_{j}
corresponds to the path from the root
w
to vertex
u_{\rm high}
, while the subpath

P^{\prime\prime}=y_{j},\ldots,y_{\ell}
is in
T_{\rm high}
.

Group 2:

The group consists of each path
P=y_{1},\ldots,y_{\ell}
in
\mathcal{T}_{\rm AP}(B,\alpha)

such that
y_{1}=w
, and for appropriate
j
,
P^{\prime}=y_{1},\ldots,y_{j}
corresponds
to the path from the root
w
to vertex
u_{\rm low}
, while the subpath

P^{\prime\prime}=y_{j},\ldots,y_{\ell}
is in subtree
T_{\rm low}
.

Let
{\tt SQR}_{1}(w,\ell)
be the contribution to
{\tt SQR}_{T}(w,\ell)
from the paths in Group 1. Similarly, we have
{\tt SQR}_{2}(w,\ell)
for the paths
in Group 2.

Arguing as in the proof of part (a) of
Theorem

10.1
, we have

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}_{0}(w,\ell)

\displaystyle\leq 3\cdot L\cdot{\upkappa}^{\ell}\kern 5.0pt.

Also, with similar arguments we obtain

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}_{1}(w,\ell)

\displaystyle\leq 3\cdot L\cdot\upkappa^{\ell}

and

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}_{2}(w,\ell)

\displaystyle\leq 3\cdot L\cdot\upkappa^{\ell}\kern 5.0pt.

Then, our result follows by noting that

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}_{T}(w,\ell)

\displaystyle={\tt SQR}_{0}(w,\ell)+{\tt SQR}_{1}(w,\ell)+{\tt SQR}_{2}(w,\ell)\kern 5.0pt.

All the above conclude the proof of part (a) of
Proposition

10.2
.

\Box

12.2.
Proof of
Proposition

10.2
, part (b)

Since both
w,\alpha\in\partial_{\rm in}B
are arbitrary, we assume w.l.o.g. that
\alpha=w
, to simplify the
notation that follows.
We consider two cases for
B
. The first corresponds to

B
being a tree, while the second one corresponds to
B
being a unicyclic graph.

When
B
is tree, note that for
w\in\partial_{\rm in}B
, we have that
block
B
and
T=\mathcal{T}_{\rm AP}(B,w)
are identical. Such
w
, is also an
\varepsilon
-block vertex.
Furthermore, we have

(12.1)

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}_{T}(w,\ell)

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{w_{i}\sim w}\left|\tanh\left(\beta\cdot\mathbold{\gamma}_{\{w,w_{i}\}}\right)\right|^{2}\cdot{\tt SQR}_{T_{i}}(w_{i},\ell-1)\kern 5.0pt,

where for each
w_{i}
, neighbour of vertex
w
,
we let
T_{i}
be the subtree of
T
that contains
w_{i}
and its descendants. Hence,

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}_{T_{i}}(w_{i},\ell-1)=\sum\nolimits_{Q}\prod\nolimits_{e\in Q}|\tanh(\beta\cdot\mathbold{\gamma}_{e})|^{2}\kern 5.74997pt,

where
Q
varies over all paths of length
\ell-1
that emanate from
w_{i}
and do not
use the edge
\{w,w_{i}\}
.

Note that the specification of block
B
implies that vertex
w
has only one
neighbour
z
in
T
, while
{\rm\Upgamma}_{w,z}\leq d^{-1/10}
.
Combining this observation with (
12.1
), we have

(12.2)

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}_{T}(w,\ell)

\displaystyle\leq d^{-1/10}\cdot{\tt SQR}_{T_{z}}(z,\ell-1)\kern 5.0pt.

For
{\tt SQR}_{T_{z}}(z,\ell-1)
, repeating the same argument as those we use to obtain (
11.16
)
in the proof
Theorem

10.1
part (b), we get

(12.3)

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}_{T_{z}}(z,\ell-1)

\displaystyle\leq\max\nolimits_{Q}\left\{\prod\nolimits_{v\in Q}{\Uptheta}(v)\right\}\kern 5.0pt,

where
Q
in varies over all paths of length
\ell-1
that emanate from vertex
z
in
T_{z}
. Recall that

z
is the single neighbour of
w
in
T
.

Let
P
be the path in the subtree
T_{z}
that maximizes the r.h.s. in (
12.3
).
Let vertex set
M
consist of each
x\in P
such that
{\Uptheta}(x)>(1-\varepsilon/2)
. Also, let
m=|M|
.

Using (
12.3
), the fact that
z
is next to the
\varepsilon
-block vertex
w
,
and
Corollary

11.2
, we have

(12.4)

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}_{T_{z}}(z,\ell-1)

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-{\varepsilon}/{2}\right)^{\ell-m-1}\prod\nolimits_{x\in M}{\Uptheta}(x)

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-{\varepsilon}/{2}\right)^{\ell-m-1}\cdot d^{-m}\cdot\left(1-{\varepsilon}/{4}\right)^{m-\ell}

(12.5)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{d^{m}(1-{\varepsilon}/{4})}\cdot\left(\frac{1-\varepsilon/{2}}{1-\varepsilon/{4}}\right)^{\ell-m-1}\leq\left(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\right)^{\ell-1}\kern 5.0pt,

where the last inequality is valid for large enough
d
.
Plugging (
12.5
) into (
12.2
) gives

(12.6)

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}_{T}(w,\ell)

\displaystyle\leq d^{-1/10}\cdot\left(1-{\varepsilon}/{4}\right)^{\ell-1}\kern 5.0pt.

The above proves
Proposition

10.2
, part (b), for the case where

B
is a tree.

We proceed with the case where
B
is a unicyclic graph and
w\in\partial_{\rm in}B
. Let

C=x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
be the unique cycle of
B
. W.l.o.g., assume that
x_{1}
is the closest
vertex of
C
to vertex
w
. Let
\{v,x_{1}\}
be the first edge in the shortest
path
P
connecting
w
to
x_{1}
. Letting the length of
P
be
|P|=r
, we
recall that
r\geq(150/\varepsilon)\cdot\log\log n
.

For such block
B
and vertex
w\in\partial_{\rm in}B
, consider the all-path
tree
T=\mathcal{T}_{\rm AP}(B,w)
. Also consider the standard subtrees of
T
, i.e., the subtrees
T_{\rm base}
,
T_{\rm high}

and
T_{\rm low}
. Similarly, consider the vertices
u_{\rm low}
and
u_{\rm high}
in
T_{\rm base}
.

The assumption that
w\in\partial_{\rm in}B
implies immediately the following result.

Corollary 12.1
.

Let
Q
be any path in
T=\mathcal{T}_{\rm AP}(B,w)
of length
\ell
, which emanates from the root and does
not contain copies of the vertices in
C
. Let also
M
be the set of heavy vertices in
Q
, i.e., for
all
x\in M
we have
{\Uptheta}(x)>1-\varepsilon/2
. The definition of
\varepsilon
-block vertices implies

\displaystyle\prod\nolimits_{x\in M}{\Uptheta}(x)\leq d^{-|M|}\cdot(1-\varepsilon/4)^{|M|-\ell-1}\kern 5.0pt.

Furthermore, working as in (
12.2
), we have

(12.7)

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}_{T}(w,\ell)

\displaystyle\leq d^{-1/10}\cdot{\tt SQR}_{T_{z}}(z,\ell-1)

where
z
is the unique neighbour of
w
in
T
. We also have

(12.8)

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}_{T_{z}}(z,\ell-1)

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{z_{i}\sim z}\left|\tanh\left(\beta\cdot\mathbold{\gamma}_{\{z,z_{i}\}}\right)\right|^{2}\cdot{\tt SQR}_{T_{z_{i}}}(z_{i},\ell-2)\kern 5.0pt,

where for each
z_{i}
, we have

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}_{T_{z_{i}}}(z_{i},\ell-2)=\sum\nolimits_{Q}\prod\nolimits_{e\in Q}|\tanh(\beta\cdot\mathbold{\gamma}_{e})|^{2}\kern 5.74997pt,

while
Q
varies over all paths
Q
in
T_{z_{i}}
of length
\ell-2
that emanate from
z_{i}
and do
not use the edge
\{z,z_{i}\}
.
Using standard argument from previous proofs, (
12.8
) implies

(12.9)

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}_{T_{z}}(z,\ell-1)

\displaystyle\leq\max\nolimits_{z_{i}}\left\{{\tt SQR}_{T_{z_{i}}}(z_{i},\ell-2)\right\}\cdot{\Uptheta}(z)\kern 5.0pt.

For
\ell\leq r
, we obtain the same bound on
{\tt SQR}_{T_{z}}(z,\ell-1)
and

{\tt SQR}_{T}(w,\ell)
as in the tree case, i.e., as in (
12.6
).

For
\ell>r
, repeating the above step
r
times, we obtain

(12.10)

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}_{T_{z}}(z,\ell-1)

\displaystyle\leq\max\nolimits_{Q=y_{1},\ldots,y_{r}}\prod\nolimits_{i\leq r-1}{\Uptheta}(y_{i})\cdot{\tt SQR}_{T_{y_{r}}}(y_{r},\ell-r)\kern 5.0pt,

where
Q
in varies over all paths of length
r-1
that emanate from vertex
z
.

If the maximum in the rhs of (
12.10
) is attained at a path different
from
P
, then all extensions of this path avoid the copies of the vertices in cycle
C
, and therefore
with an argument similar to those we use above, we obtain the same bound on
{\tt SQR}_{T}(w,\ell)

as in (
12.6
).

If the maximum in rhs of (
12.10
) is
attained by path
P
, then, using
Theorem

10.1
part (a), we have

(12.11)

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}_{T_{y_{r}}}(y_{r},\ell-r)\leq(\log n)^{3}\cdot{\upkappa}^{\ell-r}\kern 5.0pt.

Plugging (
12.11
) into (
12.10
) yields

(12.12)

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}_{T_{z}}(z,\ell-1)

\displaystyle\leq(\log n)^{3}\cdot{\upkappa}^{\ell-r}\cdot\prod\nolimits_{x\in P}{\Uptheta}(x)\kern 5.0pt.

Using
Corollary

12.1
, we further obtain

\displaystyle\prod\nolimits_{x\in P}{\Uptheta}(x)

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-{\varepsilon}/{4}\right)^{r}\kern 5.0pt.

Since
1-\varepsilon/8\geq\upkappa
,
plugging the above into (
12.12
) and working
as in (
11.22
) we obtain

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}_{T_{z}}(z,\ell-1)

\displaystyle\leq(\log n)^{3}\cdot\left(1-\varepsilon/8\right)^{2r}\cdot{\upkappa}^{\ell-r}\leq(1-\varepsilon/8)^{\ell}\kern 5.0pt,

where in the last inequality we use that
r\geq(150/\varepsilon)\cdot\log\log n
and hence

(\log n)^{3}\left(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{8}\right)^{r}<1
.
We obtain the desirable bound on
{\tt SQR}_{T}(w,\ell)
by plugging the above inequality
into (
12.7
).

All the above conclude the proof of
Proposition

10.2
part (b).

\Box

12.3.
Proof of
Proposition

10.2
, part (c)

We consider two cases for
B
. The first corresponds to
B
being a tree, while the second one
corresponds to
B
being a unicyclic graph.

For the case of
B
being a tree, we obtain the desired bound

(12.13)

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}_{\partial T}(w,\ell)

\displaystyle\leq d^{-1/10}\cdot\left(1-{\varepsilon}/{4}\right)^{\ell-1}\kern 5.0pt.

by following similar arguments to those we use to obtain (
12.6
).

Let us now focus on the case
B
being a unicyclic graph. Consider
T=\mathcal{T}_{\rm AP}(B,\alpha)

for some
\alpha\in\partial_{\rm in}B
, while let
w\in V(T)
.

Letting
\Lambda
be the set of leaves of
T
, we distinguish the following cases:

Case 1.a:

{\rm dist}_{T}(w,\Lambda)>(40/\varepsilon)\cdot\log\log n
and
\ell<(40/\varepsilon)\cdot\log\log n
,

Case 1.b:

{\rm dist}_{T}(w,\Lambda)>(40/\varepsilon)\cdot\log\log n
and
\ell\geq(40/\varepsilon)\cdot\log\log n
,

Case 2.a:

{\rm dist}_{T}(w,\Lambda)\leq(40/\varepsilon)\cdot\log\log n
and
\ell<(60/\varepsilon)\cdot\log\log n
,

Case 2.b:

{\rm dist}_{T}(w,\Lambda)\leq(40/\varepsilon)\cdot\log\log n
and
\ell\geq(60/\varepsilon)\cdot\log\log n
.

Since the copies of
\partial_{\rm in}B
in
T
belong to set
\Lambda
, for Case 1.a, we have

(12.14)

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}_{\partial T}(w,\ell)

\displaystyle=0\kern 5.0pt.

For Case 1.b, we have

(12.15)

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}_{\partial T}(w,\ell)

\displaystyle\leq d^{-1/10}\cdot{\tt SQR}_{T}(w,\ell-1)\leq 10\cdot d^{-1/10}\cdot L\cdot{\upkappa}^{\ell-1}

where for the first inequality, we recall that each copy of
\partial_{\rm in}B
in
T
is of
degree 1, while the coupling of the edge that connects it to the rest of the tree is at most
d^{-1/10}
.
The second inequality is due to
Proposition

10.2
, part (a).

Since
\varepsilon
is such that
1-\varepsilon/8\geq\upkappa
, for the rightmost quantity
in (
12.15
), we have

\displaystyle 10\cdot d^{-1/10}\cdot L\cdot{\upkappa}^{\ell-1}

\displaystyle\leq d^{-1/10}\cdot\log n\cdot\left(1-{\varepsilon}/{8}\right)^{\ell-1}\leq d^{-1/10}\cdot\log n\cdot\left(1-{\varepsilon}/{16}\right)^{2(\ell-1)}

\displaystyle\leq d^{-1/10}\cdot(1-\varepsilon/16)^{\ell-1}\kern 5.0pt,

where in the last inequality we use that
\ell\geq(40/\varepsilon)\cdot\log\log n
, and hence

\log n\cdot\left(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{16}\right)^{\ell-1}<1
.
Plugging the above inequality into (
12.15
) we obtain

(12.16)

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}_{\partial T}(w,\ell)

\displaystyle\leq d^{-1/10}\cdot(1-\varepsilon/16)^{\ell-1}\kern 5.0pt.

For Case 2.a, since
\ell\leq(60/\varepsilon)\cdot\log\log n
, no path of length
\ell
intersects
with the copies of the vertices in the cycle of
B
. Recall that the cycle is at distance
r>(150/\varepsilon)\cdot\log\log n

from
w
.
Employing the same arguments as those used for Case 1.b, we get

(12.17)

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}_{\partial T}(w,\ell)

\displaystyle\leq d^{-1/10}\cdot(1-\varepsilon/16)^{\ell-1}\kern 5.0pt.

Consider now Case 2.b. Let
{\tt SQR}_{1}(w,\ell)
be the contribution to
{\tt SQR}_{\partial T}(w,\ell)

from the paths of length
\ell
that do not intersect copies of vertices in the cycle of block
B
. Similarly, let

{\tt SQR}_{2}(w,\ell)
be the contribution to
{\tt SQR}_{\partial T}(w,\ell)
from paths of length
\ell
that
contain copies of vertices in the cycle of block
B
. We have

(12.18)

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}_{\partial T}(w,\ell)\leq{\tt SQR}_{1}(w,\ell)+{\tt SQR}_{2}(w,\ell)\kern 5.0pt.

Working as in Case 2.a., we have

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}_{1}(w,\ell)\leq d^{-1/10}\cdot(1-\varepsilon/16)^{\ell-1}\kern 5.0pt.

Furthermore, working as in Case 1.b., we get

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}_{2}(w,\ell)\leq d^{-1/10}\cdot(1-\varepsilon/16)^{\ell-1}\kern 5.0pt.

Plugging the above two inequalities into (
12.18
), implies

(12.19)

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}_{\partial T}(w,\ell)\leq 2\cdot d^{-1/10}\cdot(1-\varepsilon/16)^{\ell-1}\kern 5.0pt.

From (
12.14
),
(
12.16
), (
12.17
) and (
12.19
),
we conclude that when
B
is a unicylic block, we have

(12.20)

\displaystyle{\tt SQR}_{\partial T}(w,\ell)\leq 2\cdot d^{-1/10}\cdot(1-\varepsilon/16)^{\ell-1}\kern 5.0pt.

Part (c) in
Proposition

10.2
, follows from (
12.20
)
and (
12.13
).

\Box

13.
Proof of
Theorem

6.3

We consider the interaction matrix
\UpJ=\UpJ(\mathbold{G}(n,d/n),\{\mathbold{\gamma}_{e}\})

defined as in (
6.1
). Using matrix
\UpJ
and the parameter
\beta>0
, we consider
yet another weight for the paths of
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
. Specifically, for each path
P
, we define

(13.1)

\displaystyle\Upupsilon_{\beta}(P)

\displaystyle=\beta\cdot\sum\nolimits_{e}|\UpJ_{e}|+\sum\nolimits_{v}\log\mathrm{deg}(v)\kern 5.0pt,

where the first sum varies over all edges
e
in
\mathbold{G}
having at least one endpoint in
P
, while the second
sum varies over all vertices of
P
.

Lemma 13.1
.

There exists
d_{0}>0
such that for any
d\geq d_{0}
, and for any
0<\beta\leq\beta_{c}(d)
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\text{each path $P$ in $\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)$ s.t. $\textstyle{|P|\leq 2\cdot\frac{\log n}{d^{(1/2)+10^{-2}}}}$ has $\textstyle{\Upupsilon_{\beta}(P)\leq 2\cdot d^{-1/100}\cdot\log n}$ }\right]\geq 1-n^{-d^{47/100}}\kern 5.0pt.

The proof of
Lemma

13.1
appears in
Section

13.1
.

Fix
\varepsilon
,
d
as specified in
Theorem

6.3
and
0<\beta\leq\beta_{c}(d)
.
Let
{\mathcal{E}}
be the event that

(1)

there is an
\varepsilon
-block partition
{\mathcal{B}}
for
V_{n}
,

(2)

for every path
P
with
|P|\leq 2\cdot\frac{\log n}{d^{(1/2)+10^{-2}}}
in
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)

we have
\Upupsilon_{\beta}(P)\leq 2\cdot{d^{-1/100}\cdot\log n}
.

(3)

The maximum entry in matrix
|\UpJ|
is
\leq 10\sqrt{\log n}
.

In light of
Theorem

4.3
,
Lemma

13.1
, and
Lemma

A.2
, we have that
\Pr[{\mathcal{E}}]=1-o(1)
.

On the event
{\mathcal{E}}
, for
B\in{\mathcal{B}}
, for any
\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}^{\partial_{\rm out}B}
, let
\mu^{\sigma}_{B}
be
the 2-spin model on block
B\cup\partial_{\rm out}B
with edge weights specified by matrix
\UpJ
and
inverse temperature
\beta
.
Also, let
\uptau_{B}
be the relaxation time of the (single-site) Glauber dynamics on

\mu^{\sigma}_{B}
, for worst-case
\sigma
. That is,
\sigma
is chosen so that the relaxation time is the maximum possible.
Similarly, let
{C}_{\rm mLSI}(B)
be the modified log-Sobolev constant of Glauber dynamics for
B
.

Using the weighting scheme in (
13.1
) we prove the following result.

Theorem 13.2
.

On the event
{\mathcal{E}}
, for any
B\in{\mathcal{B}}
we have that
\uptau_{B}\leq n^{\frac{24}{\sqrt{\log d}}}
.

The proof of Theorem
13.2
appears in
Section

14
.

Proposition 13.3
.

On the event
{\mathcal{E}}
, for any
B\in{\mathcal{B}}
we have

\displaystyle{C}_{\rm mLSI}(B)\geq\left(-\log(\mu_{\min})\cdot\uptau_{B}\right)^{-1}\kern 5.0pt,

where

\mu_{\min}=\min_{\tau\in\partial_{\rm out}B}\min_{\sigma:\mu^{\tau}_{B}(\sigma)>0}\{\mu^{\tau}_{B}(\sigma)\}
.

For a proof of
Proposition

13.3
is is standard, see
Section

A.5

From
Proposition

13.3
and
Theorem

13.2
we obtain that, on the event
{\mathcal{E}}
, we have

\displaystyle{C}_{\rm mLSI}(B)

\displaystyle=\textstyle\Omega\left(|B|^{-1}\cdot n^{-\frac{24}{\sqrt{\log d}}}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Then, using the standard fact
[
35
, Lemma 2.5]
, we have

\displaystyle{C}_{\rm mLSI}(\mu_{\UpJ_{H},\beta,\uph})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\min_{B\in B}\left\{{C}_{\rm mLSI}(B)\cdot|B|\right\}\geq\Omega\left(n^{-\left(1+\frac{24}{\sqrt{\log d}}\right)}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

The above concludes the proof of
Theorem

6.3
.

13.1.
Proof of
Lemma

13.1

For brevity let
\ell=2\cdot\frac{\log n}{d^{(1/2)+10^{-2}}}
, while recall that
\upz=d^{-1/100}
.

Let
{\mathcal{K}}
be the set of paths
P
in
\mathbold{G}
with
|P|\leq\ell
, and
\Upupsilon_{\beta}(P)>{\upz\cdot\log n}
.
For path
P
, let
F_{1}(P)
, and
F_{2}(P)
be the set of edges in
\mathbold{G}
that have exactly
one, and exactly two endpoints in
P
, respectively.

Fix
\eta\in(0,1)
and let the event
{\mathcal{E}}_{P}=\{|F_{1}(P)|\leq(1+\eta)\cdot d\cdot\ell\}\cap\{|F_{2}(P)|\leq(1+\eta)\cdot d\cdot\ell\}
.
Using the law of total probability we get

(13.2)

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\textstyle{\Upupsilon_{\beta}(P)>{\upz\cdot\log n}}\right]\leq\Pr\left[\textstyle{\Upupsilon_{\beta}(P)>{\upz\cdot\log n}}\ \middle|\ {\mathcal{E}}_{P}\right]+\Pr[\bar{{\mathcal{E}}}_{P}]\kern 5.0pt,

where
\bar{{\mathcal{E}}}_{P}
is the complement of the event
{{\mathcal{E}}}_{P}
.

Each vertex of
P
contributes at most
n
edges in
F_{1}(P)
, each of them with probability
d/n
.

|F_{1}(P)|
is dominated by a
{\tt Binom}(n\ell,d/n)
. Using the Chernoff bound, we get

(13.3)

\displaystyle\Pr\left[|F_{1}(P)|>(1+\eta)\cdot d\ell\right]\leq\exp\left({-d\ell\cdot\eta^{2}/3}\right)\leq n^{-d^{\frac{49}{100}}}\kern 5.0pt,

where the last inequality holds for large
d
.

Similarly,
|F_{2}(P)|
is dominated by a
{\tt Binom}(\ell^{2},d/n)+\ell
, as there are already
\ell
edges in

P
, and there can be at most
\ell^{2}
edges in total with both endpoints in
P
. For

\xi=\frac{n}{\ell}-2
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr[|F_{2}(P)|>(1+\eta)\cdot d\cdot\ell]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\left[|F_{2}(P)|\geq(1+\xi)\cdot\frac{d\cdot\ell^{2}}{n}+\ell\right]

(13.4)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left({-\frac{\xi^{2}}{2+\xi}}\cdot\frac{d\cdot\ell^{2}}{n}\right)\leq n^{-d^{49/100}}\kern 5.0pt,

The first inequality follows from the observation that

(1+\eta)\cdot d\cdot\ell\geq(1+\xi)\cdot\frac{d\cdot\ell^{2}}{n}+\ell
.
The second inequality is an application of the Chernoff bound, while the last inequality holds for large
d
.

Using the union bound, we have

(13.5)

\displaystyle\Pr[\bar{{\mathcal{E}}}_{P}]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\left[|F_{1}(P)|>(1+\eta)\cdot d\cdot\ell\right]+\Pr\left[|F_{2}(P)|>(1+\eta)\cdot d\cdot\ell\right]\leq n^{-d^{48.5/100}}\kern 5.0pt.

We also have

(13.6)

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\beta\cdot\sum\nolimits_{e}|\UpJ_{e}|\geq(19/10)\cdot\upz\cdot\log n\ |\ {\mathcal{E}}_{P}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\left[\beta\cdot\sum\nolimits_{1\leq j\leq N}|\mathbold{\gamma}_{i}|\geq(19/10)\cdot\upz\log n\right],

where
N=2(1+\eta)\cdot\ell\cdot d
and
\mathbold{\gamma}_{j}
, for
j\in[N]
, are i.i.d. standard gaussians.

Furthermore, for
\zeta<1/4
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\beta\cdot\sum\nolimits^{N}_{j=1}|\mathbold{\gamma}_{i}|\geq(19/10)\cdot\upz\cdot\log n\right]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\left[\beta\cdot\sum\nolimits^{N}_{j=1}|\mathbold{\gamma}_{i}|\geq(19/18)\cdot N\cdot\beta\cdot\mathbb{E}[\mathbold{\gamma}_{1}]\right]

(13.7)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-(2+\zeta)\cdot d\cdot\ell\cdot\frac{{(1/18)^{2}}}{\pi}\right)\leq n^{-d^{49/100}}\kern 5.0pt.

The first inequality above holds because
(19/10)\upz\log n<(19/18)N\beta\mathbb{E}[\gamma_{1}]
.
For the second inequality we use
Theorem

A.3
. Recall that

N=2(1+\eta)\cdot\ell\cdot d
.

All the above imply

(13.8)

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\beta\cdot\sum\nolimits_{e}|\UpJ_{e}|\geq(19/10)\cdot\upz\cdot\log n\ |\ {\mathcal{E}}_{P}\right]

\displaystyle\leq n^{-d^{49/100}}\kern 5.0pt.

Also note

(13.9)

\displaystyle\sum\nolimits_{v\in V(P)}\log\mathrm{deg}(v)=\log\left(\prod\nolimits_{v\in V(P)}\mathrm{deg}(v)\right)\leq\ell\cdot\log\left(\ell^{-1}\cdot\sum\nolimits_{v\in V(P)}\mathrm{deg}(v)\right)\kern 5.0pt,

where the last inequality follows from the arithmetic-geometric mean inequality.

On the event
{\mathcal{E}}_{P}
, we also have

\displaystyle\sum\nolimits_{v\in V(P)}\mathrm{deg}(v)=|F_{1}(P)|+2\cdot|F_{2}(P)|\leq 3\cdot(1+\eta)\cdot d\cdot\ell\kern 5.0pt.

Plugging the above into (
13.9
), for
\eta<1/8
, we have

(13.10)

\displaystyle\sum\nolimits_{v\in V(P)}\log\mathrm{deg}(v)\leq\ell\cdot\log(3(1+\eta)d)\leq d^{-1/3}\cdot\log n\kern 5.0pt.

Combining (
13.8
) and (
13.10
), we get

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\textstyle{\Upupsilon_{\beta}(P)>2\cdot\upz\cdot\log n}\ \middle|\ {\mathcal{E}}_{P}\right]\leq n^{-d^{49/100}}\kern 5.0pt,

which per (
13.2
), and (
13.5
), yields

\Pr\left[\Upupsilon_{\beta}(P)>2\cdot{\upz\cdot\log n}\right]\leq n^{-d^{48/100}}
.
Finally, we focus on the cardinality of set
{\mathcal{K}}
. It is easy
to show that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|{\mathcal{K}}|]

\displaystyle\leq 2n\cdot d^{{\ell}}\cdot\Pr\left[\textstyle{\Upupsilon_{\beta}(P)>2\cdot\upz\cdot\log n}\right]\leq n^{-d^{47/10}}\kern 5.0pt.

The lemma follows from the above and the Markov’s inequality.

\Box

14.
Proof of
Theorem

13.2

We use the following well-known comparison result from
[
39
]
for our proof.

Proposition 14.1
.

For graph
G=(V,E)
, let
(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be the block dynamics, with set of blocks
{\mathcal{R}}
, such that each vertex
u\in V
belongs to
M_{u}
different blocks.
Furthermore, let
(Y_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be the single site dynamics on
G
.

Let
\uptau_{\rm block}
and
\uptau_{\rm rel}
be the relaxation times of
(X_{t})
and
(Y_{t})
, respectively.
Furthermore, for each block
B\in{\mathcal{R}}
, let
\uptau_{B}
be the relaxation time of the
single site dynamics on
B
, given any arbitrary condition at
\partial_{\rm out}B
.
Then,

\displaystyle\uptau_{\rm rel}

\displaystyle\leq\textstyle\uptau_{\rm block}\cdot\left(\max_{B\in{\mathcal{R}}}\{\uptau_{B}\}\right)\cdot\left(\max_{u\in V}\{M_{u}\}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Recall the event
{\mathcal{E}}
defined in
Section

13
, satisfying properties
1
,
2
, and
3
.

For a unicyclic block
B\in{\mathcal{B}}
, let
C=(w_{1},\ldots,w_{\ell})
be its unique cycle.
Recall that
\ell\leq 4\frac{\log n}{(\log d)^{4}}
.
We partition the set of vertices in cycle
C
into parts of
M=\left\lceil\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{\log d}}\;\right\rceil
vertices. E.g., the first
group consists of the vertices
w_{1},\ldots,w_{M}
, the second one consists of
w_{M+1},\ldots,w_{2M}
, etc. The last part can
have fewer than
M
vertices. Suppose that we delete the edges of the cycle whose endpoints lie between different parts.

We let
T_{i}(B)
be the connected component in
B
that consists of the
vertices of the cycle in part
i
, i.e., the vertices
w_{(i-1)M},\ldots,w_{iM}
.
Note that the vertices in
T_{i}(B)
induce a subgraph of
B
which is tree.

Let
{\mathcal{T}}={\mathcal{T}}({\mathcal{B}})
be the collection of the following trees:

(a)

for every multi-vertex block
B\in{\mathcal{B}}
that is tree,
{\mathcal{T}}
includes the
tree
B\cup\partial_{\rm out}B
,

(b)

for every unicyclic block
B\in{\mathcal{B}}
,
{\mathcal{T}}
includes every tree
T_{i}(B)
.

For each tree of the type (a) above, the root is assumed to be a (any) heavy vertex
w
such that
{\Uptheta}(w)>1-\varepsilon/2
.
For each tree of type (b), i.e., for each
T_{i}(B)
, the root is assumed to be a vertex
w
in the short cycle (any vertex).

To avoid repetitions, we use the following convention for the rest of the proof: the Glauber dynamics, or the block dynamics on a subgraph of
\mathbold{G}
,
is always assumed to be with respect to the marginal of the Gibbs distribution
\mu_{\UpJ,\beta,\uph}
at that subgraph. When necessary,
we also impose boundary conditions at the subgraph’s boundary.

The following result provides a bound on the relaxation time of tree
T\in{\mathcal{T}}
by utilising a recursive argument.

Theorem 14.2
.

For a tree
T\in{\mathcal{T}}
, rooted at vertex
v
, and a fixed configuration
\sigma

at
\partial_{\rm out}T
, let
\uptau_{\rm rel}=\uptau_{\rm rel}(T,\sigma)
be the relaxation time of
Glauber dynamics on
T
. We have

\displaystyle\uptau_{\rm rel}

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(m(T,v)\right)\kern 5.0pt,

where
m(T,v)=\max_{P}\{{\Upupsilon}(P)\}
and the maximum is over all root-to-leaf paths
P
in
T
.

The proof of Theorem
14.2
is standard and has appeared in several works
in the literature, e.g., see
[
27
, Theorem 16.1]
for a proof.

For every block
B\in{\mathcal{B}}
which is tree rooted at vertex
v
, Theorem
14.2
implies

(14.1)

\displaystyle\uptau_{B}\leq\exp\left(m(B,v)\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Recall that
m(B,v)=\max\{{\Upupsilon}(P)\}
and the maximum is over all the paths

P
in
B
from the root
v
to the leaves.
The proof of
Theorem

4.3
(and in particular
Theorem

18.7
in this proof)
implies for the block partition
{\mathcal{B}}
that all paths considered
for
m(B,v)
are of length at most
2\cdot\frac{\log n}{d^{(1/2)+10^{-2}}}
.

For what follows, let
\upz=d^{-1/100}
. On the event
{\mathcal{E}}
, in particular due to (
2
), we have

m(B,v)\leq 2\cdot{\upz\cdot\log n}
.
Plugging this bound into (
14.1
) we get

(14.2)

\displaystyle\uptau_{B}

\displaystyle\leq n^{2\cdot\upz}\kern 5.0pt.

This establishes the desired bound for
\uptau_{B}
for the case where
B\in{\mathcal{B}}
is a tree.

For the case where
B
is a unicyclic graph, we prove the following result.

Lemma 14.3
.

For the case where
B\in{\mathcal{B}}
is unicyclic we have that
\uptau_{B}\leq n^{\frac{24}{\sqrt{\log d}}}
.

The proof of
Lemma

14.3
is in
Section

14.1
.

Theorem

13.2
follows from (
14.2
),
Lemma

14.3
.

\Box

14.1.
Proof of
Lemma

14.3

Consider the unicyclic block
B
.
As per standard notation, we let
C=(w_{1},\ldots,w_{\ell})
be the cycle inside
B
, for some
\ell\leq 4\frac{\log n}{\log^{4}d}
.

Consider the block dynamics on
B
with a fixed boundary
\sigma
at
\partial_{\rm out}B
.
The blocks in the dynamics correspond to the components
T_{i}(B)
we define above.
Let
\uptau_{\rm block}
be the relaxation time of this block dynamics
with an arbitrary boundary condition.

Proposition

14.1
and
Theorem

14.2
relate
\uptau_{\rm block}
with

\uptau_{B}
through the following inequality:

(14.3)

\displaystyle\uptau_{B}\leq\uptau_{\rm block}\cdot\max_{i}\exp\left(m(T_{i}(B),v_{i})\right)\kern 5.0pt,

where
v_{i}
is the root of
T_{i}(B)
and
weight
m(T_{i}(B),v_{i})
is defined in the statement of
Theorem

14.2
.

Suppose the maximum on the r.h.s. of (
14.3
) is
attained for some number
j
. Then, recall that

(14.4)

\displaystyle m(T_{j}(B),v_{j})

\displaystyle=\max\nolimits_{\widehat{P}}\{{\Upupsilon}(\widehat{P})\}\kern 5.0pt,

and the maximum is over all root-to-leaf paths
\widehat{P}
in
T_{j}(B)
.

Let
P
be the path that maximizes the r.h.s. of (
14.4
).
In light of (
14.5
), (
14.3
)
and (
14.4
),

Lemma

14.3
follows by showing that

(14.5)

\displaystyle\uptau_{\rm block}

\displaystyle\leq n^{\frac{1}{2\sqrt{d}}}\kern 5.0pt.

and, for any block
T_{j}(B)
and any root-to-leaf path
{P}
, we have

(14.6)

\displaystyle\Upupsilon(P)

\displaystyle\leq 22(\log n)/\sqrt{\log d}\kern 5.0pt.

Firstly we focus on proving (
14.6
).
Note that for any vertex
w\in T_{j}(B)
and for any path
Q
that connect
w

to the leaves of
T_{j}(B)
without using any edges that belong to
cycle
C
we have
|Q|\leq 2\frac{\log n}{d^{(1/2)+10^{-2}}}
.

Path
P
may be longer than
2\cdot\frac{\log n}{d^{(1/2)+10^{-2}}}
, as it
can traverse many edges of the cycle inside the component
T_{i}(B)

before reaching a leaf. For such a path, we do not have any guarantees
on
\Upupsilon(P)
. Recall that our assumptions imply that for every path
Q
such that
|Q|\leq 2\frac{\log n}{d^{(1/2)+10^{-2}}}
,
we have
\Upupsilon(Q)\leq 2\cdot\upz\cdot\log n
, where
\upz=d^{-1/100}
.

If
P
uses edges of the short cycle, then we can partition
P
into two subpaths
P_{A}
and
P_{B}
,
in the sense that
P=P_{A},P_{B}
. All the edges in
P_{A}
are between vertices of cycle
C
, while no
edges in
P_{B}
belong to
C
. From the construction of
T_{j}(B)
we have

|P_{A}|\leq M=\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{\log d}}
, while
|P_{B}|\leq 2\frac{\log n}{d^{(1/2)+10^{-2}}}
.

Furthermore, we have

(14.7)
