\displaystyle\Upupsilon(P)\leq\Upupsilon(P_{A})+\Upupsilon(P_{B})+1\kern 5.0pt.

Since we assume that the maximum entry in matrix
\UpJ
is at most
10\sqrt{\log n}
, and

|P_{A}|\leq\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{\log d}}
, a simple calculation implies that

(14.8)

\displaystyle\Upupsilon(P_{A})

\displaystyle\leq 20(\log n)/\sqrt{\log d}

and

\displaystyle\Upupsilon(P_{B})

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot\upz\cdot\log n

where the bound on
\Upupsilon(P_{B})
follows from property
2
of the event
{\mathcal{E}}
.
We obtain (
14.6
) by plugging the above bounds
into (
14.7
).

We now show that (
14.5
) is true.
Let
(X_{t})
and
(Y_{t})
be two copies of the block dynamics
with the same boundary condition at
\sigma
at
\partial_{\rm out}T
.
We couple the two chains maximally such that at each transition we update the same block in both of them. Also, note that the coupling is such that, if for some
t_{0}
we have
X_{t_{0}}=Y_{t_{0}}
,
then for any
t>t_{0}
, we also have
X_{t}=Y_{t}
.

The number of blocks is
\lceil\frac{\ell}{M}\rceil<5\frac{\sqrt{\log n}}{(\log d)^{7/8}}
. We consider epochs each of length
N=\sqrt{\log n}
. We argue that at the end of each epoch the chains in the pair are at the same configuration with probability at least
n^{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}}
, i.e., we have that

(14.9)

\displaystyle\min_{X_{0},Y_{0}}\Pr[X_{N}=Y_{N}\ |\ X_{0},Y_{0}]

\displaystyle\geq n^{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}}\kern 5.0pt.

Then, it is elementary to show that after
50n^{\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}}
many epochs, the probability the two chains agree is
>0.8
.
Clearly, this implies
\uptau_{\rm block}\leq n^{\frac{1}{2\sqrt{d}}}
, establishing (
14.5
).

Suppose that at time
t>0
, we update block
\widehat{B}
in the block dynamics.
Recall that the coupling updates the same block in the two copies. Then we use the following result.

Claim 14.4
.

There is a coupling such that

\displaystyle\Pr[X_{t}(\widehat{B})=Y_{t}(\widehat{B})\ |\ {\mathcal{F}}_{2}]

\displaystyle\geq\exp\left(-\sqrt{(\log n)/d}\right)\kern 5.0pt,

where
{\mathcal{F}}_{2}
is the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the configurations of
X_{t}
,
Y_{t}

at
B\setminus\widehat{B}
.

Let
\mathcal{U}_{\rm all}
be the event that during the first epoch all blocks are updated at least once. For
L=\lceil\ell/M\rceil
and
0<i\leq L
, let
\mathcal{G}_{i}
be the event that
the last time block
\widehat{B}=T_{i}(B)
was updated during the epoch, we had
X_{t}(\widehat{B})=Y_{t}(\widehat{B})
.

For any
X_{0},Y_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr[X_{N}=Y_{N}\ |\ X_{0},Y_{0}]

\displaystyle\geq\Pr[\mathcal{U}_{\rm all},\ \cap_{0<i\leq L}\mathcal{G}_{i}\ |\ X_{0},Y_{0}]

\displaystyle=\Pr[\mathcal{U}_{\rm all}\ |\ X_{0},Y_{0}]\cdot\prod\nolimits_{0<j\leq L}\Pr[\cap_{0<i\leq L}\mathcal{G}_{i}\ |\ X_{0},Y_{0},\ \mathcal{U}_{\rm all},\ \cap_{0<j<i}\mathcal{G}_{j}]\kern 5.0pt.

A simple coupon collector argument implies that
\Pr[\mathcal{U}_{\rm all}\ |\ X_{0},Y_{0}]\geq 0.99
.
Plugging this bound on
\Pr[\mathcal{U}_{\rm all}\ |\ X_{0},Y_{0}]
and
the one
14.4
into the above inequality, we get

(14.10)

\displaystyle\Pr[X_{N}=Y_{N}\ |\ X_{0},Y_{0}]

\displaystyle\geq 0.99\cdot\exp\left(L\cdot\sqrt{(\log n)/d}\right)\ \geq\ n^{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}}\kern 5.0pt,

where the last inequality follows by using the elementary bound

L\leq 5\frac{\sqrt{\log n}}{(\log d)^{7/8}}
.

All the above conclude the proof of
Lemma

14.3
.

\Box

Proof of Claim
14.4
.

W.l.o.g., assume that
\widehat{B}
corresponds to
T_{1}(B)
.
Then, the vertices of the block that can have disagreeing neighbours outside the block
are
w_{1}
and
w_{M}
. Specifically, vertex
w_{1}
has neighbour
w_{\ell}
and
w_{M}
has neighbour

w_{M+1}
.

The probability
\Pr[X_{t}(\widehat{B})=Y_{t}(\widehat{B})\ |\ {\mathcal{F}}_{2}]
is minimised by
having at least one disagreement at
w_{\ell}
and
w_{M+1}
, i.e., otherwise this
probability is
1
.

The coupling we use is as follows: First, we couple maximally vertex
w_{1}
and then, given the
outcome for
w_{1}
, we couple maximally vertex
w_{M}
. We have

(14.11)

\displaystyle\Pr[X_{t}(\widehat{B})=Y_{t}(\widehat{B})\mid{\mathcal{F}}_{2}]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\Pr[X_{t}(w_{M})=Y_{t}(w_{M})\mid X_{t}(w_{1})=Y_{t}(w_{1}),\ {\mathcal{F}}_{2}]\cdot\Pr[X_{t}(w_{1})=Y_{t}(w_{1})\mid{\mathcal{F}}_{2}]\kern 5.0pt.

We start by focusing on the coupling for
w_{1}
. We have that

(14.12)

\displaystyle\Pr[X_{t}(w_{1})=Y_{t}(w_{1})\mid{\mathcal{F}}_{2}]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\min_{\eta^{+},\eta^{-}}\Pr[X_{t}(w_{1})=Y_{t}(w_{1})\ |\ X_{t}(\{w_{\ell},w_{M+1}\})=\eta^{+},\ Y_{t}(\{w_{\ell},w_{M+1}\})=\eta^{-},{\mathcal{F}}_{2}]\kern 5.0pt.

To see the reason why the above is true, note that the configuration at vertices
w_{\ell}

and
w_{M+1}
affects the marginal at
w_{1}
from two directions.
The first one is over the edge
\{w_{1},w_{\ell}\}
, the second one is over the path
(w_{1},\ldots,w_{M},w_{M+1})
.

Then, it is standard to show that

\displaystyle\Pr[X_{t}(w_{1})=Y_{t}(w_{1})\ |\ {\mathcal{F}}_{2}]

\displaystyle\geq\exp(-\beta(|\UpJ_{e}|+|\UpJ_{e^{\prime}}|))\kern 5.0pt,

where recall that
\UpJ_{e}
and
\UpJ_{e^{\prime}}
are the coupling parameters for the edge that connects
w_{1}
to
w_{\ell}
and
w_{M}
to
w_{M+1}
, respectively.
Since we have that
\UpJ_{e},\UpJ_{e^{\prime}}\leq 10\sqrt{\log n}
, the above inequality implies

\displaystyle\Pr[X_{t}(w_{1})=Y_{t}(w_{1})\ |\ {\mathcal{F}}_{2}]

\displaystyle\geq\exp\left(-\sqrt{(\log n)/d}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Using similar arguments as above, we obtain

\displaystyle\Pr[X_{t}(w_{\ell})=Y_{t}(w_{\ell})\ |\ X_{t}(w_{1})=Y_{t}(w_{1}),\ {\mathcal{F}}_{2}]

\displaystyle\geq\exp\left(-\sqrt{(\log n)/d}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

The claim follows by plugging the above two inequalities into (
14.11
).
∎

15.
Proof of
Theorem

4.3

To prove
Theorem

4.3
, we firstly introduce a new notion of partition
of the set of vertices of
\mathbold{G}=\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
. We call it
(\varepsilon,r)
-decomposition.
This notion is slightly different from what we describe in
Definition

4.2
.
The new decomposition is a stepping stone towards establishing the existence of the

\varepsilon
-block partition.

Consider distribution
\mu_{\UpJ,\beta,\uph}
on
\mathbold{G}=\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)

as defined in (
4.1
) and (
4.2
)
for
\beta
and
\uph
as specified in
Theorem

4.3
.
Given parameter
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, consider the
\varepsilon
-weights
as defined in (
4.6
) and (
4.7
) with respect to
the distribution
\mu_{\UpJ,\beta,\uph}
.

For integer
k\geq 0
, we introduce the notion of
(\varepsilon,k)
-block vertex.
Specifically, every
u\in V_{n}
is called
(\varepsilon,k)
-block vertex if the following holds:

(1)

every path
P
of length
\leq\log n
emanating from
u
,
satisfies
\UpM(P)<1
,

(2)

for every edge
e
within distance
k
from
u
, we have
{\rm\Upgamma}_{e}\leq d^{-1/10}
,

(3)

for every vertex
v
within distance
k+1
from
u
, we have

\sum_{w\sim v}(\UpJ_{v,w})^{2}\leq(1+\varepsilon)d
.

The above generalises the notion of
\varepsilon
-block vertex,
as this corresponds to the
(\varepsilon,k)
-block vertex for
k=0
.

Definition 15.1

(
(\varepsilon,k)
-decomposition)
.

For parameters
\varepsilon,k>0
, consider the
\varepsilon
-weights defined in (
4.6
) and (
4.7
).
The partition
\mathcal{B}=\{B_{1},\ldots,B_{N}\}
of the vertex set
V_{n}

is called
(\varepsilon,k)
-decomposition if for every
B\in{\mathcal{B}}
the following is true:

(1)

the vertices in block
B
induce in
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
a tree with at most one extra edge,

(2)

if
B
is a multi-vertex block, the following holds:

(a)

every
w\in\partial_{\rm out}B
is a
(\varepsilon,k)
-block vertex with
exactly one neighbour in
B
,

(b)

if
B
contains a cycle, this is a short one, i.e., its length is

\leq 4\frac{\log n}{(\log d)^{4}}
,

(c)

the distance of the short cycle in
B
from
\partial_{\rm out}B
is

\geq(150/\varepsilon)\cdot\log\log n
.

(3)

if
B=\{u\}
is a single-vertex block, then
u
is an
(\varepsilon,k)
-block vertex.

An important difference between the above and
Definition

4.2
is that it uses the

(\varepsilon,k)
-block vertices. Furthermore, condition 2.(a) requires the block-vertices
to be in
\partial_{\rm out}B
of every multi-vertex block
B
. For the
\varepsilon
-block
partition, condition 2.(a) requires the block-vertices to be in
\partial_{\rm in}B
.

Theorem 15.2
.

For any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, integer
0\leq k\leq 100
,
\uph\in\mathbb{R}^{V_{n}}
, there exists
d_{0}=d_{0}(\varepsilon)
such that for any

d\geq d_{0}
and
\beta<\beta_{c}(d)
, the following is true:

Let the Gibbs distribution
\mu_{\UpJ,\beta,\uph}
, specified by
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
and interaction matrix
\UpJ
defined as
in (
4.1
). Then, with probability
1-o(1)
over the instance of

\mu_{\UpJ,\beta,\uph}
, the vertex set
V_{n}
admits an
(\varepsilon,k)
-decomposition.

The proof of
Theorem

15.2
appears in
Section

16
.

Let
\varepsilon,\upzeta\in(0,1)
be such that
\upzeta=\varepsilon\cdot(1-10^{-4})<1
.
Let
{\mathcal{D}}
be a
(\varepsilon,0)
-decomposition and
{\mathcal{E}}
be
(\upzeta,100)
-decomposition.
Note that
Theorem

15.2
implies that we typically have both
{\mathcal{D}}
and
{\mathcal{E}}
.

We show (later in
Proposition

15.3
) that there is a natural coupling such
that for every multi-vertex block
A\in{\mathcal{D}}
, there exists a multi-vertex block
B\in{\mathcal{E}}

such that
A\subset B
. Note that the containment is strict. Furthermore,
the distance between
\partial_{\rm out}A
and
\partial_{\rm out}B
is at least 90.

Theorem

4.3
follows by showing that we can construct an
\varepsilon
-block
partition
{\mathcal{B}}
by using the decompositions
{\mathcal{D}}
and
{\mathcal{E}}
.
Specifically, we show a construction for
{\mathcal{B}}
such that each multi-vertex block
K\in{\mathcal{B}}
is obtained
as a sub-block of a multi-vertex block
B\in{\mathcal{E}}
, i.e.,
K\subseteq B
.

Fix
B\in{\mathcal{E}}
and let
\Upgamma_{B}
be the set of multi-vertex blocks

A\in{\mathcal{D}}
such that
A\subset B
. Also, let set
\UpQ_{B}
contain all
vertices in
B
which are within distance
90
from
\partial_{\rm out}B
.

Let
L
be the union of the vertex sets induced by the blocks in
\Upgamma_{B}
.
Then, iteratively, set

(15.1)

\displaystyle L\leftarrow L\cup(\partial_{\rm out}L\setminus\UpQ_{B})\kern 5.0pt.

When no more vertices can be added into
L
, i.e.,

\partial_{\rm out}L\setminus\UpQ_{B}=\emptyset
, we set

(15.2)

\displaystyle K=L\cup\partial_{\rm out}L\kern 5.0pt.

Repeating the above steps for all multi-vertex blocks in
{\mathcal{E}}
, we obtain the multi-vertex
blocks in
{\mathcal{B}}
. The vertices in
V_{n}
which do not belong to any of the multi-vertex
blocks in
{\mathcal{B}}
are the single-vertex blocks of
{\mathcal{B}}
.

Proposition 15.3
.

In the above construction,
{\mathcal{B}}
is an
\varepsilon
-block partition.

Theorem

4.3
follows as a corollary from
Theorem

15.2
and
Proposition

15.3
.

\Box

15.1.
Proof of
Proposition

15.3

Recall that
{\mathcal{D}}
is an
(\varepsilon,0)
-decomposition, while
{\mathcal{E}}

is an
(\upzeta,100)
-decomposition, where
\upzeta=\varepsilon(1-10^{-4})<1
.

Firstly, we prove that we can have
{\mathcal{D}}
and
{\mathcal{E}}
such that for every multi-vertex block
B\in{\mathcal{E}}
and

A\in{\mathcal{D}}
satisfying
A\cap B\neq\emptyset
we have

C.1:

we have
A\subset B
,

C.2:

the distance between
\partial_{\rm out}A
and
\partial_{\rm out}B
is at least 90.

Consider the weights of the paths induced by the
\varepsilon
-weights and
\upzeta
-weights, respectively.
It is not hard to verify that for every path
P
, we have
\UpM_{\varepsilon}(P)\leq\UpM_{\upzeta}(P)
.
This observation implies that every
(\upzeta,100)
-block vertex is also an
(\varepsilon,0)
-block vertex.

For the multi-vertex block
B\in{\mathcal{E}}
, recall that
\UpQ_{B}
is the set of
vertices inside
B
such that
{\rm dist}(\UpQ_{B},\partial_{\rm out}B)\leq 90
.

Claim 15.4
.

For any multi-vertex block
B\in{\mathcal{E}}
, all vertices in
\UpQ_{B}
are
(\varepsilon,0)
-block vertices.

For tree-like block
B\in{\mathcal{E}}
, consider each vertex
u\in B\setminus\UpQ_{B}
, which is not an
(\varepsilon,0)
-block vertex.

15.4
implies that for any path from
u
to
\partial_{\rm out}B
,
there is an
(\varepsilon,0)
-block vertex which is at distance
>90
from
\partial_{\rm out}B
.
Hence, letting
A\in{\mathcal{D}}
be the multi-vertex block that
u
belongs to, it is easy to check that

A
and
B
satisfy
C.1
and
{\bf C.2}
.

Assume now that
B\in{\mathcal{E}}
contains the short cycle
C
.
Let vertex
u\in B\setminus\UpQ_{B}
which is not an
(\varepsilon,0)
-block vertex and does not
belong to
C
. Also, let block
A\in{\mathcal{D}}
be such that
u\in A
.
If block
A
is tree-like, then, using similar arguments as those in the previous paragraph,
we see that
C.1
and
{\bf C.2}
are satisfied for
A
and
B
.

We additionally need to ensure that
C.1
and
{\bf C.2}
are satisfied
when the short cycle
C
in block
B\in{\mathcal{E}}
belongs to block
A\in{\mathcal{D}}
.
In this case, it might be that
{\bf C.1}
and
{\bf C.2}
are not satisfied because
of the extra constraint we have for the distance of
C
from the boundary of
the corresponding block.
That is,
C
is at distance
\geq\frac{150}{\upzeta}\log\log n
from
\partial_{\rm out}B

and at distance
\geq\frac{150}{\varepsilon}\log\log n
form
\partial_{\rm out}A
.
This means that
\partial_{\rm out}A
might need to use vertices
that are further away than those in
\UpQ_{B}
.

A simple calculation reveals that
{\rm dist}{\UpQ_{B},C}\gg(150/\varepsilon)\cdot\log\log n
. To see this, note that
\partial_{\rm in}B
is at
distance
\frac{150}{\upzeta}\log\log n
from
C
. Then, the distance between any

w\in\UpQ_{B}
and cycle
C
is at least

(15.3)

\displaystyle\frac{150}{\upzeta}\log\log n-90>\frac{150}{\varepsilon}\log\log n\kern 5.0pt.

The inequality (
15.3
) holds for sufficiently large
n
.

From all the above, it is not hard to deduce that in every path from cycle
C
to

\partial_{\rm out}B
, there is at least one
(\varepsilon,0)
-block vertex which is
at distance
>90
from
\partial_{\rm out}B
and at distance
\geq(150/\varepsilon)\cdot\log\log n

from
C
. Hence, block
A
that contains cycle
C
and block
B
satisfy
{\bf C.1}
and
{\bf C.2}
.

Hence,
{\bf C.1}
and
{\bf C.2}
always hold for
{\mathcal{D}}
and
{\mathcal{E}}
. We now show that
{\mathcal{B}}
, constructed
as we described above, is an
\varepsilon
-block partition.

The description of how we obtain
{\mathcal{B}}
from
{\mathcal{E}}
and
{\mathcal{D}}
makes it clear
that
{\mathcal{B}}
specifies a partition of the set of vertices in
V_{n}
with parts the blocks of
{\mathcal{B}}
.
It remains to show that
{\mathcal{B}}
satisfies all constraints in
Definition

4.2
.

Fix multi-vertex blocks
K\in{\mathcal{B}}
and
B\in{\mathcal{E}}
, such that
K
is obtained from
B

in the process described in (
15.1
) and (
15.2
).
It follows from the process that we always have
K\subseteq B
.
Hence,
K
can only be a tree with an extra edge, i.e., like the blocks in
{\mathcal{E}}
.
This implies that
{\mathcal{B}}
satisfies conditions 1 and 2.b in
Definition

4.2
.

From the construction of
K
, i.e., (
15.2
), we have

\partial_{\rm in}K\subseteq\UpQ_{B}
.
Then, by
15.4
, we have that
\partial_{\rm in}K

are
(\varepsilon,0)
-block vertices, which is equivalent to
saying that they are
\varepsilon
-block vertices.
Furthermore, it is not hard to verify that at step (
15.2
),
all vertices in
\partial_{\rm out}L
have exactly one neighbour in
L
.
But then, since
\partial_{\rm in}K=\partial_{\rm out}L
, we have that
the number of neighbours of each vertex
u\in\partial_{\rm in}K
inside
block
K
is 1.
We conclude that
{\mathcal{B}}
satisfies condition 2.a in
Definition

4.2
.

As argued in the previous paragraph, we have
\partial_{\rm in}K\subseteq\UpQ_{B}
.
If
K
has a short cycle
C
, then the distance of
C
from
\partial_{\rm in}K
is
the same as that from
C
to
\UpQ_{B}
. But then, we have shown in (
15.3
)
that this distance is
\gg\frac{150}{\varepsilon}\log\log n
. This implies that
2.c in
Definition

4.2
is also true for
{\mathcal{B}}
.

Finally, note that every vertex
u\in V_{n}
, which is a single-vertex block in
{\mathcal{B}}
,
either comes from a single-vertex block in
{\mathcal{E}}
or it comes from a multi-vertex block
B\in{\mathcal{E}}
.
In the first case, we have that
u
is an
(\upzeta,100)
-block vertex
which implies that it is an
(\varepsilon,0)
-block vertex, too.
In the second case, we have that
u\in\UpQ_{B}
. But according to

15.4
,
u
is an
(\varepsilon,0)
-block vertex.
We conclude that each single-vertex block in
{\mathcal{B}}
consists of an
(\varepsilon,0)
-block vertex,
which is equivalent to saying it consists of an
\varepsilon
-block vertex.
Hence,
{\mathcal{B}}
satisfies condition 3 in
Definition

4.2
.

This concludes the proof of
Proposition

15.3
.
\Box

Proof of
15.4
.

Fix a multi-vertex block
B\in{\mathcal{E}}
and consider the set
\UpQ_{B}
of the vertices inside
B

such that
{\rm dist}(\UpQ_{B},\partial_{\rm out}B)\leq 90
.
We show that any vertex
w\in\UpQ_{B}
is an
(\varepsilon,0)
-block vertex.

Since every vertex in
\partial_{\rm out}B
is a
(\upzeta,100)
-block vertex,
then each
w\in\UpQ_{B}
is such that

(1)

for every edge
e
, incident to vertex
w
, we have
{\rm\Upgamma}_{e}\leq d^{-1/10}
,

(2)

we have
\sum_{z\sim w}(\UpJ_{z,w})^{2}\leq(1+\varepsilon)d
.

Hence, it suffices to prove that for any path
P
inside
B
that connects

w\in\UpQ_{B}
with a vertex
u\in B
which is not
(\varepsilon,0)
-block vertex,
we have
\UpM_{\varepsilon}(P)<1
.

Letting
H
be the set of heavy vertices in path
P
, we have

\displaystyle\UpM_{\varepsilon}(P)

\displaystyle=\prod\nolimits_{v\in P}\UpM_{\varepsilon}(v)\ \leq\ (1-\varepsilon/4)^{|P|-|H|}\cdot\prod\nolimits_{v\in H}\UpM_{\varepsilon}(v)

(15.4)

\displaystyle\leq(1-\varepsilon/4)^{|P|-|H|}\cdot d^{|H|}\cdot\prod\nolimits_{v\in H}{\Uptheta}(v)\kern 5.0pt.

Since
w\in\UpQ_{B}
, while
\partial_{\rm out}B
consists of
(\upzeta,100)
-block vertices, we have

(15.5)

\displaystyle\prod\nolimits_{x\in H}{\Uptheta}(x)\leq d^{-|H|}\cdot(1-\upzeta/4)^{|H|-|P|-90}\kern 5.0pt.

Therefore, plugging (
15.5
) into (
15.4
) gives

(15.6)

\displaystyle\UpM_{\varepsilon}(P)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{1-\varepsilon/4}{1-\upzeta/4}\right)^{|P|-|H|}\cdot(1-\upzeta/4)^{-90}\leq\left(1-10^{4}\cdot\frac{\upzeta}{4-\upzeta}\right)^{|P|-|H|}\cdot(1-\upzeta/4)^{-90}\kern 5.0pt,

where the second inequality follows because we chose
\upzeta
such that

\upzeta=\varepsilon(1-10^{-4})
.

Notice that
(1-\upzeta)^{-90}
is a fixed number independent of
d
, while

|P|-|H|\geq\frac{1}{2\upzeta}\log d
. Hence, for any fixed number
\varrho<1
, we have
\UpM_{\varepsilon}(P)<\varrho
, by choosing sufficiently large
d
.
All the above imply that for sufficiently large
d
,
w
is an
(\varepsilon,0)
-block vertex.
This concludes the proof of
15.4
.
∎

16.
Proof of
Theorem

15.2

16.1.
Existence of Decomposition

Let
\varepsilon,k,d
and
\beta
be as specified in
Theorem

15.2
. Also, let
\mathbold{G}=\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
,
the collection of the i.i.d. standard gaussian random variables
\{\mathbold{\gamma}_{e}\}_{e\in E(\mathbold{G})}
and the interaction
matrix
\UpJ
obtained as described in (
4.1
)
with respect to
\mathbold{G}
and
\{\mathbold{\gamma}_{e}\}
.

To prove
Theorem

15.2
we describe an algorithm that generates an
(\varepsilon,k)
-decomposition for typical instances of
\UpJ
.
To be more precise, the output of the algorithm is either the
(\varepsilon,k)
-decomposition
\mathcal{B}=\{B_{1},\ldots,B_{N}\}
or the status “fail”.
The algorithm returns “fail” if it cannot find the desired block decomposition of
V_{n}
.

Recall that a cycle
C
of
\mathbold{G}
is called “short” if its length is at most

4\frac{\log n}{(\log d)^{4}}
. We let
{\mathcal{C}}
be the set
of short cycles in
\mathbold{G}
.
Firstly, the algorithm finds set
{\mathcal{C}}
and checks the validity of
16.1
.

Condition 16.1
.

The distance of any two cycles in
{\mathcal{C}}
is at least
\textstyle 2\frac{\log n}{(\log d)^{2}}
.

If
16.1
is false, then the algorithm terminates and outputs “fail”.

Otherwise, i.e.,
16.1
is true, the algorithm computes the set of
(\varepsilon,k)
-block vertices. With the set of these vertices available, we proceed with the creation of multi-vertex blocks in
{\mathcal{B}}
.
We start with the unicyclic blocks.
To this end, we need the following condition to be true:

Condition 16.2
.

For every
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
, every path
P
of length
\geq 2\cdot\frac{\log n}{d^{(1/2)+10^{-2}}}
that does not intersect with
C
contains
at least one
(\varepsilon,k)
-block vertex
u
such that
{\rm dist}(C,u)\geq(150/\varepsilon)\cdot\log\log n
.

If
16.2
is false, then the algorithm terminates and outputs “fail”.

Given that
16.2
is true, for each cycle
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
, block
B_{C}
is specified
as follows: Let
{\mathcal{Q}}_{C}
be the set of vertices that are distance

\geq(150/\varepsilon)\cdot\log\log n
from
C
. Then,
B_{C}
consists of each vertex

V_{n}\setminus{\mathcal{Q}}_{C}
and every vertex in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{C}
that is reachable by
C

through a path
P
such that
P\cap{\mathcal{Q}}_{C}
does not contain an
(\varepsilon,k)
-block.
Note that we have

(16.1)

\displaystyle B_{C}\subseteq N\left(C,\textstyle{2\cdot\frac{\log n}{d^{(1/2)+10^{-2}}}}\right),\;\text{ for all }C\in{\mathcal{C}}\kern 5.0pt,

where
N(S,r)
is the set of vertices which is within graph distance
r
from set
S
.

The above and
16.1
,
16.2
imply that, taking large
d
, there are no
two cycles
C,C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}
such that their corresponding blocks
B_{C}
and
B_{C^{\prime}}
intersect.
This concludes the construction of the unicyclic blocks in
{\mathcal{B}}
and we proceed with
the tree-like ones.

Let
U
be the set that consists of each vertex
u
which is not
contained in any unicyclic block, while
u
is not an
(\varepsilon,k)
-block vertex.

Condition 16.3
.

For every
u\in U
, and for every vertex
w
at distance
\geq 4\cdot\frac{\log n}{d^{(1/2)+10^{-2}}}
from
u
, every path that connects
u
to

w
contains at least one
(\varepsilon,k)
-block vertex.

If
16.3
is false, then the algorithm terminates and outputs “fail”.

Otherwise, i.e., if
16.3
is true, for each vertex
u\in U
whose block has not been specified yet, the algorithm creates the block
B_{u}
that consists of
u
, and
all vertices
w
that are reachable from
u
through a path
(u=v_{0},\ldots,v_{\ell}=w)
such that none of the
v_{i}
’s is an
(\varepsilon,k)
-block vertex.

Due to
16.3
, we have

(16.2)

\displaystyle B_{u}\subseteq N\left(u,\textstyle{{4}\cdot\frac{\log n}{d^{(1/2)+10^{-2}}}}\right),\;\text{ for all }u\in U\kern 5.0pt.

All the above concludes the construction of the multi-vertex blocks in
{\mathcal{B}}
. However,
there may still be vertices
w
whose block
B_{w}
is not specified.
For every such vertex
w
, we let
B_{w}=\{w\}
.

We use the following theorem to argue that Conditions
16.1
,
16.2
and
16.3
are typically true.

Theorem 16.4
.

For any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
0\leq k\leq 100
,
\uph\in\mathbb{R}^{V_{n}}
, there exists
d_{0}=d_{0}(\varepsilon)
such that for any

d\geq d_{0}
and
\beta<\beta_{c}(d)
, the following is true:

Let the Gibbs distribution
\mu_{\UpJ,\beta,\uph}
, specified by
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
and interaction matrix
\UpJ
defined
as in (
4.1
). Also, let

{\mathcal{E}}_{\rm All}
be the event that “Conditions
16.1
,
16.2
and
16.3
are true”. We have

\displaystyle\Pr[{\mathcal{E}}_{\rm All}]\geq 1-n^{-{2/3}}\kern 5.0pt,

where the probability term is with respect to the instances of
\mu_{\UpJ,\beta,\uph}
.

The proof of
Theorem

16.4
appears in
Section

17
.

We use
Theorem

16.4
, to show is that when the
algorithm does not fail
{\mathcal{B}}
is an
(\varepsilon,k)
-block decomposition.

By construction all single-vertex blocks must be
(\varepsilon,k)
-block vertex, i.e.
since all non-block vertices are considered in the first and second stages of the algorithm.
Hence,
{\mathcal{B}}
satisfies condition 3 in
Definition

15.1
.

If
B
is a multi-vertex block then, by construction, every vertex of
\partial_{\rm out}B

must be an
(\varepsilon,k)
-block vertex.

Moreover,
B\cup\partial_{\rm out}B
should contain the same number of cycles
as
B
does, implying that every vertex in
\partial_{\rm out}B
has exactly one neighbour in

B
.
To see why this true, for the sake of contradiction assume the opposite.
That is, assume that there exists a multi-vertex block
B
and
z\in\partial_{\rm out}B
such that
z
has two
neighbours in
B
. Then, since our blocks are low diameter, our assumption implies that there is a short
cycle in the vertices induced by
B\cup\partial_{\rm out}B
that contains
z
.
This is a contradiction. If
B
is unicyclic, then the existence of this additional short cycle violates

16.1
. On the other hand, if
B
is a tree, then
this short cycle must have been considered at the first stage of the algorithm, so it cannot emerge at the second stage of the algorithm. Hence,
{\mathcal{B}}
satisfies condition (
2a
)
in
Definition

15.1
.

Note that above argument implies also that every block created in the first stage of the algorithm must be unicylic, while every block created in the second stage must be a tree. Therefore, we see that
{\mathcal{B}}

satisfies conditions (
1
) and (
2b
) in
Definition

15.1
.

Also, from the construction of the unicyclic block of
{\mathcal{B}}
, it is easy to verify
that (
2c
) is true.

We now observe that no two blocks intersect. In particular, unicyclic blocks do not intersect
with each other due to
16.1
and (
16.1
).
The vertices in
U
do not belong to any of the unicyclic blocks.
Any two multi-vertex blocks
B_{z}
and
B_{x}
cannot intersect with
each other as they are separated by using block vertices at their boundaries.

Hence, we conclude that the set of blocks that is created by the algorithm is a

(\varepsilon,k)
-block decomposition.

17.
Proof of
Theorem

16.4

We start by introducing few notions. For integer
r\geq 0
, a path
P
of
G
is

r
-locally simple
if
N_{r}(P)
, i.e., the set of all vertices within distance
r
from
P
,
induces a tree in
G
. Furthermore, for
\ell\geq 0
, we define the set of paths

(17.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{r,\ell}(G)

\displaystyle=\left\{P\ :\ \text{$P$ is of length $\ell$ and it is $r$-{{locally simple}}}\right\}\kern 5.0pt.

Theorem 17.1
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
and integer
0\leq k\leq 100
there exists
d_{0}=d_{0}(\varepsilon)\geq 1

such that for any
d\geq d_{0}
, and for any
0<\beta\leq\beta_{c}(d)
,
\uph\in\mathbb{R}^{V_{n}}
the
following is true:

Let the Gibbs distribution
\mu_{\UpJ,\beta,\uph}
, specified by
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
and interaction
matrix
\UpJ
defined as in (
4.1
). Then, for
r=\ell=\frac{\log n}{d^{(1/2)+10^{-2}}}
,
let
{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{P}}
be the event that “every path in
{\mathcal{P}}_{r,\ell}(\mathbold{G})
contains an
(\varepsilon,k)
-block
vertex.” We have
\Pr\left[{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{P}}\right]\geq 1-{n}^{-d^{1/8}}
.

The proof of
Theorem

17.1
appears in
Section

17.1
.

Let
{\mathcal{E}}_{S}
be the event that “every set of vertices
S
in
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
with
|S|\leq 2\frac{\log n}{(\log d)^{2}}
, spans at most
|S|
edges.”

Lemma

7.6
implies that
\Pr[{\mathcal{E}}_{S}]\geq 1-n^{-3/4}
.

Then, a simple union bound implies

(17.2)

\displaystyle\Pr[{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{P}}\cap{\mathcal{E}}_{S}]\geq 1-n^{-2/3}\kern 5.0pt.

Theorem

16.4
follows by showing on the events
{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{P}}
and
{\mathcal{E}}_{S}

Conditions
16.1
,
16.2
, and
16.3
are true.

We start by noticing that event
{\mathcal{E}}_{S}
implies
16.1
. For contradiction,
assume that on event
{\mathcal{E}}_{S}
there exist short cycles
C,C^{\prime}
within distance

\frac{\log n}{(\log d)^{2}}
from each other. Let
P
be a path of minimum length connecting
C

to
C^{\prime}
. Then, for sufficiently large
d
, we have

\displaystyle|C\cup C^{\prime}\cup P|\leq\textstyle 2\cdot 4\frac{\log n}{\log^{4}d}+\frac{\log n}{\log^{2}d}<2\frac{\log n}{\log^{2}d}\kern 5.0pt,

but
C\cup C^{\prime}\cup P
induces a connected graph with at least two cycles. Hence, it contains
at least
|C\cup C^{\prime}\cup P|+1
edges. This is a contradiction because we have assumed
{\mathcal{E}}_{S}
.

We now show the following result.

Lemma 17.2
.

On the events
{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{P}}
and
{\mathcal{E}}_{S}
, Conditions
16.2
and
16.3

are true.

Proof.

We start by focusing on
16.2
. Assume that events
{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{P}}
and
{\mathcal{E}}_{S}
occur.

Let
B_{C}
be a unicyclic block created from cycle
C\in{\mathcal{C}}
. Let set
\widehat{C}
consist of the
vertices in
C
and all other vertices which are within distance
(150/\varepsilon)\cdot\log\log n
from
C
.
Also, let
P=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{s})
be a path that emanates from
\widehat{C}
,
while for
i=2,\ldots,s
, vertex
v_{i}
is not in
\hat{C}
and it is not an
(\varepsilon,k)
-block vertex.

Condition
16.2
follows by showing that the events
{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{P}}
and
{\mathcal{E}}_{S}
imply that
|P|<q
, where
q=\frac{3}{2}\cdot\frac{\log n}{d^{(1/2)+10^{-2}}}
.
We equivalently show that if
q\leq|P|\leq\frac{4}{3}q
, then
P
must contain an
(\varepsilon,k)
-block vertex.

Event
{\mathcal{E}}_{S}
implies that path
P
must
contain a sub-path in
{\mathcal{P}}_{r,\ell}(\mathbold{G})
, where recall that
r=\ell=\frac{\log n}{d^{(1/2)+10^{-2}}}
. If no such subpath existed in
P
, then we could find another
short cycle
C^{\prime}
intersecting
P
. But then, this would imply that
cycles
C
and
C^{\prime}
are so close that event
{\mathcal{E}}_{S}
is not true.
Furthermore, event
{\mathcal{E}}_{{\mathcal{P}}}
implies that the sub-path of
P

which belong to
{\mathcal{P}}_{r,\ell}(\mathbold{G})
contains
(\varepsilon,k)
-block
vertex.
Hence events
{\mathcal{E}}_{S}
and
{\mathcal{E}}_{{\mathcal{P}}}
, imply
16.2
.

We proceed with
16.3
.
Let
W
be the set of vertices that do not belong to any of the unicylic blocks in
{\mathcal{B}}
.
Also, let
U\subseteq W
consists of each
u
that it is not an
(\varepsilon,k)
-block vertex.

Let
u\in U
and
w\in W
, while let path
P
be from
u
to
w
such that every vertex of
P
is in set
W
.
The events
{\mathcal{E}}_{S}
and
{\mathcal{E}}_{{\mathcal{P}}}
imply that if
|P|\geq\frac{2q}{3}
, where recall that
q=\frac{3}{2}\cdot\frac{\log n}{d^{(1/2)+10^{-2}}}
,
then
P
contains a sub-path
P^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{r,\ell}(\mathbold{G})
. Moreover,
P^{\prime}
has an
(\varepsilon,k)
-block vertex.
Hence, events
{\mathcal{E}}_{S}
and
{\mathcal{E}}_{{\mathcal{P}}}
imply
16.3
.

Lemma

17.2
follows.
∎

All the above conclude the proof of
Theorem

16.4
.

\Box

17.1.
Proof of
Theorem

17.1

Recall that the path
P
of
\mathbold{G}=\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
is
r
-locally simple if
N_{r}(P)
, i.e., the set of all
vertices within distance
r
from
P
, induces a tree in
\mathbold{G}
. Recall also set

(17.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{r,\ell}(\mathbold{G})=\left\{P\ :\ \text{$P$ is of length $\ell$ and it is $r$-{{locally simple}}}\right\}\kern 5.74997pt.

For what follows, we set
r=\ell=\frac{\log n}{d^{(1/2)+10^{-2}}}
.
Let the event

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}_{A}

\displaystyle=\{\text{every path in ${\mathcal{P}}_{r,\ell}(\mathbold{G})$ contains an $(\varepsilon,k)$-block vertex}\}\kern 5.0pt.

Theorem

17.1
follows by showing
\Pr[{\mathcal{S}}_{A}]\geq 1-n^{-d^{1/8}}
.

For our proof we will also use the following refinement: for
q\leq\log n
, we say that a vertex
u

in
\mathbold{G}
is
(\varepsilon,k)
-
block vertex of range

q
, if the following holds

(1)

every path
P
of length
\leq q
emanating from
u
,
satisfies
\UpM(P)<1
,

(2)

for every edge
e
within distance
k
from
u
, we have
{\rm\Upgamma}_{e}\leq d^{-1/10}
,

(3)

for every vertex
v
within distance
k+1
from
u
, we have

\sum_{w\sim v}(\UpJ_{v,w})^{2}\leq(1+\varepsilon)d
.

Clearly, an
(\varepsilon,k)
-block vertex corresponds to an
(\varepsilon,k)
-block vertex of range
\log n
.

We establish
Theorem

17.1
in two steps.
First, we consider the event

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}_{B}

\displaystyle=\{\text{every path in ${\mathcal{P}}_{r,\ell}(\mathbold{G})$ contains an
$(\varepsilon,k)$-block vertex of range $r$}\}\kern 5.0pt.

Note that
{\mathcal{S}}_{B}
is different from
{\mathcal{S}}_{A}
in that it considers block vertices of range
r=\frac{\log n}{d^{(1/2)+10^{-2}}}
, rather than standard block vertices. Next, we consider the event

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}_{C}

\displaystyle=\{\text{for every path $P$ in $\mathbold{G}$, with $r\leq|P|\leq\log n$, we have $\UpM(P)<1$}\}\kern 5.0pt.

Note that
{\mathcal{S}}_{C}
is about the weights of paths
P
of length between
r
and
\log n
.

Furthermore, in
Section

20.2
and
Section

17.2

we prove the following results:

Lemma 17.3
.

Under the hypotheses of
Theorem

17.1
for

\varepsilon,k,d
and
\beta
, we have
\Pr[{\mathcal{S}}_{C}]\geq 1-n^{-d^{1/5}}
.

Proposition 17.4
.

Under the hypotheses of
Theorem

17.1
for
\varepsilon,k,d
,

\beta
, we have
\Pr[{\mathcal{S}}_{B}]\geq{1-n^{-d^{10/75}}}
.

Observing that
{\mathcal{S}}_{A}\supseteq{\mathcal{S}}_{C}\cap{\mathcal{S}}_{B}
, and applying the union bound gives that:

\displaystyle\Pr[{\mathcal{S}}_{A}]\geq\Pr[{\mathcal{S}}_{C}\cap{\mathcal{S}}_{B}]=1-\Pr\left[\ \overline{{\mathcal{S}}_{C}}\cup\overline{{\mathcal{S}}_{B}}\ \right]\geq 1-n^{-d^{1/5}}-n^{-d^{10/75}}\geq 1-n^{-d^{1/8}}\kern 5.0pt.

Theorem

17.1
follows.
\Box

17.2.
Proof of
Proposition

17.4

To ease the notation, for what follows consider the space induced by

(\mathbold{G},\{\mathbold{\gamma}_{e}\},\beta)
, where
\mathbold{G}=\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
,
\{\mathbold{\gamma}_{e}\}_{e\in E(\mathbold{G})}

are i.i.d. standard Gaussians and
\beta\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.
The triple
(\mathbold{G},\{\mathbold{\gamma}_{e}\},\beta)
encodes all the necessary information about the interaction matrix
\UpJ
and the Gibbs distribution
\mu_{\UpJ,\beta,\uph}

we need for the analysis since the external field
\uph
does not have any effect.

For what follows, we have
r=\ell=\frac{\log n}{d^{(1/2)+10^{-2}}}
. Recall that event
{\mathcal{S}}_{B}
is defined by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}_{B}

\displaystyle=\{\text{every path in ${\mathcal{P}}_{r,\ell}(\mathbold{G})$ contains an $\varepsilon$-block vertex of range $r$}\}\kern 5.0pt,

where set
{\mathcal{P}}_{r,\ell}(\mathbold{G})
is defined in (
17.1
).

For every
(\ell+1)
-tuple
P=(v_{0},\ldots,v_{\ell})
of vertices inducing a path in
\mathbold{G}
we define the events

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}_{P}

\displaystyle=\{P\in\mathcal{P}_{r,\ell}(\mathbold{G})\}

and

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{P}=\left\{P\text{ contains $<({4}/{10})\ell$ many $(\varepsilon,k)$-block vertices of range $r$}\right\}\kern 5.0pt.

We define

(17.4)

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits_{P\in V^{(\ell+1)}_{n}}{\mathds{1}}_{{\mathcal{E}}_{P}}\times{\mathds{1}}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{P}}\kern 5.0pt.

It is easy to verify that

(17.5)

\displaystyle\Pr[\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{B}]\leq\Pr[X>0]\kern 5.0pt,

where
\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{B}
is the complement of the event
{{\mathcal{S}}}_{B}
.

For
P=(v_{0},\ldots,v_{\ell})
in
{V_{n}}^{\ell+1}
, we define the following
random process generating the random tree
\boldsymbol{\UpT}=\boldsymbol{\UpT}(P,d)
. Starting from
set
P
we execute the following steps:

(1)

we (deterministically) add the edges
\{v_{i-1},v_{i}\}
, for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,\ell\}
, making
P
a path,

(2)

from each
v_{i}
in
P
, we hang a GW tree rooted at
v_{i}
, with offspring distribution
{\tt Binom}(n,d/n)
,

(3)

we truncate each GW tree at depth
r
.

Consider
(\boldsymbol{\UpT},\{\overline{\mathbold{\gamma}}_{e}\},\beta)
, where

\{\overline{\mathbold{\gamma}}_{e}\}_{e\in E(\boldsymbol{\UpT})}
are i.i.d. standard Gaussians.
We define
\varepsilon
-weights at the vertices and paths of
\boldsymbol{\UpT}
as described in
Section

4
, using
\{\overline{\mathbold{\gamma}}_{e}\}
and
\beta
.
With respect to this weighting-scheme, we have the event

(17.6)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{\boldsymbol{\UpT}}

\displaystyle=\{P\text{ contains fewer than $({4}/{10})\ell$ many $(\varepsilon,k)$-block vertices of range $r$ in }\boldsymbol{\UpT}\}\kern 5.0pt.

Lemma 17.5
.

For
P\in V^{\ell+1}_{n}
, let
\mathbold{G}_{P}
be an instance of
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
conditional on
P
being a path.
Also, let
\boldsymbol{\UpT}=\boldsymbol{\UpT}(P,d)
. There exists a coupling between
(\mathbold{G}_{P},\{\mathbold{\gamma}_{e}\},\beta)
and
(\boldsymbol{\UpT},\{\overline{\mathbold{\gamma}}_{e}\},\beta)
, such that

{\mathds{1}}_{{\mathcal{E}}_{P}}\times{\mathds{1}}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{P}}\leq{\mathds{1}}_{{\mathcal{E}}_{\UpT}}\kern 5.0pt.

Proof.

We consider the following “BFS tree of the path
P
” in
\mathbold{G}_{P}
, which we denote with
\boldsymbol{T}
.

Specifically, for
i=0,1,\ldots,\ell
, we obtain a tree
\boldsymbol{T}_{i}
of depth
r
, hanging at
vertex
v_{i}
using the following recursive exploration procedure.

For
0\leq s\leq r-1
, assume that the first
s
levels of
\boldsymbol{T}_{i}
have been revealed, and let

\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}\}
be the vertices of its
s
-th level. Then, we obtain the level
(s+1)

by revealing
x_{j}
’s neighbors in
\mathbold{G}_{P}
, excluding the connection with vertices
that have been already explored, or belong to the path
P
, for
1\leq j\leq m
.
That is, the children of
x_{j}
in
\boldsymbol{T}_{i}

will be precisely its neighbors in
\mathbold{G}_{P}
that do not belong to: (i) any tree
\boldsymbol{T}_{l}
for
l<i
, (ii) any
of the vertices that have been revealed to belong in
\boldsymbol{T}_{i}
, up to this point, (iii) the path
P
.
Tree
\boldsymbol{T}
is the union of the
\boldsymbol{T}_{i}
’s and
P
.

The offspring of each vertex
u
in
\boldsymbol{T}
, is dominated by
{\tt Binom}(n,d/n)
. Therefore, we
can couple the graph structures of
\mathbold{G}
and
\boldsymbol{\UpT}
, so that there
exists
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\UpT}}
, subtree of
\boldsymbol{\UpT}
which is isomorphic to
\boldsymbol{T}
.
Let the adjacency preserving bijection
h:V(\boldsymbol{T})\to V(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\UpT}})

between
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\UpT}}
and
\boldsymbol{T}
.

For each pair of edges
e=\{u,w\}
in
E(\boldsymbol{T})
and
\hat{e}=\{h(u),h(w)\}
in

E(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\UpT}})
we couple
\mathbold{\gamma}_{e}
,
\overline{\mathbold{\gamma}}_{\hat{e}}
identically, i.e.,
we have
\overline{\mathbold{\gamma}}_{\hat{e}}=\mathbold{\gamma}_{e}
. For the remaining edges
e
of
\boldsymbol{\UpT}
, we draw

\overline{\mathbold{\gamma}}_{e}\sim N(0,1)
, independently.

All the above, complete the coupling construction.

Consider now the subgraph of
\mathbold{G}_{P}
induced by
N_{r}(P)
, and notice that this will be different from
\boldsymbol{T}

exactly when
N_{r}(P)
contains at least one cycle. In that case we have
{\mathds{1}}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{P}}=0
, and thus,

{\mathds{1}}_{{\mathcal{E}}_{P}}\times{\mathds{1}}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{P}}\leq{\mathds{1}}_{{\mathcal{E}}_{\boldsymbol{\UpT}}}
holds trivially.

On the other hand, if
N_{r}(P)
is a tree, then we have that
{\mathds{1}}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{P}}=1
, and
N_{r}(P)
coincides
with
\boldsymbol{T}
. Since the number of block vertices of
P
decreases if we add more vertices or more edges
in
N_{r}(P)
, and
\boldsymbol{T}
is a subtree of
\boldsymbol{\UpT}
we have that

{\mathds{1}}_{{\mathcal{E}}_{P}}\times{\mathds{1}}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{P}}={\mathds{1}}_{{\mathcal{E}}_{P}}\leq{\mathds{1}}_{{\mathcal{E}}_{\boldsymbol{\UpT}}}
.

All the above conclude the proof of
Lemma

17.5
.
∎

Furthermore, we have the following result.

Proposition 17.6
.

For
\varepsilon>0
, for
1\leq k\leq 100
, there exists
d_{0}=d_{0}(\varepsilon)\geq 1
, such that for all
d\geq d_{0}
, and

\beta\leq\beta_{c}(d)
, the following is true:

For
r=\ell=\frac{\log n}{d^{(1/2)+10^{-2}}}
, and
P\in V^{\ell+1}_{n}
, let

(\boldsymbol{\UpT},\{\overline{\mathbold{\gamma}}_{e}\},\beta)
where
\boldsymbol{\UpT}=\boldsymbol{\UpT}(P,d)
. We have

\displaystyle\Pr[{\mathcal{E}}_{\boldsymbol{\UpT}}]

\displaystyle\leq n^{-d^{1/7}}\kern 5.0pt,

where event
{\mathcal{E}}_{\boldsymbol{\UpT}}
is defined by (
17.6
).

The quantification of parameter
k
in
Proposition

17.6
is necessary to
specify event
{\mathcal{E}}_{\boldsymbol{\UpT}}
.
The proof of
Proposition

17.6
appears in
Section

18
.

Lemma

17.5
and
Proposition

17.6
, imply that

(17.7)

\displaystyle\Pr[X>0]\leq n^{-d^{10/75}}\kern 5.0pt,

where the random variable
X
is defined in (
17.4
).
Specifically, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[X]

\displaystyle=\sum_{P\in V^{(\ell+1)}}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathds{1}}_{{\mathcal{E}}_{P}}\times{\mathds{1}}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{P}}\right]\leq n^{(\ell+1)}\cdot\left({d}/{n}\right)^{\ell}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathds{1}}_{{\mathcal{E}}_{P}}\times{\mathds{1}}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{P}}\mid P\text{ is a path}\right]\kern 5.0pt,

which due to
Lemma

17.5
, and
Proposition

17.6
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[X]

\displaystyle\leq n\cdot{d}^{\ell}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathds{1}}_{{\mathcal{E}}_{\boldsymbol{\UpT}}}\right]\leq n\cdot{n}^{\frac{\log d}{d^{(1/2)+10^{-2}}}}\cdot n^{-d^{1/7}}\kern 5.0pt.

Therefore, there exists
d_{0}=d_{0}(\varepsilon)\geq 1
, such that
for every
d\geq d_{0}
, (
17.7
) is true.

Since
X
is non-negative, Markov’s inequality and (
17.7
) yield

\displaystyle\Pr[X>0]\leq\mathbb{E}[X]\leq{n^{-d^{10/75}}}\kern 5.0pt.

Proposition

17.4
follows by plugging the above into (
17.5
).

\Box

18.
Proof of
Proposition

17.6

Recall that
r=\ell=\frac{\log n}{d^{(1/2)+10^{-2}}}
.
Let
P=(v_{0},\ldots,v_{\ell})\in{V_{n}}^{\ell+1}
and
\boldsymbol{\UpT}=\boldsymbol{\UpT}(P,d)
.
Consider an instance of
(\boldsymbol{\UpT},\{\overline{\mathbold{\gamma}}_{e}\},\beta)
as in

Section

17.2
. For
\varepsilon,k
and
\beta
as specified in the
statement of
Proposition

17.6
, consider
\varepsilon
-weights
for the vertices and paths in
\boldsymbol{\UpT}
.

Definition 18.1

(Vertex Weight induced by Path)
.

For every
i\in\{0,\ldots,\ell\}
we define the weight

\displaystyle{\tt W}_{P}(v_{i})

\displaystyle=\max\nolimits_{Q}\left\{\UpM(Q)\right\}\kern 5.0pt,

where
Q
varies over all paths of length
\leq r
, that emanate from
v_{i}
and do not intersect with
P
.

Definition 18.2

(Left/Right-Block Vertex)
.

We say that vertex
v_{j}\in P
is an

•

left-block vertex with respect to
P
, if for all
t\leq j
, we have that

\prod^{j}_{k=t}{\tt W}_{P}(v_{k})<1
,

•

right-block vertex with respect to
P
, if for all
t\geq j
, we have that

\prod^{t}_{k=j}{\tt W}_{P}(v_{k})<1
.

Perhaps it is useful to remark that there is no assumption as to what is
\UpJ_{e}

for all edges within distance
k
of a (right) left-block vertex
w
.
We have the following lemma.

Lemma 18.3
.

If a vertex
v_{i}
of
P
, is both left-block, and right-block vertex with respect to
P
, then every path
Q
of length
\leq\log n
that emanates from
v_{i}
, satisfies
\UpM(Q)<1
.

Proof of
Lemma

18.3
.

Let

Q
be an arbitrary path of length
r
, starting at vertex
v_{i}
. We will show that
\UpM(Q)<1
. Let
v_{j}
be the last vertex of
P
that appears on
Q
, (note that this is well-defined since
Q
starts at
v_{i}
, and
{\boldsymbol{\UpT}}
is a tree).

W.l.o.g. assume that
j\geq i
, using just the fact that
v_{i}
is right-block, we will show that
\UpM(Q)<1
,
(if
j\leq i
, we use the fact that
v
is left-block). Let
Q_{1}
be the sub-path of
Q
staring at
v_{i}
and arriving at
v_{j-1}
,
and
Q_{2}
be the sub-path of
Q
staring at
v_{j}
and arriving at the last vertex of
Q
, (so that
Q
is the concatenation of
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
).
We have

\displaystyle\UpM(Q)

\displaystyle=\UpM(Q_{1})\cdot\UpM(Q_{2})=\left(\prod^{j-1}_{k=i}\UpM(v_{k})\right)\cdot\UpM(Q_{2})\leq\left(\prod^{j-1}_{k=i}{\tt W}_{P}(v_{k})\right)\cdot{\tt W}_{P}(v_{j})<1\;\kern 5.0pt,

where the first inequality follows from the definition of
{\tt W}_{P}
, and the last inequality is due to the fact that
v_{i}
is a
right-block vertex.
∎

We define the events

(18.1)

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathrm{left}}_{\boldsymbol{\UpT}}}

\displaystyle=\left\{P\text{ contains fewer than {$(1-10^{-3})\ell$} vertices that are left-block in }{\boldsymbol{\UpT}}\right\}\kern 5.0pt,

(18.2)

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathrm{right}}_{\boldsymbol{\UpT}}}

\displaystyle=\left\{P\text{ contains fewer than {$(1-10^{-3})\ell$} vertices that are right-block in }{\boldsymbol{\UpT}}\right\}\kern 5.0pt.

We call a vertex
u\in P
light, if

•

for every edge
e
within distance
k
from
u
, we have
{\rm\Upgamma}_{e}\leq d^{-1/10}
,

•

for every vertex
v
within distance
k+1
from
u
, we have

\sum_{w\sim v}(\UpJ_{v,w})^{2}\leq(1+\varepsilon)d
.

We let the event

(18.3)

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathrm{light}}_{\boldsymbol{\UpT}}}

\displaystyle=\left\{P\text{ contains fewer than }({99}/{100})\ell\text{ vertices in }\boldsymbol{\UpT}\text{ that are light}\right\}\kern 5.0pt.

Recall

{\mathcal{E}}_{\boldsymbol{\UpT}}=\left\{P\text{ contains fewer than {$(1-2\cdot 10^{-3})\ell$} vertices that are $(\varepsilon,k)$-block of range $r$ in }{\boldsymbol{\UpT}}\right\}\kern 5.0pt.

Using
Lemma

18.3
and a simple counting argument, it is not hard to verify that

(18.4)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{\boldsymbol{\UpT}}\subseteq{{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathrm{left}}_{\boldsymbol{\UpT}}}\cap{{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathrm{right}}_{\boldsymbol{\UpT}}}\cap{{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathrm{light}}_{\boldsymbol{\UpT}}}\kern 5.0pt.

We will prove the following result.

Lemma 18.4
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
and
0\leq k\leq\sqrt{\log d}
,
there exists
d_{0}=d_{0}(\varepsilon)\geq 1
, such that for all
d\geq d_{0}
, and

0<\beta\leq\beta_{c}(d)
, the following is true:

For
r=\ell=\frac{\log n}{d^{(1/2)+10^{-2}}}
, and
P=(v_{0},\ldots,v_{\ell})\in V_{n}^{\ell+1}
, let
(\boldsymbol{\UpT},\{\overline{\mathbold{\gamma}}_{e}\},\beta)
where
\boldsymbol{\UpT}=\boldsymbol{\UpT}(P,d)
. Then,

(18.5)

\displaystyle\Pr\left[{{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathrm{light}}_{\boldsymbol{\UpT}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq n^{-\frac{d^{1/5}}{4}}\kern 5.0pt.

Proof of
Lemma

18.4
.

Recall that
N_{k}(v,\boldsymbol{\UpT})=N_{k}(v)
denotes the set of vertices in
\boldsymbol{\UpT}
that are within
distance
k
from
v
. For
v\in V(\boldsymbol{\UpT})
we define the events

{\mathcal{K}}_{1}(v)=\{\exists w,z\in N_{k}(v)\text{ with }{\rm\Upgamma}_{w,z}>d^{-1/10}\}
, and

{\mathcal{K}}_{2}(v)=\{\exists w\in N_{k}(v)\text{ with }\sum_{z\sim w}(\UpJ_{w,z})^{2}>(1+\varepsilon)d\}
.

We claim that

(18.6)

\displaystyle\Pr[{\mathcal{K}}_{i}\text{ occurs for}<({99.5}/{100})\ell\text{ of vertices in }P]\leq n^{-\frac{d^{1/5}}{2}},

\displaystyle i=1,2\kern 5.0pt.

Below we establish (
18.6
) only for
{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
, as the corresponding inequality for
{\mathcal{K}}_{2}

is obtained by similar arguments. For each vertex
v\in V(\boldsymbol{\UpT})
, let

\displaystyle{\rm P}_{\rm Bad}(v)=\Pr\left[\exists\;w,z\in N_{k}(v)\text{ with }{\rm\Upgamma}_{\{w,z\}}>d^{-1/10}\right]\kern 5.0pt.

We define the event
{\mathcal{E}}_{v,k}=\{\deg(w)\leq d^{2},\forall w\in N_{k}(v)\}
. From the law of total
probability, we have

(18.7)

\displaystyle{\rm P}_{\rm Bad}(v)

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\left[\exists\;w,z\in N_{k}(v)\text{ with }{\rm\Upgamma}_{\{w,z\}}>d^{-1/10}\mid{\mathcal{E}}_{v,k}\right]+\Pr[{\mathcal{E}}_{v,k}]\kern 5.0pt.

For an edge
e\in E(\boldsymbol{\UpT})
to be heavy, i.e.,
{\rm\Upgamma}_{e}>d^{-1/10}
, we have

(18.8)

\displaystyle\Pr[{\rm\Upgamma}_{e}>d^{-1/10}]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr[\beta\cdot|\mathbold{\gamma}_{e}|>d^{-1/10}]\leq\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}\cdot\frac{\upkappa}{d}}\cdot d^{1/10}\cdot\exp\left(-\frac{d^{8/10}}{\upkappa}\right)\kern 5.74997pt,

where the last inequality follows from the tail bound of the standard gaussian
\mathbold{\gamma}_{e}
.

Since on the event
{\mathcal{E}}_{v,k}
we have that
N_{k}(v)
spans at most
d^{2k}
edges
in
\boldsymbol{\UpT}
, we have

(18.9)

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\exists\;w,z\in N_{k}(v)\text{ with }{\rm\Upgamma}_{\{w,z\}}>d^{-1/10}\mid{\mathcal{E}}_{v,k}\right]\leq d^{2k}\cdot\exp\left(-d^{8/10}/\upkappa\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Moreover, using standard Chernoff bound, e.g., see
[
8
]
, and the
union bound we see

(18.10)

\displaystyle\Pr[{\mathcal{E}}_{v,k}]\leq d^{2k}\cdot\Pr[\deg(v)>d^{2}]\leq d^{2k}\cdot\exp(-d^{2})\kern 5.0pt.

Therefore, plugging the bounds (
18.10
), (
18.9
) into (
18.7
)
we get

(18.11)

\displaystyle{\rm P}_{\rm Bad}(v)\leq 2d^{2k}\cdot\exp\left(-4d^{8/10}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

We split the vertices of
P
into
k
groups. In particular, let

\displaystyle V_{j}=\{v_{i}\in V(P):i\equiv j\pmod{k}\}

\displaystyle\text{ for }j=1,2,\ldots,k\kern 5.0pt.

From the union bound, we get

\displaystyle\Pr[{\mathcal{K}}_{1}\text{ occurs for}<({99.5}

\displaystyle/{100})\ell\text{ of vertices in }P]

(18.12)

\displaystyle\leq\sum\nolimits_{1\leq s\leq k}\Pr\left[{\mathcal{K}}_{1}\text{ occurs for}<({99.5}/{100})\ell\text{ of vertices in }V_{s}\right]\kern 5.0pt.

Since the couplings on the edges of
\boldsymbol{\UpT}
are drawn independently,
each of the vertices in
V_{s}
is light independently of the others.

Thus, we see that the first term in the lhs (
18.12
) is overestimated by

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle=\Pr\left[{\mathcal{K}}_{1}\text{ occurs for}<({99.5}/{100})\ell\text{ of vertices in }V_{s}\right]

(18.13)

\displaystyle=\sum\nolimits^{\ell}_{t=\lfloor 0.005\ell\rfloor}\binom{\ell}{t}\cdot(1-{\rm P}_{\rm Bad}(v))^{\ell-t}({\rm P}_{\rm Bad}(v))^{t}\kern 5.0pt.

Plugging
\binom{\ell}{t}\leq 2^{\ell}
and
(1-{\rm P}_{\rm Bad}(v))^{\ell-t}\leq 1
into the above inequality,
we have

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle\leq 2^{\ell}\sum\nolimits^{\ell}_{t=\lfloor 0.005\ell\rfloor}({\rm P}_{\rm Bad}(v))^{t}\leq 2^{\ell}\cdot 2^{0.005\ell}\cdot d^{0.01\cdot k\cdot\ell}\cdot\exp\left(-0.02\cdot d^{8/10}\cdot\ell\right)\leq n^{-d^{1/5}}\kern 5.0pt,

for
d
sufficiently large. From symmetry, the same bound holds for every
V_{j}
, i.e.,

(18.14)

\displaystyle\Pr\left[{\mathcal{K}}_{1}\text{ occurs for}<({99.5}/{100})\ell\text{ of vertices in }V_{j}\right]\leq n^{-d^{1/5}},

\displaystyle\text{ for all }j\in[k]\kern 5.0pt.

Plugging (
18.14
) into (
18.12
) we get (
18.6
) for
{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
. Using similar
arguments we establish (
18.6
) for
{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
. By the union bound

\displaystyle\Pr\left[{{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathrm{light}}_{\boldsymbol{\UpT}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum\nolimits_{i=1,2}\Pr\left[{\mathcal{K}}_{i}\text{ occurs for}<({99.5}/{100})\ell\text{ of vertices in }P\right]\leq n^{-\frac{d^{1/5}}{4}}\kern 5.0pt,

concluding the proof of
Lemma

18.4
.
∎

Proposition 18.5
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
d_{0}=d_{0}(\varepsilon)\geq 1
, such that for all
d\geq d_{0}
, and

0<\beta\leq\beta_{c}(d)
, the following is true:

For
r=\ell=\frac{\log n}{d^{(1/2)+10^{-2}}}
, and
P=(v_{0},\ldots,v_{\ell})\in V_{n}^{\ell+1}
, let
(\boldsymbol{\UpT},\{\overline{\mathbold{\gamma}}_{e}\},\beta)
where
\boldsymbol{\UpT}=\boldsymbol{\UpT}(P,d)
. Then,

(18.15)

\displaystyle\Pr\left[{{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathrm{left}}_{\boldsymbol{\UpT}}}\right]\leq n^{-d^{1/6}},

and

\displaystyle\Pr\left[{{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathrm{right}}_{\boldsymbol{\UpT}}}\right]\leq n^{-d^{1/6}}\kern 5.0pt,

where the probability is over the instances of
(\boldsymbol{\UpT},\{\overline{\mathbold{\gamma}}_{e}\},\beta)
.

Using
Proposition

18.5
,
Lemma

18.4
, (
18.4
) and the union bound, we further get

\Pr\left[{\mathcal{E}}_{\boldsymbol{\UpT}}\right]\leq\Pr\left[{{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathrm{left}}_{\boldsymbol{\UpT}}}\right]+\Pr\left[{{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathrm{right}}_{\boldsymbol{\UpT}}}\right]+\Pr\left[{{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathrm{light}}_{\boldsymbol{\UpT}}}\right]\leq 4\cdot n^{-d^{1/6}}\leq n^{-d^{1/7}}\kern 5.0pt.

The above concludes
Proposition

17.6
.

\Box

18.1.
Proof of
Proposition

18.5

We prove the bound of
Proposition

18.5
only for
\Pr[{{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathrm{left}}_{\boldsymbol{\UpT}}}]
, as the derivations for
\Pr[{{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathrm{right}}_{\boldsymbol{\UpT}}}]
are identical.

We define the set of
heavy
vertices of
P
by ,

\displaystyle H=H(P)=\left\{i\in\{0,\ldots,\ell\}:{\tt W}_{P}(v_{i})\geq 1\right\}\kern 5.0pt.

Recall the definition of
{\tt W}_{P}(v_{i})
from
Definition

18.2
.

For
i\in H
, let
t_{i}
be the greatest index in
\{i,i+1,\ldots,\ell\}
such that for all
t\in\{i,i+1,\ldots,t_{i}\}

\displaystyle\prod\nolimits_{i\leq k\leq t}{\tt W}_{P}(v_{k})\geq 1\kern 5.0pt,

and let
L_{i}=\{i,i+1,\ldots,t_{i}\}
. For
i\notin H
, define
L_{i}=\emptyset
, and let
L=L_{0}\cup L_{1}\cup\ldots\cup L_{\ell}
.

Lemma 18.6
.

For all
j\in\{0,1,\dots,\ell\}\setminus L
, vertex
v_{j}
is a left-block vertex with respect to
P
.

Proof.

We show the contrapositive, i.e., assuming
v_{j}
is not left-block with respect to
P
, we show that
j\in L
. If
v_{j}
is not left-block, then there must exist at least one index
i\in\{0,1,\ldots,j\}
such that

\prod^{j}_{k=i}\;{\tt W}_{P}(v_{k})\geq 1
.

Let
i^{*}
be the greatest such index; we claim that
j\in L_{i^{*}}
, i.e., for every
t\in\{i^{*},i^{*}+1,\ldots,j\}
we have
\prod^{t}_{k=i^{*}}\;{\tt W}_{P}(v_{k})\geq 1
. Indeed, if there was a
t\in\{i^{*},i^{*}+1,\ldots,j\}
such that
\prod^{t}_{k=i^{*}}\;{\tt W}_{P}(v_{k})<1
, then we would have that
\prod^{j}_{k=t+1}\;{\tt W}_{P}(v_{k})\geq 1
, which contradicts with the fact that
i^{*}
is the greatest such index.
∎

The result below shows that, typically, only a small fraction of vertices in
P
to belong in
L
.

Theorem 18.7
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
d_{0}=d_{0}(\varepsilon)\geq 1
, such that for all
d\geq d_{0}
, and

0<\beta\leq\beta_{c}(d)
, the following is true:

For
r=\ell=\frac{\log n}{d^{(1/2)+10^{-2}}}
, and
P=(v_{0},\ldots,v_{\ell})\in V_{n}^{\ell+1}
, let

(\boldsymbol{\UpT},\{\overline{\mathbold{\gamma}}_{e}\},\beta)
where
\boldsymbol{\UpT}=\boldsymbol{\UpT}(P,d)
. Then,

\displaystyle\Pr\left[|L|\geq\ell\right]\leq n^{-d^{1/6}}\kern 5.0pt.

In light of
Lemma

18.6
, and
Theorem

18.7
,
Proposition

18.5
follows.
Therefore, to finish proving
Proposition

18.5
, it only remains to prove
Theorem

18.7
.

19.
Proof of
Theorem

18.7

Applying the union bound, we see that

(19.1)

\displaystyle|L|

\displaystyle=\left|\bigcup\nolimits_{i\in H}L_{i}\right|\leq\sum\nolimits_{i\in H}|L_{i}|\kern 5.0pt.

We use the following strategy to estimate each
|L_{i}|
for
i\in H
.
Each heavy vertex,
v_{i}
, introduces a weight,
{{\tt W}}_{P}(v_{i})>1
. If
L_{i}=\{i,i+1\ldots,j\}
, then notice that
\{i,i+1\ldots,j+1\}
is the first interval on the right of
i
that “absorbs” weight
{\tt W}_{P}(v_{i})
, i.e.,
j
is the smallest index in
\{0,\ldots,i\}
such that

{\prod\nolimits_{i\leq k\leq j+1}{\tt W}_{P}(v_{k})<1}\kern 5.0pt.

Let
\theta=\varepsilon/4
. If
H\cap L_{i}=\emptyset
, we observe that
v_{i}
requires
\lceil-\log_{(1-\theta)}{{\tt W}}_{P}(v_{i})\rceil
light vertices to get
{{\tt W}}_{P}(v_{i})
absorbed.
Similarly, if
H\cap L_{i}\neq\emptyset
, the number of light vertices needs to be

\left\lceil-\sum\nolimits_{j\in H\cap L_{i}}\log_{(1-\theta)}{\tt W}_{P}(v_{j})\right\rceil\kern 5.0pt.

Due to (
19.1
), the above yields
the following corollary

Corollary 19.1
.

Let
\theta=\varepsilon/4
, then we have that

(19.2)

\displaystyle|L|\leq|H|-\sum\nolimits_{i\in H}\log_{(1-\theta)}{\tt W}_{P}(v_{i})\kern 5.0pt.

We also use the following technical lemma, which we prove in
Section

19.1
.

Lemma 19.2
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
d_{0}=d_{0}(\varepsilon)\geq 1
, such that for all
d\geq d_{0}
, and

0<\beta\leq\beta_{c}(d)
, the following is true:

For
r=\ell=\frac{\log n}{d^{(1/2)+10^{-2}}}
, and
P=(v_{0},\ldots,v_{\ell})\in V_{n}^{\ell+1}
,
let
(\boldsymbol{\UpT},\{\overline{\mathbold{\gamma}}_{e}\},\beta)
where
\boldsymbol{\UpT}=\boldsymbol{\UpT}(P,d)
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[({\tt W}_{P}(v_{i}))^{t}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-{\varepsilon}/{4}\right)^{q}

\displaystyle\textrm{for }0\leq i\leq\ell\kern 5.0pt,

where
t=d^{95/100}
and
q=d^{93/100}
.

We also have the following result.

Lemma 19.3
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
d_{0}=d_{0}(\varepsilon)\geq 1
, such that for all
d\geq d_{0}
,
and
0<\beta\leq\beta_{c}(d)
, the following is true:

For
r=\ell=\frac{\log n}{d^{(1/2)+10^{-2}}}
,
and
P=(v_{0},\ldots,v_{\ell})\in V_{n}^{\ell+1}
, let
(\boldsymbol{\UpT},\{\overline{\mathbold{\gamma}}_{e}\},\beta)
where

\boldsymbol{\UpT}=\boldsymbol{\UpT}(P,d)
. Then,

(19.3)

\displaystyle\Pr\left[|H|\geq\ell\cdot d^{-1/10}\right]\leq n^{-d^{1/4}}\kern 5.0pt,

where
H
is the set of heavy vertices defined above.

Proof of
Lemma

19.3
.

Let
I_{1}
,
I_{2}
,be the sets of odd, even indices of
\{0,\ldots,\ell\}
, respectively. Writing

H_{1}=H\cap I_{1}
,
H_{2}=H\cap I_{2}
, and using the union bound, it is easy to see that

(19.4)

\displaystyle\Pr\left[|H|\geq\ell\cdot d^{-1/10}\right]\leq\Pr\left[|H_{1}|\geq(1/2)\cdot\ell\cdot d^{-1/10}\right]+\Pr\left[|H_{2}|\geq(1/2)\cdot\ell\cdot d^{-1/10}\right]\kern 5.0pt.

H_{1}
and
H_{2}
are identically distributed. Hence, it suffices to show that

\displaystyle\Pr\left[|H_{1}|\geq(1/2)\cdot\ell\cdot d^{-1/10}\right]\leq(1/2)\cdot n^{-d^{1/4}}\kern 5.0pt.

Notice that for any two indices
i,j\in I_{1}
, the corresponding random variables
{\tt W}_{P}(v_{i}),{\tt W}_{P}(v_{j})
,
are i.i.d., and thus, each index
i\in I_{1}
belongs to
H_{1}
with probability at most
\Pr[{\tt W}_{P}(v_{1})\geq 1]
.
Specifically, per
Lemma

19.2
, and Markov’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[{\tt W}_{P}(v_{1})\geq 1\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\left({\tt W}_{P}(v_{i})\right)^{t}\right]\leq\left(1-{\varepsilon}/{4}\right)^{q}\kern 5.0pt,

where
t=d^{95/100}
and
q=d^{93/100}
.

Therefore,
|H_{1}|
is upper bounded by the number of successes of a binomial distribution with
\ell/2
number
of trials, and probability of success
\left(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\right)^{q}
. Hence, for
m=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\ell}{d^{1/10}}

and noting that
m\gg\frac{\ell}{2}(1-\varepsilon/4)^{q}
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[|H_{1}|\geq m\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum\nolimits_{m\leq k\leq\ell/2}\binom{\ell/2}{k}\cdot\exp\left(\log{\left(1-{\varepsilon}/{4}\right)}\cdot q\cdot k\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\ell}{2}\cdot\binom{\ell/2}{m}\cdot\exp\left(\log{\left(1-{\varepsilon}/{4}\right)}\cdot q\cdot m\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\ell}{2}\cdot\left(\frac{e\cdot\ell}{2m}\right)^{m}\cdot\exp\left(\log{\left(1-{\varepsilon}/{4}\right)}\cdot q\cdot m\right)

\displaystyle\leq(\log n)\exp\left(\log n\left(d^{-1/2}+\log(1-\varepsilon/4)\cdot(1/2)\cdot\upz\cdot d^{\frac{33}{100}}\right)\right)\kern 5.0pt.

In the last inequality we used the inequalities
q\cdot m\leq 1/2\cdot\cdot d^{\frac{33}{100}-\upz}\cdot\log n
,

\frac{\ell}{2}\leq\log n
and

(19.5)

\displaystyle\left({\textstyle\frac{e\cdot\ell}{2m}}\right)^{m}

\displaystyle\leq\left({\textstyle e\cdot d^{\frac{1}{10}}}\right)^{m}\leq\exp\left({\textstyle\frac{\log n}{\sqrt{d}}}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Hence, for
d\geq d_{0}(\varepsilon)
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[|H_{1}|\geq(1/2)\cdot\ell\cdot d^{-1/10}\right]=\Pr\left[|H_{1}|\geq m\right]\leq n^{-d^{1/4}}\leq(1/2)\cdot n^{-d^{1/4}}\kern 5.0pt,

as desired, concluding the proof of
Lemma

19.3
.
∎

We have the following bound on the upper tail of
\sum_{i\in H}\log{\tt W}_{P}(v_{i})
.

Theorem 19.4
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
d_{0}=d_{0}(\varepsilon)\geq 1
, such that for all
d\geq d_{0}
, and

0<\beta\leq\beta_{c}(d)
, the following is true:

For
r=\ell=\frac{\log n}{d^{(1/2)+10^{-2}}}
, and
P=(v_{0},\ldots,v_{\ell})\in V_{n}^{\ell+1}
, let

(\boldsymbol{\UpT},\{\overline{\mathbold{\gamma}}_{e}\},\beta)
where
\boldsymbol{\UpT}=\boldsymbol{\UpT}(P,d)
. Then,

\Pr\left[\sum\nolimits_{i\in H}\log{\tt W}_{P}(v_{i})\geq\ell\cdot d^{-1/50}\middle||H|\leq\ell\cdot d^{-{1}/{10}}\right]\leq n^{-d^{1/3}}\kern 5.0pt.

Given
Theorem

19.4
, we establish
Theorem

18.7
as follows.

Proof of
Theorem

18.7
.

Write
\theta=\varepsilon/4
, due to
Corollary

19.1
, we have

(19.6)

\displaystyle\Pr\left[|L|\geq\ell\right]\leq\Pr\left[|H|+\theta^{-1}\cdot\sum\nolimits_{i\in H}\log{\tt W}_{P}(v_{i})\geq\ell\right]\kern 5.0pt.

From the law of total probability we have

(19.7)

\Pr\left[|H|+\theta^{-1}\cdot\sum\nolimits_{i\in H}\log{\tt W}_{P}(v_{i})\geq\ell\right]\leq\\
\Pr\left[|H|\geq\ell\cdot d^{-1/10}\right]+\Pr\left[|H|+\theta^{-1}\cdot\sum\nolimits_{i\in H}\log{\tt W}_{P}(v_{i})\geq\ell\middle||H|\leq\ell\cdot d^{-1/10}\right]\kern 5.0pt.

Moreover, for sufficiently large
d
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[|H|+\frac{1}{\theta}\cdot\sum_{i\in H}\log{\tt W}_{P}(v_{i})\geq\ell\middle||H|\leq\frac{\ell}{d^{1/10}}\right]\leq\Pr\left[\sum_{i\in H}\log{\tt W}_{P}(v_{i})\geq\frac{\ell}{d^{1/50}}\middle||H|\leq\frac{\ell}{d^{1/10}}\right]\kern 5.0pt.

Plugging the above into (
19.7
), then from
Theorem

19.4
,
and
Lemma

19.3
we get

\displaystyle\Pr\left[|H|+\theta^{-1}\cdot\sum\nolimits_{i\in H}\log{\tt W}_{P}(v_{i})\geq\ell\right]\leq n^{-d^{1/3}}+{n}^{-d^{1/4}}\leq{n}^{-d^{1/6}}\kern 5.0pt.

This concludes the proof of
Theorem

18.7
.
∎

Let us now prove
Theorem

19.4
.

Proof of
Theorem

19.4
.

Similarly to the proof of
Lemma

19.3
, we let
I_{1}
, and
I_{2}
,be the sets of odd, and even indices of
\{0,\ldots,\ell\}
, respectively. Writing
H_{1}=H\cap I_{1}
, and
H_{2}=H\cap I_{2}
, and using the union bound, we see it is sufficient to prove that

\displaystyle 2\cdot\Pr\left[\sum\nolimits_{i\in H_{1}}\log{\tt W}_{P}(v_{i})\geq\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{\ell}{d^{1/50}}\middle||H_{1}|\leq\frac{\ell}{d^{{1}/{10}}}\right]\leq n^{-d^{1/3}}\kern 5.0pt.

From Markov’s inequality we get that for every
t\geq 0

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\sum_{i\in H_{1}}\log{\tt W}_{P}(v_{i})\geq\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{\ell}{d^{1/50}}\middle||H_{1}|\leq\frac{\ell}{d^{{1}/{10}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\exp(t\cdot\sum_{i\in H_{1}}\cdot\log{\tt W}_{P}(v_{i}))\middle||H_{1}|\leq\frac{\ell}{d^{{1}/{10}}}\right]}{\exp\left(\frac{t}{2\cdot d^{1/50}}\cdot\ell\right)}

(19.8)

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\exp(t\cdot\log{\tt W}_{P}(v_{1}))\ |v_{1}\in H_{1}\right]\right)^{{\ell}\cdot d^{-1/10}}}{\exp\left(\frac{t}{2\cdot d^{1/50}}\cdot\ell\right)}\kern 5.0pt,

where the equality follows from the fact that the random variables
{\tt W}_{P}(v_{j})
’s where
j\in H_{1}
are i.i.d.

Let us write
v
instead of
v_{1}
, and notice that since
{\tt W}_{P}(v)
is non-negative, we also have

(19.9)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\exp(t\cdot\log{\tt W}_{P}(v))\ |\ v\in H_{1}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\left({\tt W}_{P}(v)\right)^{t}\right]}{\Pr[{\tt W}_{P}(v)\geq 1]}\kern 5.0pt.

For the enumerator of (
19.9
) we use the bounded provided by

Lemma

19.2
. Let us now bound the denominator of (
19.9
), i.e.,

\Pr[{\tt W}_{P}(v)\geq 1]
. Recalling the definitions of
{\Uptheta},\UpM
, and
{\tt W}
,
we have

\displaystyle\Pr[{\tt W}_{P}(v)\geq 1]\geq{\Pr\left[\,\UpM(v)\geq 1\right]}\geq{\Pr[{\Uptheta}(v)\geq 1]}\geq\Pr[{\Uptheta}(v)\cdot\mathds{1}\{d<\mathrm{deg}(v)\leq 2d\}\geq 1]\kern 5.0pt.

Using Baye’s rule, we get

(19.10)

\displaystyle\Pr[{\tt W}_{P}(v)\geq 1]\geq\Pr[{{\Uptheta}(v)\geq 1}\mid d\leq\mathrm{deg}(v)\leq 2d]\cdot\Pr[d\leq\mathrm{deg}(v)\leq 2d]\kern 5.0pt.

Let us now focus on the first factor in the rhs of (
19.10
).
Recall

{\Uptheta}(v)=\sum\nolimits_{1\leq i\leq\mathrm{deg}(v)}\left|\tanh\left({\beta}\mathbold{\gamma}_{i}\right)\right|^{2}\kern 5.0pt,

for i.i.d., standard gaussians
\mathbold{\gamma}_{i}
’s.
With that in mind,
we have

\displaystyle\Pr[{\Uptheta}(v)\geq 1\mid d\leq\mathrm{deg}(v)\leq 2d]

\displaystyle\geq\left(\Pr\left[\;\left|\tanh\left({\beta}\mathbold{\gamma}_{i}\right)\right|^{2}\geq d^{-1}\;\right]\right)^{2d}

\displaystyle\geq\left(2\cdot\Pr\left[\;\mathbold{\gamma}_{i}\geq\beta^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{arctanh}\left(d^{-1/2}\right)\;\right]\right)^{2d}

\displaystyle\geq\left(2\cdot\Pr\left[\;\mathbold{\gamma}_{i}\geq(2\beta)^{-1}\cdot\left(\frac{1+({1}/\sqrt{d})}{1-({1}/\sqrt{d})}-1\right)\;\right]\right)^{2d}

\displaystyle\geq\left(2\cdot\Pr\left[\;\mathbold{\gamma}_{i}\geq\frac{1}{\beta\cdot(\sqrt{d}-1)}\;\right]\right)^{2d}\kern 5.0pt.

Since
\beta\leq\beta_{c}(d)
, there exists
\lambda\leq 1/2
such
that
\beta=\frac{\lambda}{\sqrt{d}-1}
, and thus, we
rewrite the above as

\displaystyle\Pr[{\Uptheta}(v)\geq 1\mid d\leq\mathrm{deg}(v)\leq 2d]\geq\left(2\cdot\Pr\left[\;\mathbold{\gamma}_{i}\geq\lambda^{-1}\right]\right)^{2d}\kern 5.0pt.

Since
\lambda^{-1}>0
, we have
\Pr\left[\;\mathbold{\gamma}_{i}\geq\lambda^{-1}\right]<1/2
.
Hence, there exists constant
C_{1}>0
, such that

(19.11)

\displaystyle\Pr[{\Uptheta}(v)\geq 1\mid d\leq\mathrm{deg}(v)\leq 2d]\geq\exp(-C_{1}\cdot d)\kern 5.0pt.

Regarding the second factor in the rhs of (
19.10
), we have

(19.12)

\displaystyle\Pr[d\leq\mathrm{deg}(v)\leq 2d]

\displaystyle=\Pr[d\leq\mathrm{deg}(v)]-\Pr[\mathrm{deg}(v)>2d]=\frac{1}{2}-\Pr[\mathrm{deg}(v)>2d]\geq\frac{1}{4}.

Where the last inequality follows from Chernoff bounds.

Plugging (
19.11
) and (
19.12
) into (
19.10
), there exists constant
C>0
such that

(19.13)

\displaystyle\Pr[{\tt W}_{P}(v)\geq 1]\geq\exp(-C\cdot d)\kern 5.0pt.

Choosing
t=d^{95/100}
, while plugging into
(
19.9
) the bound on
\mathbb{E}\left[({\tt W}_{P}(v_{1}))^{t}\right]

from
Lemma

19.2
and (
19.13
), we get

\mathbb{E}\left[\exp(t\cdot\log{\tt W}_{P}(v))\ |\ v\in H_{1}\right]\leq e^{d\cdot C}\cdot\left(1-\varepsilon/4\right)^{d^{\frac{93}{100}}}\leq\exp\left(\textstyle{d\cdot\frac{C}{2}}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Plugging the above into (
19.8
) for
t=d^{\frac{95}{100}}
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\sum\nolimits_{i\in H_{1}}\log{\tt W}_{P}(v_{i})\geq(1/2)\cdot\ell\cdot d^{-1/50}\middle||H_{1}|\leq\ell\cdot d^{-1/10}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-d^{\frac{92}{100}}\cdot\ell\right)\leq(1/2)\cdot n^{-d^{1/3}}\kern 5.0pt,

as desired. This concludes the proof of
Theorem

19.4
.
∎

19.1.
Proof of
Lemma

19.2

Fix
i\in\{0,\ldots,\ell\}
. Let us write
v
instead of
v_{i}
. For
s\geq 0
, and a path
Q=(w_{0},\ldots,w_{s})
, let us define

\displaystyle\UpM^{\rm even}(Q)=\prod\nolimits_{0\leq i\leq\lfloor s/2\rfloor}\UpM(w_{2i})\kern 5.0pt,

that is, the weight of
Q
contributed only by vertices at even distance from
w_{0}
. Similarly, define
\UpM^{\rm odd}(Q)
to be the weight of
Q
contributed only by vertices at odd distance from
w_{0}
. Let us also define
also define
{\tt W}_{P}^{\rm even}(v)=\max_{Q}\{\UpM^{\rm even}(Q)\}
, and
{\tt W}_{P}^{\rm odd}(v)=\max_{Q}\{\UpM^{\rm odd}(Q)\}
where the maximisation is over all paths
Q
of length at most
r
, that emanate from
v
and do not intersect with
P
, i.e., they to do not share vertices. Since
{\tt W}_{P}(v)\leq{\tt W}_{P}^{\rm odd}(v)\cdot{\tt W}_{P}^{\rm even}(v)
, the union bound yields

\Pr\left[{\tt W}_{P}(v)\geq 1\right]\leq\Pr[{\tt W}_{P}^{\rm odd}(v)\geq 1]+\Pr[{\tt W}_{P}^{\rm even}(v)\geq 1]\kern 5.0pt,

and thus, it suffices to show that

\Pr[{\tt W}_{P}^{\rm even}(v)\geq 1]\leq\left(1-{\varepsilon}/{4}\right)^{q}/2\kern 5.0pt,

Writing
\mathrm{path}_{v}(r)
to denote all paths of length
r
in
{\boldsymbol{\UpT}}
, that emanate from
v
, and do not intersect with
P
, and
\mathrm{path}_{v}(\leq r)=\cup_{k\leq r}\mathrm{path}_{v}(k)
, we see that for any
t>0
we have that

(19.14)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left({\tt W}_{P}^{\rm even}(v)\right)^{t}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\max_{Q\in\mathrm{path}_{v}(\leq r)}\{\UpM^{\rm even}(Q)\}\right)^{t}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{Q\in\mathrm{path}_{v}\left(\leq r\right)}\left(\UpM^{\rm even}(Q)\right)^{t}\right]\kern 5.0pt.

Note that we cannot pull the sum out of the expectation in the rhs of the inequality above, as the set
\mathrm{path}_{v}\left(\leq r\right)
is a random variable. Therefore, we think in the following way. For
k=0\ldots r
, there are at most
n^{k}
potential paths of length
k
emanating from
v
, and each potential path of has probability
(d/n)^{k}
to be present in
{\boldsymbol{\UpT}}
. Denoting with
(w_{0},\ldots,w_{k})
an arbitrary such potential path emanating from
v
, i.e.,
w_{0}=v
, we see that

(19.15)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left({\tt W}_{P}^{\rm even}(v)\right)^{t}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{r}n^{k}\cdot\left(\frac{d}{n}\right)^{k}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\prod_{i=0}^{\lfloor k/2\rfloor}\UpM\left(w_{2i}\right)\right)^{t}\right]\leq\sum_{k=0}^{r}d^{k}\cdot\left(\mathbb{E}\left[{\UpM^{t}\left(v\right)}\right]\right)^{\lfloor k/2\rfloor+1}\kern 5.0pt,

where the last inequality follows from the independence of the weights
\UpM
corresponding to same parity vertices along
(w_{0},\ldots,w_{\ell})
.
To upper bound
\mathbb{E}\left[{\UpM^{t}\left(v\right)}\right]
we consider two regimes in terms of the degree of vertex
v
:

(19.16)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\UpM^{t}\left(v\right)}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{\UpM^{t}\left(v\right)}\cdot{\mathds{1}}\{\mathrm{deg}(v)\leq 3d\}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[{\UpM^{t}\left(v\right)}\cdot{\mathds{1}}\{\mathrm{deg}(v)>3d\}\right]

Let us now focus on the first term of (
19.16
). Writing
g(x)
for the
pdf of
\UpM(v)\cdot{\mathds{1}}\{\mathrm{deg}(v)\leq 3d\}
, and noticing that
\UpM(v)
is at most

3d^{2}
, we see that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\UpM^{t}\left(v\right)}\cdot{\mathds{1}}\{\mathrm{deg}(v)\leq 3d\}\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1-\varepsilon/4}x^{t}\cdot g(x)\;dx+\int_{1-\varepsilon/4}^{3d^{2}}x^{t}\cdot g(x)\;dx

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-{\varepsilon}/{4}\right)^{t}\cdot\Pr\left[{\Uptheta}(v)\leq 1-{\varepsilon}/{2}\right]+(3d^{2})^{t}\cdot\int_{1-\varepsilon/2}^{3d^{2}}g(x)\;dx

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-{\varepsilon}/{4}\right)^{t}+(3d^{2})^{t}\cdot\Pr\left[{\Uptheta}(v)\geq{1-{\varepsilon}/{2}}\right]

(19.17)

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-{\varepsilon}/{4}\right)^{t}+(3d^{2})^{t}\cdot\exp\left(-d\cdot{\varepsilon^{2}}/{(18\upkappa^{2})}\right)\kern 5.0pt,

where (
19.17
) follows from
Theorem

4.1
.
We now focus the large-degree case. Accounting only for the randomness on the degree of
v
, and overestimating each term of
\UpM(v)
to be equal to
d
, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\UpM^{t}\left(v\right)}\cdot{\mathds{1}}\{\mathrm{deg}(v)>3d\}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=3d}^{n}(d\cdot k)^{t}\cdot\Pr[\mathrm{deg}(v)=k]\leq d^{t}\cdot\sum_{k=3d}^{n}k^{t}\cdot\binom{n}{k}\cdot\left(\frac{d}{n}\right)^{k}

(19.18)

\displaystyle\leq d^{t}\cdot\sum_{k=3d}^{n}k^{t}\cdot\left(\frac{ne}{k}\right)^{k}\cdot\left(\frac{d}{n}\right)^{k}\leq d^{t}\cdot\sum_{k=3d}^{n}k^{t}\cdot\left(\frac{de}{k}\right)^{k}\kern 5.0pt.

Substituting (
19.17
) and (
19.18
) to (
19.16
), gives

\mathbb{E}\left[{\UpM^{t}\left(v\right)}\right]\leq\left(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\right)^{t}+(3d^{2})^{t}\cdot\exp\left(-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{18\upkappa^{2}}\cdot d\right)+d^{t}\cdot\sum_{k=3d}^{n}k^{t}\cdot\left(\frac{de}{k}\right)^{k}\kern 5.0pt.

Since
ed/k<1
, for every
k>3d
, choosing
t=d^{{95}/{100}}
, we see that there exist
d_{0}(\varepsilon)\geq 1
, such that for every
d\geq d_{0}
, we have that

\mathbb{E}\left[{\UpM^{t}\left(v\right)}\right]\leq(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{4})^{t^{\prime}}
, where
t^{\prime}=d^{{94}/{100}}
. We now bound (
19.15
) as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left({\tt W}^{\rm even}_{P}(v)\right)^{{t}}\right]\leq\frac{(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{4})^{{t^{\prime}}}}{1-d^{2}\cdot(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{4})^{{t^{\prime}}}}{\leq{\left(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\right)^{{q}}}}\kern 5.0pt,

where we can take
q=d^{93/100}
.

20.
Remaining Proofs

20.1.
Proof of
Theorem

4.1

First, recall that
{\Uptheta}(v)=\sum_{{z\sim v}}{\rm\Upgamma}^{2}_{\{v,z\}}
, with

{\rm\Upgamma}_{e}=\left|\tanh\left(\beta\cdot\mathbold{\gamma}_{e}\right)\right|

where
\mathbold{\gamma}_{e}
follows the standard Gaussian distribution.
Since
|\tanh(x)|\leq|x|
, for every real
x
, we see that

{\rm\Upgamma}_{e}\leq{\beta}\cdot|\mathbold{\gamma}_{e}|
.
Hence, bounding from above the upper-tail of
\sum_{e}{\beta}^{2}\cdot|\mathbold{\gamma}_{e}|^{2}
, provides an upper bound to the corresponding tail of
\sum_{e}{\rm\Upgamma}_{e}^{2}
, i.e., for every
x\in\mathbb{R}
we have

(20.1)

\textstyle\Pr\left[{\Uptheta}(v)\geq x\right]\leq\Pr\left[\sum_{e}{\beta}^{2}\cdot|\mathbold{\gamma}_{e}|^{2}\geq x\right]\kern 5.0pt.

Note also that using Wald’s lemma we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[{\Uptheta}(v)]=d\cdot\mathbb{E}[|\tanh(\beta\cdot\mathbold{\gamma})|^{2}]\leq d\cdot\mathbb{E}[|\beta\cdot\mathbold{\gamma}|^{2}]=d\cdot\beta^{2}\kern 5.74997pt.

Let the event
{\mathcal{E}}:=\{\mathrm{deg}(v)\leq\!\left(1+\frac{\updelta}{2}\right)d\}
.
From the total probability law we have

(20.2)

\displaystyle\Pr\left[{\Uptheta}(v)\geq d\beta^{2}+{\updelta}/{2}\right]\!\leq\!\Pr\left[{\Uptheta}(v)\geq d\beta^{2}\!+\!{\updelta}/{2}\!\mid\!{\mathcal{E}}\right]\!\!+\Pr\left[{\mathcal{E}}\right]\kern 5.0pt.

Let us now focus on the first term in the rhs of (
20.2
). Applying the tail bounds for chi-square distribution
we see that the first term is upper bounded by

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\sum_{i=1}^{(1+\updelta/2)d}\mathbold{\gamma}^{2}_{i}\geq d+\frac{\updelta}{2\beta^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\frac{(1+\updelta/2)d}{2}\cdot\left[\frac{1-\updelta/2}{\beta^{2}(1+\updelta/2)d}-1-\log\left(\frac{1-\updelta/2}{\beta^{2}(1+\updelta/2)d}\right)\right]\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Since
0<\beta<\sqrt{{1}/{d}}
, we have

(20.3)

\displaystyle\Pr\left[{\Uptheta}(v)\geq d\cdot\beta^{2}+\frac{\updelta}{2}\mid{\mathcal{E}}\right]\leq\exp\left(-\frac{1+\updelta/2}{2}d\left[\frac{2\updelta^{2}}{(1+\delta)}\right]\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\updelta^{2}}{(1+\delta/2)}\cdot d\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Let us now turn to the second term of (
20.2
). Since
\mathrm{deg}(v)
is a sum of independent Bernoulli random variables, applying the Chernoff tail bound imply

(20.4)

\displaystyle\Pr[{\mathcal{E}}]=\Pr\left[\mathrm{deg}(v)>(1+\delta/2)d\right]\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\delta^{2}}{8+2\delta}d\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Substituting (
20.3
) and (
20.4
), in (
20.2
) gives (
4.5
).

\Box

20.2.
Proof of Lemma
17.3

We show that for an arbitrary path
P
in
\mathbold{G}
, with
r\leq|P|\leq\log n
, we have that

(20.5)

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\UpM(P)\geq 1\right]\leq{n^{-d^{1/4}}}\kern 5.0pt.

In light of (
20.5
), applying the union bound over all such paths gives us the result

\displaystyle\Pr\left[{\mathcal{S}}_{1}\right]\geq 1-\sum_{k=r}^{\log n}\binom{n}{k+1}\left(\frac{d}{n}\right)^{k}\cdot n^{-d^{1/4}}\geq 1-\sum_{k=r}^{\log n}{n}\cdot{d}^{k}\cdot n^{-d^{1/4}}\geq 1-n^{-d^{1/5}},

where the last inequality holds for large
d
and
n
. Therefore, we now focus on proving (
20.5
).

Let
P=(v_{0},\ldots,v_{k})
, with
r\leq k\leq\log n
. We split the vertices of
P
into two sets,
\mathrm{In}(P)
, and
\mathrm{Out}(P)
. The set
\mathrm{In}(P)
is comprised by all vertices
v_{i}
of
P
that are adjacent to a vertex in
V(P)\setminus\{v_{i-1},v_{i+1}\}
, and
\mathrm{Out}(P)=V(P)\setminus\mathrm{In}(P)
, so that

(20.6)

\displaystyle\UpM(P)=\UpM\left(\mathrm{In}(P)\right)\cdot\UpM(\mathrm{Out}(P))=\prod_{v\in\mathrm{In}(P)}\UpM(v)\cdot\prod_{w\in\mathrm{Out}(P)}\UpM(w)\kern 5.0pt.

We next bound separately
\UpM\left(\mathrm{In}(P)\right)
, and
\UpM\left(\mathrm{Out}(P)\right)
, so that their product is less than
1
, w.h.p..

Let us start by bounding
\UpM\left(\mathrm{In}(P)\right)
. We first notice the following tail bound for
|\mathrm{In}(P)|
:

(20.7)

\displaystyle\Pr\left[|\mathrm{In}(P)|\geq 2\sqrt{d}\right]\leq\sum_{s=\sqrt{d}}^{k}\binom{k^{2}}{s}\cdot\left(\frac{d}{n}\right)^{s}\leq\sum_{s=\sqrt{d}}^{k}\left(\frac{d\cdot k^{2}}{n}\right)^{s}\leq 2\cdot\left(\frac{d\cdot k^{2}}{n}\right)^{\sqrt{d}}\leq n^{-{\sqrt{d}}/{2}}\kern 5.0pt,

where the last two inequalities hold for large
d
and
n
.

For a vertex
v_{i}
in
P
, let
\mathrm{deg}_{\rm in}(v_{i})
be the number of neighbors
that
v_{i}
has in
P
, while let
\mathrm{deg}_{\rm out}(v_{i})
be the number of
neighbors in
V\setminus P
.

For a vertex
w\in\mathrm{In}(P)
we have that
\mathrm{deg}_{\rm out}(w)
is dominated by

{\tt Binom}(n,d/n)
. Then, we obtain

(20.8)

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathrm{deg}_{\rm out}(w)\geq d\cdot\log n,\;\text{ for at least one }w\in\mathrm{In}(P)\ \mid\ |\mathrm{In}(P)|<2\sqrt{d}\right]\leq n^{-d^{{9}/{10}}}\kern 5.0pt.

Indeed, the Chernoff bound gives

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\ \mathrm{deg}_{\rm out}(w)\geq d\cdot\log n\ |\ w\in\mathrm{In}(P)\right]\leq\exp\left(-\frac{(\log n-2)^{2}}{\log n}\cdot d\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\log n}{2}\cdot d\right)\leq n^{-d^{95/100}}\kern 5.0pt,

where the last two inequalities hold for large enough
d
and
n
.
The above and a simple union-bound imply (
20.8
).

Let
B_{1}
be the event that
|\mathrm{In}(P)|<2\sqrt{d}
, while let

B_{2}
be the event that for all
w\in\mathrm{In}(P)
we have
\mathrm{deg}_{\rm out}(w)<d\log n
.

On the events
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
, we have that for all
w\in\mathrm{In}(P)

(1)

\mathrm{deg}(w)<d\log n+2+2\sqrt{d}
,

(2)

\UpM(w)\leq 2d^{2}\log n
.

The first item follows by noticing that
\mathrm{deg}(w)=\mathrm{deg}_{\rm in}(w)+\mathrm{deg}_{\rm out}(w)
,
and that
\mathrm{deg}_{\rm in}(w)\leq 2+|\mathrm{In}(P)|
, for all
w\in\mathrm{In}(P)
.

The second item follows from the first item, and by noticing that
for any vertex
v
in the graph we have
\UpM(v)\leq d\cdot\mathrm{deg}(v)
.
Hence, we have that

(20.9)

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\UpM(\mathrm{In}(P))<(2d^{2}\log n)^{2\sqrt{d}}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\Pr[B_{1}\cap B_{2}]\geq 1-\Pr[\overline{B}_{1}]-\Pr[\overline{B}_{2}]\geq 1-n^{-d^{1/3}}\kern 5.0pt,

for the last inequality we use

eq.

20.8
&
eq.

20.7
.

To bound
\UpM(\mathrm{Out}(P))
, we follow the proof strategy of Lemma
19.2
. In particular, we partition
\mathrm{Out}(P)
into two sets: the set of vertices with even index in
P
, and the set of vertices with odd index in
P
. Moreover, we let
\UpM_{\rm even}(\mathrm{Out}(P))
be the product of weights over the set of even vertices in
\mathrm{Out}(P)
, and
\UpM_{\rm odd}(\mathrm{Out}(P))
similarly. We claim that

(20.10)

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\UpM_{\rm even}(\mathrm{Out}(P))\geq(\log n)^{-d}\right]\leq n^{-d^{2/5}}\kern 5.0pt.

Indeed, Markov’s inequality yields that for every
t>0

(20.11)

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\UpM_{\rm even}(\mathrm{Out}(P))\geq(\log n)^{-d}\right]\leq{\mathbb{E}\left[\UpM^{t}_{\rm even}(\mathrm{Out}(P))\right]}\cdot(\log n)^{t\cdot d}\kern 5.0pt.

In the proof of Lemma
19.2
, we have that for
t=d^{95/100}
, and
s=d^{94/100}
and arbitrary vertex
v
, we have that
\mathbb{E}\left[{\UpM^{t}\left(v\right)}\right]\leq(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{4})^{s}
, (notice that although we prove this for the random tree construction, the arguments work precisely for
\mathbold{G}(n,d/n)
as well). Acknowledging the fact that
\UpM_{\rm even}(\mathrm{Out}(P))
is a product of i.i.d. random variables, we get that

(20.12)

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\UpM_{\rm even}(\mathrm{Out}(P))\geq(\log n)^{-d}\right]\leq\frac{\left(1-\varepsilon/4\right)^{ks/2}}{(\log n)^{-t\cdot d}}\leq{(\log n)^{d^{2}}}\cdot{n^{-d^{42/100}}}\leq n^{-d^{2/5}}\kern 5.0pt,

Notice that, by symmetry,
(
20.10
) holds for
\UpM_{\rm odd}(\mathrm{Out}(P))
as well. Moreover, since
\UpM(\mathrm{Out}(P))=\UpM_{\rm odd}(\mathrm{Out}(P))\cdot\UpM_{\rm even}(\mathrm{Out}(P))
, using the union bound further yields

(20.13)

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\UpM(\mathrm{Out}(P))<(\log n)^{-2d}\right]\geq 1-2\cdot n^{-d^{2/5}}\geq 1-n^{-d^{1/3}}\kern 5.0pt.

From the union bound, equations (
20.9
) and (
20.13
) , and the fact that

\UpM(P)=\UpM(\mathrm{In}(P))\cdot\UpM(\mathrm{Out}(P))
,
we have that

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\UpM(P)<1\right]\geq 1-2\cdot n^{-d^{1/3}}\geq 1-n^{-d^{1/4}}\kern 5.0pt,

concluding the proof of Lemma
17.3
.

Appendix A
Some Standard Proofs

A.1.
Proof of Lemma
7.6

Write
{\mathcal{E}}_{S}
for the event that every set of vertices
S\subseteq V(\mathbold{G})
, with cardinality at most
2\frac{\log n}{\log^{2}d}
, spans at most
|S|
edges in
\mathbold{G}
. We have

\displaystyle\Pr\Big[\;\overline{{\mathcal{E}}_{S}}\;\Big]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{2\frac{\log n}{(\log d)^{2}}}\binom{n}{k}\binom{\binom{k}{2}}{k+1}\left(\frac{d}{n}\right)^{k+1}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{2\frac{\log n}{(\log d)^{2}}}\left(\frac{ne}{k}\right)^{k}\left(\frac{k^{2}e}{2(k+1)}\right)^{k+1}\left(\frac{d}{n}\right)^{k+1}

\displaystyle\leq
